                                                                                 iii
                                                                  Abstract

Heterogeneous many-core platforms are becoming the de facto standard architectures
for embedded systems, fulfilling their high demand for computational power conse-
quent to the rapid increase in the number and the workload of the applications in such
systems. Contrarily to historically being statically one-time programmed and the mix
of running applications being fixed, modern embedded systems are often subject to a
highly dynamic environment in which the mix of concurrently executed applications,
the availability of resources, and the non-functional requirements of each application
may change at run time on-demand. This necessitates adaptive application mapping
and system management schemes to effectively cope with the system dynamism. In
recent years, applications with hard real-time requirements are being increasingly ob-
served in emerging embedded systems in various time-critical areas, e.g., automotive,
telecommunications, and avionics. In this context, novel design methodologies are
required to enable an adaptive mapping and management of hard real-time applications
on many-core platforms with verified worst-case timing guarantees.
   Recently, so-called Hybrid Application Mapping (HAM) methodologies have been
proposed that split the design process into an offline application mapping phase and
an online system management phase and, thereby, can enable a dynamic mapping
and management of hard real-time applications on many-core platforms. In HAM,
for each application, a set of high-quality mapping alternatives with diverse resource
requirements and verified timing guarantees are computed offline in a Design Space
Exploration (DSE). These mappings are then used online by a Run-time Platform
Manager (RPM) to launch the application on-demand by selecting a precomputed
mapping that adheres to the current timing constraints of the application and the
resource availability in the system. In line with this hybrid scheme, the work at
hand proposes a collection of methodologies, analyses, and techniques that enable
an automated mapping and adaptive management of hard real-time applications on
heterogeneous many-core platforms with verified timing guarantees.
   Foremost, a formal timing analysis technique is proposed which can derive worst-
case timing guarantees for hard real-time applications in (heterogeneous) many-core
systems. In light of this analysis, a novel mapping optimization approach is proposed
in which the amount of resources allocated per mapping is fine-tuned according



                                                                                    v
to the timing requirements of the application to obtain high-quality mappings with
satisfactory timing guarantees and enhanced resource efficiency.
   In many-core systems, hard real-time applications are particularly susceptible to
built-in autonomous Dynamic Thermal Management (DTM) mechanisms which are
responsible for preserving the thermal safety of the system. At the sight of thermal
violations (overheating), DTM mechanisms suspend or decelerate the execution of the
applications that are running in the thermally affected regions which may lead to the
violation of their real-time constraints. To eliminate such fatal interferences, this work
proposes a thermally composable HAM methodology that preserves the thermal safety
of the system proactively at all times and, thereby, eliminates the exposure of real-time
applications to DTM without jeopardizing the thermal integrity of the system.
   The offline DSE in HAM typically delivers a large set of Pareto-optimal mappings
which imposes a considerable management overhead on the RPM and may degrade
its responsiveness. This can be particularly fatal for hard real-time applications due
to their strict dependence on prompt reactions from the RPM, e.g., upon a change in
their timing constraints. As a remedy, this work proposes an automatic mapping-set
distillation methodology that distills a promising subset of the mappings for online
use to alleviate the RPM’s overhead while retaining a diverse blend of quality- and
resource-demand trade-offs to address various run-time scenarios.
   For a seamless satisfaction of the timing constraints of hard real-time applications
in many-core systems, on-the-fly adaptation of their mappings becomes inevitable
in view of drastic changes in their timing constraints or the failure of resources they
use, e.g., due to faults or overheating. To that end, this work proposes two mapping
adaptation methodologies for hard real-time applications in many-core systems. In
the first approach, adaptations are realized in the form of a reconfiguration between
precomputed mappings, while the second approach empowers fine-grained mapping
adaptations in the form of the migration of any subset of the application’s tasks to
any desired locations. Both methodologies are supplied with formal timing analysis
techniques and lightweight admission checks to verify the real-time conformity of each
adaptation option online according to the current timing constraints of the application.

                                                                                     xi
                                                            Introduction
                                                                              1
For decades, the microprocessor industry has been able to deliver an exponential
performance increase across semiconductor technology generations commensurate
with Moore’s Law [Moo65]. Since hitting the power wall and the end of Dennard
Scaling [DGR+ 74] in the year 2005, this trend has been maintained by a shift from
frequency scaling to multi-core integration. Over the past decade, advances in the semi-
conductor industry coupled with novel on-chip communication and storage schemes
have enabled a massive integration of processing cores on a single chip with a scalable
performance yield, marking the beginning of the many-core era.


1.1 Embedded Many-Core Systems
Embedded systems have today become an indispensable part of our daily lives and are
extensively used in various areas, e.g., automotive and land transportation, aerospace
and avionics, telecommunications, medical support and healthcare, industrial automa-
tion, and consumer electronics. Recent trends in the embedded industry indicate an
unprecedented increase in the processing-power requirements of emerging embedded
systems which partly roots in the rapid increase in the number of applications that are
hosted in a modern embedded system. The large set of processing cores integrated in
a many-core chip delivers an immense processing power to meet the ever-increasing
performance requirements of emerging and future embedded applications and systems.
This potential has given rise to a widespread shift from single/multi-core platforms
towards many-core platforms in the embedded domains of computing in recent years.
   The computational power of a many-core chip can, however, be exploited only
partially due to the increased on-chip density of power consumption and the limita-
tions of the current chip packaging and cooling technologies for dissipating the heat
generated thereby. In fact, to preserve the thermal integrity of the chip, a considerable
share of cores must remain idle or heavily underutilized, a phenomenon termed Dark
Silicon [EBA+ 11]. As a remedy for this utilization penalty, architectural heterogeneity
has proven particularly effective where various types of processing resources with
diverse performance and power characteristics are integrated on a single chip to cater
to the specific performance requirements of individual applications. Consequently,



                                                                                       1
1 Introduction


heterogeneous many-core architectures that encompass a variety of low-power, high-
performance, and specialized processing resources on a single chip are becoming the
de facto standard platforms for emerging embedded systems.
  Heterogeneous many-core platforms provide a promising solution to meet the perfor-
mance requirements of modern embedded systems. Putting this potential into practice,
however, entails addressing several design challenges. In the following, an overview
of the major design challenges of modern embedded systems on (heterogeneous)
many-core platforms is provided.

1.1.1 Application Mapping Challenges and Trends
Embedded applications typically come with a set of non-functional requirements in
terms of timing, safety, security, reliability, etc. A satisfactory execution of such
applications, thus, not only necessitates their functionally correct execution, but also
the satisfaction of their non-functional requirements. Therefore, the analysis and
verification of non-functional properties of execution is a crucial aspect of design
which can render the design process particularly challenging. Real-time requirements,
i.e., constraints on the worst-case timing properties of execution, are predominant
examples of non-functional requirements in embedded systems. The verification of
these requirements often relies on complex and compute-intensive timing analyses
which are also difficult to develop, especially in the case of heterogeneous many-core
system targets.
   The large number of applications hosted within one system is another factor which
contributes significantly to the design complexity of modern embedded systems. To
exploit the potential computational power of many-core platforms, parallel and multi-
threaded computing models are adopted in the development of embedded applications.
In such models, the functionality of each application is partitioned into a set of
(processing) tasks that can be executed in parallel on different resources [Mar06;
Moy13]. In this context, the design complexity of optimally mapping applications
onto the resources of a many-core platform grows commensurate with the space of
possible task-to-resource assignment options. This space scales exponentially with the
number of tasks in the system and can quickly become intractable even for automated
Design Space Exploration (DSE) schemes. The resulting immense design space
necessitates scalable design methodologies for emerging embedded systems. This
calls for reconsidering the methodologies in practice and introducing novel design
schemes suited for large-scale embedded systems.
   The workload dynamism in emerging embedded applications and systems is another
major factor which contributes significantly to their design complexity on many-
core platforms. In a dynamic system, individual applications may be launched and
terminated on-demand, oftentimes independently from each other and in reaction to
unforeseeable external events, e.g., user requests. In fact, in many embedded systems,
only a fraction of all supported applications are simultaneously active at each point


2
                                                   1.1 Embedded Many-Core Systems


in time. Moreover, both the workload and the non-functional requirements of an
application may also vary over time, resulting in a dynamic demand of that application
for resources throughout its lifespan. In this context, novel application mapping and
system design schemes are required which can handle the relentless fluctuations in the
mixes of concurrently executed applications and the dynamism in the workload and
non-functional requirements of each application. This renders an adaptive mapping
and management of applications in modern embedded systems inevitable. In fact,
only by using a proper adaptive system management scheme, it will be possible to
achieve a high utilization of the underlying resources while meeting the non-functional
requirements of individual applications in view of the system dynamics.
   In recent years, composability has been established as a system property crucial for
mitigating the design complexity of embedded systems [AMH+ 11; Kop11]. System
composability enables the complex process of mapping a set of applications onto
platform resources to be partitioned into a per-application mapping phase, followed
by a system integration phase based on the results of the former. This practice substi-
tutes the traditional integrated mapping scheme with a constructive (per-application)
alternative which enables a significant reduction in the complexity of the mapping
process. Moreover, in light of composability, the dynamism in the mixes of concur-
rently executed applications and the workload of each application can be addressed by
performing the system integration at run time. This empowers dynamic and adaptive
application mapping and system management schemes. In such schemes, applications
are launched on-demand according to the current system state and availability of
resources, and once finished, they are removed from the platform to release their
occupied resources to be used by other running or incoming applications.

1.1.2 Prospect of Real-Time Applications
Online mapping of applications in many-core systems is subject to stringent time
and processing-power limitations: Not only are the system management decisions
conducted on the resources of the embedded platform itself which have limited
computational power, but also, the overhead of the decision-making processes directly
affects the responsiveness of the system to incoming application-launch requests. Thus,
dynamic mapping schemes must inevitably resort to fast heuristics to find, in due time,
a mapping of an incoming application onto the resources of the platform such that it
(i) merely relies on the available (free) resources of the platform and (ii) satisfies the
non-functional requirements of the application. Verifying the satisfaction of real-time
requirements, however, involves computationally intensive and thus time-consuming
timing analyses that cannot be practically performed online. Therefore, an online
computation of mappings for applications with real-time requirements is generally
deemed impractical.
   In recent years, so-called Hybrid Application Mapping (HAM) methodologies have
emerged which offer a practical solution to this problem, see [SSK+ 13; WWG+ 18b;


                                                                                        3
1 Introduction


PGH+ 20] for an overview. HAM methodologies employ a compute-intensive offline
application characterization phase followed by a lightweight online management
phase. In the offline phase, a DSE is performed for each application to compute a
set of Pareto-optimal1 mapping alternatives with diverse resource requirements and
worst-case timing guarantees which address various online resource-availability and
timing-requirement scenarios. These mappings are then used online by a lightweight
Run-time Platform Manager (RPM) which launches each application on-demand using
one of its precomputed mappings according to the current availability of resources and
timing requirements of the application. By enforcing temporal and/or spatial isolation
between concurrently executed applications, timing composability is established,
meaning that concurrent applications are decoupled from each other w.r.t. their (worst-
case) timing behavior [AMH+ 11]. This ensures that the statically obtained timing
guarantees for each mapping also hold seamlessly at run time in the presence of other
applications in the system.

Major Open Challenges

Empowered by composability, HAM outlines a viable solution for mitigating the com-
plexity of mapping and management of real-time applications in emerging embedded
systems targeting heterogeneous many-core platforms. In the case of hard real-time
applications, however, several crucial design and verification hurdles must still be
addressed before this hybrid design scheme can be put into practice: For the timing
verification of hard real-time applications, novel worst-case timing analysis techniques
are required that comply with the tiled architecture of emerging many-core platforms,
their network-on-chip communication infrastructure, and their distributed memory
scheme. Moreover, achieving a high system utilization necessitates adaptive resource-
reservation policies to enable fine-tuning the reserved resources of each application
according to its compute-power demand and timing constraints and, thereby, avoiding
an over-reservation or under-utilization of resources.
   Thermal aspects are particularly crucial for real-time applications. In emerging
many-core platforms, the increased on-chip temperature serves as an indirect source of
inter-application interference since the unsafe thermal behavior of one application can
trigger autonomous thermal-control mechanisms in the system which may interfere
with the execution of several running applications. This annihilates system compos-
ability and may lead to the violation of real-time constraints, rendering thermal safety
and thermal composability crucial factors to be regarded and enforced alongside timing
requirements to ensure a deadline-adhering execution of hard real-time applications.
   In a many-core platform, running applications are occasionally also subject to unex-
pected critical situations, e.g., resource failures due to hardware faults or overheating.

 1 Given a set of mappings optimized w.r.t. a set of design objectives, a mapping is called Pareto optimal

    if it is not dominated by any other mappings w.r.t. the objectives [BTT98].



4
                                                       1.2 Dissertation Contributions


Such events may affect the execution behavior of applications if not counteracted
accordingly by adapting their mapping during their execution, e.g., to evacuate an
overheated region. In the context of hard real-time applications, novel mechanisms are,
thus, required to enable the on-the-fly adaptation of mappings with verified worst-case
timing guarantees to enforce a seamless satisfaction of hard timing constraints before,
during, and after the adaptation process. Real-time mapping adaptation mechanisms
can also be employed in a non-critical setting, e.g., to improve the system’s perfor-
mance through load balancing or to release resources that are required for an incoming
application without an adverse impact on currently running applications.
   In response to the challenges outlined above, the dissertation at hand proposes
a collection of methodologies, analyses, and techniques in line with the principles
of HAM to enable an automated mapping and adaptive management of hard real-
time applications on heterogeneous many-core platforms. In the following, the key
contributions of this work are briefly introduced.


1.2 Dissertation Contributions
Figure 1.1 provides an overview of the major contributions of this dissertation, inte-
grated into the flow of Hybrid Application Mapping (HAM) as the promising scheme
for an adaptive mapping and management of hard real-time applications in many-core
systems. The proposed methodologies and techniques are highlighted in color and span
across the areas of (i) mapping optimization and worst-case timing analysis (green),
(ii) thermal safety and thermal composability (red), (iii) automatic mapping-set distil-
lation (orange), (iv) real-time mapping reconfiguration (blue), and (v) real-time task
migration (cyan). When integrated into the offline application characterization and
online management phases of HAM as depicted in Figure 1.1, these contributions
enable a thermally safe and composable, scalable, and adaptable mapping and man-
agement of hard real-time applications on heterogeneous many-core platforms with
guaranteed worst-case timing properties. These contributions are briefly introduced in
the following.

1.2.1 Mapping Optimization and Timing Analysis
The ability to reason about the timing properties of applications is a fundamental and
indispensable trait of any application mapping methodology used in the context of
real-time applications and systems. In the case of hard real-time applications, worst-
case timing analyses are required to provide timing guarantees for each mapping,
accounting for the worst-case timing behavior of the application’s tasks and the worst-
case timing interference imposed on them when accessing shared resources. In a
composable system, the maximum degree of such timing interferences is regulated
through temporal/spatial isolation among concurrently executed applications, enforced

Figure 1.1: An overview of the dissertation contributions (highlighted in color) inte-
             grated into the offline and online phases of Hybrid Application Mapping
             (HAM). Dashed blocks denote some of the existing components of HAM.


by an exclusive reservation of resources (or resource budgets) per application at its
launch time. In the current practice of application mapping in many-core systems, due
to the limitations of existing timing analyses, a fixed inter-application isolation scheme
is enforced throughout the system. This imposes a fixed resource-reservation policy



6
                                                       1.2 Dissertation Contributions


on every mapping of every application in the system, regardless of their actual timing
and compute-power requirements. Such a fixed isolation scheme often results in
sub-optimal mappings and leads to a significant under-utilization of system resources.
   As a remedy, this work proposes an isolation-aware mapping optimization and
analysis methodology consisting of an (i) isolation-aware mapping optimization
approach and a (ii) worst-case timing analysis which can be integrated into the offline
characterization phase of HAM (see the green blocks in Figure 1.1). These components
enable exploring different choices of isolation schemes (temporal or spatial) for each
resource and in each mapping of each application during the mapping optimization
process and provide worst-case timing guarantees for each mapping obtained thereby,
see [PSW+ 19]. As a result, the proposed methodology empowers the offline DSE
in HAM to find high-quality mappings in which the amount of reserved resources
is well-adjusted to the timing and compute-power requirements of the application.
The key enabler for this methodology is the proposed timing analysis. Contrarily to
existing techniques, the proposed analysis is applicable to mappings with arbitrary
combinations of isolation schemes on their allocated resources and delivers safe yet
tight worst-case timing guarantees per mapping according to the choices of isolation
schemes applied to the resources used by it. This methodology is presented in detail
in Chapter 3.

1.2.2 Thermal Safety and Thermal Composability
The massive integration of resources in emerging many-core platforms entails a
significant increase in the density of power consumption on the chip, leading to
an increased on-chip temperature which can engender overheated regions and even
jeopardize the thermal integrity of the chip. To ensure thermal safety, many-core
platforms employ autonomous thermal-control mechanisms that continuously monitor
the thermal state of the chip and take countermeasures such as power gating at the sight
of thermal violations. These countermeasures, however, interfere with the execution
of the applications that are running in the thermally affected regions and may lead to
the violation of their real-time constraints. In the current practice of HAM, thermal
safety is disregarded altogether. Hence, existing HAM methodologies not only fail
in ensuring a seamless satisfaction of real-time constraints but also cannot preserve
the system composability, since the unsafe thermal behavior of one application may
indeed trigger thermal-control countermeasures that affect also the other (possibly
real-time) applications in their close vicinity.
   As a remedy, this work proposes a thermally safe and thermally composable method-
ology which can be incorporated into the flow of HAM to proactively enforce thermal
safety throughout the system at all times, see [PSK+ 19]. As a result, thermal-control
countermeasures of the chip are never triggered and, thereby, composability is pre-
served. The proposed methodology is composed of two components: a (i) thermal-
safety analysis and a lightweight (ii) thermal-safety admission check (see the red


                                                                                      7
1 Introduction


blocks in Figure 1.1). The thermal-safety analysis is integrated into the offline applica-
tion characterization phase of HAM and computes an abstract thermal-safety index
for each generated mapping. The thermal-safety admission check is used online at
the launch time of each application and examines the thermal safety of each mapping
candidate for the current system utilization based on its precomputed thermal-safety
index. Using this approach, mapping candidates which may induce thermal violations
for the current system load are proactively identified and dismissed. Thereby, the
exposure of real-time applications to thermal-control countermeasures is eliminated
without compromising the thermal integrity of the chip. The proposed methodology is
presented in detail in Chapter 4.

1.2.3 Automatic Mapping-Set Distillation
During the offline DSE in HAM, mappings are often optimized w.r.t. multiple quality
objectives, e.g., latency, throughput, energy consumption, and reliability. Moreover, in
the case of heterogeneous many-core platforms, the number of allocated cores from
each core type is also optimized which introduces one design objective per core type2 .
As a result, the offline DSE in HAM often embodies a many-objective optimization
setting and typically delivers a large set of Pareto-optimal mappings. Such a high
volume of mappings imposes an unacceptable management and storage overhead for
online use. Selecting a promising subset of Pareto-optimal mappings for online use
is a crucial but challenging task due to the incomparability of the mappings w.r.t. the
design objectives.
   As a remedy, this work proposes a mapping-set distillation methodology (see the
orange block in Figure 1.1). The proposed methodology selects a subset of Pareto-
optimal mappings for online use such that the retained set of mappings features two
properties: (i) diverse quality trade-offs to meet various online quality requirements
and (ii) diverse yet efficient resource combinations to enhance the embeddability of
the application in view of a dynamic availability of resources at run time, see [PGT17].
To identify the promising mappings, the proposed methodology distinguishes quality
objectives from resource objectives and treats each set accordingly: First, the space
of quality objectives is partitioned into regions of comparable quality trade-offs.
Subsequently, resource-efficient mappings are distilled from different quality regions
using Pareto ranking of mappings w.r.t. resource objectives. Mappings distilled using
this approach are demonstrated to outperform those retained using multi-objective
truncation techniques w.r.t. both embeddability and diversity of quality trade-offs.
This methodology is presented in detail in Chapter 5.



 2 Optimizing the number of allocated cores per core type is a common practice in HAM to enable

    addressing various resource availability scenarios by diversifying the resource demand of mappings.



8
                                                         1.2 Dissertation Contributions


1.2.4 Hard Real-Time Online Mapping Adaptation
In many-core systems, running applications are occasionally subject to unexpected
critical situations, e.g., resource failures due to hardware faults or the emergence of hot
spots. Such events might impair the expected behavior of applications and, therefore,
must be counteracted accordingly to preserve the expected execution behavior. In this
context, adapting the mapping of running applications during their execution becomes
inevitable. Mapping adaptation is predominantly realized by means of task migration
which may cause changes in the timing behavior of applications during and after their
adaptation process. For hard real-time applications, the worst-case temporal overhead
of each adaptation process must be analyzed to guarantee a seamless satisfaction of the
timing constraints during and after the adaptation process. Such a timing verification,
however, necessitates compute-intensive worst-case timing analyses that are not suited
for online use. In this context, to enable on-the-fly mapping adaptations with hard real-
time guarantees and affordable run-time compute overhead, this work proposes two
contributions which are outlined in the following and presented in detail in Chapter 6.

Hard Real-Time Mapping Reconfiguration

The work at hand proposes a hard real-time mapping reconfiguration methodology
that enables on-demand mapping adaptations in the form of a transition between
the statically computed mappings of an application with hard timing guarantees,
see [PWG+ 17; PWG+ 19]. A reconfiguration between precomputed mappings, on the
one hand, eliminates the need for an online timing verification of the post-adaptation
mapping since worst-case timing guarantees are already provided for each mapping
during the offline DSE. On the other hand, as the mappings are known offline, the
worst-case latency of the reconfiguration process for each pair of source and destination
mappings can also be analyzed offline and, then, provided to the RPM for online use.
   The proposed mapping reconfiguration methodology is composed of three compo-
nents (see the blue blocks in Figure 1.1): a (i) timing-predictable reconfiguration mech-
anism which is used online by the RPM to perform mapping reconfigurations timing-
predictably, a (ii) reconfiguration analysis which is performed offline [PWG+ 17] or
partially offline and partially online [PWG+ 19] to derive the necessary information
(including worst-case timing guarantees) for each reconfiguration to be used by the
RPM, and a lightweight (iii) reconfiguration admission check which is used online
by the RPM to examine the admissibility of a reconfiguration w.r.t. the given timing
constraints of the application. To the best of our knowledge, this methodology is
the first solution to enable on-the-fly adaptation of individual applications with hard
real-time guarantees in the context of (heterogeneous) many-core systems.




                                                                                         9
1 Introduction


Hard Real-Time Task Migration

Mapping reconfiguration empowers the dynamic adaptation of real-time applications
only to a limited extent since (i) it is restricted to a set of statically computed options
which may not be able to address all possible online situations. Moreover, (ii) it often
involves rearranging the deployment of most or even all tasks of the application which
typically entails a significant temporal overhead that might not be affordable subject
to the current timing constraints of the application.
   To lift these restrictions, this work proposes a hard real-time task migration method-
ology which enables an on-demand migration of any subset of an application’s tasks
to any destination cores with worst-case timing guarantees. Hence, it empowers fine-
grained mapping adaptations without relying on a static timing analysis or a restricted
set of adaptation options, see [PSW+ 20]. The proposed migration methodology is
composed of three components which are used online by the RPM (see the cyan blocks
in Figure 1.1): a (i) timing-predictable migration mechanism which enables the RPM
to perform task migrations timing-predictably, a lightweight (ii) migration analysis to
formally bound the worst-case latency of the migration processes, and a lightweight
(iii) migration admission check which examines the admissibility of the migration
latency w.r.t. the given timing constraints of the application and the fluctuations in its
timing behavior during and after the migration processes.


1.3 Dissertation Organization
The remainder of this work is organized as follows: In Chapter 2, the fundamental
concepts required for this work are presented, including an overview of many-core
architectures, the fundamentals of real-time execution in many-core systems, the
system model used in this work, the procedure of application mapping in many-core
systems, and an overview of different classes of many-core application mapping
methodologies in practice. Chapter 3 focuses on resource reservation and timing
analysis in the scope of many-core systems and presents the proposed isolation-
aware mapping optimization and worst-case timing analysis methodology in detail.
In Chapter 4, the thermal aspects associated with the mapping and management
of applications in many-core systems are discussed, and the proposed thermally
composable HAM methodology is presented. Chapter 5 delineates the impact of
mapping-set distillation on the viability of HAM and presents the proposed automatic
mapping-set distillation methodology. Chapter 6 focuses on the topic of online
mapping adaptation for hard real-time applications in many-core systems and presents
the proposed real-time mapping reconfiguration and task migration methodologies.
Finally, Chapter 7 concludes this dissertation by providing a summary of this work
and outlining possible future directions.

                                                                              2
This chapter introduces the fundamental concepts which form the basis of this work.
Given that the work at hand aims at empowering a satisfactory execution of hard real-
time applications in many-core systems, first, an overview of heterogeneous many-core
platforms is provided in Section 2.1. Then, the fundamentals of real-time execution on
many-core platforms are presented in Section 2.2. Following that, the system model
considered in this work is detailed in Section 2.3. Finally, the principles of application
mapping in many-core systems are discussed in Section 2.4 which elaborates on
the procedure of system-level application mapping, provides an overview of design
methodologies for mapping and management of applications in many-core systems,
and presents the design methodology considered in this work.


2.1 Heterogeneous Many-Core Platforms
This section provides an overview of heterogeneous many-core platforms. First,
the many-core evolution path and key characteristics are presented in Section 2.1.1.
Thereafter, the architectural properties, memory scheme, and communication scheme
of many-core platforms are introduced in Sections 2.1.2 to 2.1.4, respectively.

2.1.1 Many-Core Evolution
The introduction of Multi-Processor System-on-Chip (MPSoC) platforms in the em-
bedded areas dates back to the early 2000s. The first MPSoCs designed for embedded
applications include Lucent Daytona [AAB+ 00] for wireless base stations (2000),
C-5 Network Processor [Cor01] for network packet processing (2001), Philips Viper
Nexperia [DJR01] for multimedia processing (2001), and STMicroelectronics No-
madik [AAC+ 03] for mobile computing (2003) [WJM08]. In each of these so-called
multi-core platforms, a set of cores, memories, caches (and, optionally, other peripher-
als, e.g., application-specific hardware accelerators) are integrated on a chip with a
bus-based interconnection scheme and a shared-memory communication scheme.
   For years, the multi-core integration technology invested in MPSoC platforms
has been able to address the ever-growing performance requirements of embedded



                                                                                      11
2 Fundamentals


applications and systems. The computational capacity of a multi-core platform scales
proportionally to its number of cores and can be exploited by multi-threaded and par-
allel applications where the functionality of each application is partitioned into a set
of (processing) tasks that can be executed concurrently on different resources [Moy13;
Mar06]. In this context, a higher number of cores on a chip leads to a higher number
of concurrent processes which brings about a higher volume of communication traffic
between cores and memories. This results in a higher demand for communication
bandwidth and memory throughput to meet the performance requirements of the
applications. Nonetheless, on the one hand, the service latency of a shared memory
grows with the number of its concurrent access requests. On the other hand, due to its
poor bandwidth scalability, a bus-based interconnection infrastructure can fulfill the
bandwidth requirements of a system only for a limited volume of communication traf-
fic. Consequently, the bus-based interconnection and shared-memory communication
scheme of multi-core platforms eventually became the bottleneck for the bandwidth
scalability of MPSoCs. This renders a multi-core integration beyond a handful of
cores effectively futile due to the stagnation of the deliverable system performance.
   To address this bandwidth scalability restriction, a series of fundamental modifi-
cations have been applied in the architecture, organization, and operation of MPSoC
platforms, leading to the introduction of the so-called many-core platforms. Fore-
most, the shared-memory communication scheme and the bus-based interconnection
infrastructure have been substituted with scalable alternatives: The globally shared
memory scheme has been replaced by a scalable distributed memory scheme in which
each memory is shared among only a small collection of its neighboring resources,
eliminating remote memory-access privileges. Such a distributed memory scheme
mitigates the volume of requests per memory, resulting in a reduced service latency per
request. In the same line, the centralized bus-based interconnection infrastructure has
been substituted by Network-on-Chip (NoC) structures with distributed scheduling and
routing. This enables the concurrent operation of multiple communication channels
and, thereby, offers a scalable bandwidth capacity [NFM+ 12; BD02].
   The outlined combination of the distributed memory scheme and the NoC-based
interconnection infrastructure offers an unprecedented bandwidth scalability. It has
enabled the microprocessor industry to integrate exceedingly large numbers of cores
on a single chip with a scalable performance capacity w.r.t. both computation and
communication. This has led to the introduction of many-core platforms into the
embedded market. Following this line of evolution, the main distinctions between
multi- and many-core platforms lie in their on-chip interconnection infrastructure and
memory scheme.

2.1.2 Platform Architecture
A many-core platform typically integrates a large set of processing, communication,
and memory resources on a single chip. Examples of many-core platforms include


12
                                             2.1 Heterogeneous Many-Core Platforms


the Tilera TILE-Gx many-core family [Til12], Kalray MPPA-256 (Massively Parallel
Processor Array) [dDAB+ 13], Intel SCC (Single-chip Cloud Computer) [HDH+ 10],
Intel Xeon Phi [Sod15], and the KiloCore [BSP+ 17]. A many-core platform typically
follows a modular and regular organization of resources where resources are clustered
into a set of so-called (compute) tiles that are interconnected via a NoC. Furthermore,
a distributed memory scheme is applied across the chip such that the memory space
on each tile is accessible only to the resources located on that tile.
   In a homogeneous many-core platform, all processing resources are instances of
the same type of core. A heterogeneous many-core platform, on the other hand,
encompasses various types of processing resources to tailor the computational power
provided to each application according to its computational requirements and, thereby,
enhance the energy-performance balance of the whole system. In a heterogeneous
many-core platform, the cores comprised within one tile are typically homogeneous,
while different tiles contain different types of cores. Figure 2.1 illustrates an exemplary
heterogeneous tiled many-core platform composed of 16 tiles with a 4 × 4 mesh NoC
interconnection where different core types are distinguished by color. Note that, in
addition to compute tiles, a many-core platform may also be provisioned with a set of
memory tiles that offer mass storage on the chip and a set of I/O tiles for connectivity
with off-chip peripherals, e.g., sensors and actuators.

Compute-Tile Organization

Figure 2.2 provides a detailed view of the internal organization of different tiles of
the exemplary many-core platform illustrated in Figure 2.1. In a many-core platform,
the internal architecture of each tile resembles the architecture of a multi-core plat-
form: Each tile consists of (i) a set of cores, (ii) a set of memories, and (iii) a Network
Adapter (NA). The NA is a part of the NoC, acting as an interface for the exchange of
data between tiles via the NoC. Additional peripherals, such as hardware accelerators,
may also be incorporated on a tile. Within each tile, a bus-based interconnection in-
frastructure is employed which enables the local cores and the NA to access the on-tile
memories via a set of (memory) buses. In addition to the memories, each tile can be
equipped with shared L2 caches and each core can have private data/instruction L1
caches. Different tiles on the platform may have different numbers of cores, memories,
and buses, as exemplified in Figure 2.2.

Network-on-Chip (NoC) Architecture

Tiles in a many-core platform are interconnected via a NoC which consists of a set of
Network Adapters (NAs) (one NA located on each tile), a set of routers (one router
per tile), and a set of links that connect routers to each other and their local NAs. The
arrangement of tiles in a many-core platform depends on its choice of NoC topology,
e.g., mesh, tree, ring, torus, and star [BDY06; DT04]. A regular two-dimensional



                                                                                       13
2 Fundamentals

               NA       memories                NA       memories                NA     memories                NA       memories

               NA       memories                NA       memories                NA     memories                NA       memories

               NA       memories                NA       memories                NA     memories                NA       memories

        0                                1                                2                              3                          X

Figure 2.1: Example of a heterogeneous tiled many-core architecture consisting of
                16 tiles with a 4 × 4 mesh Network-on-Chip (NoC) interconnection. The
                XY-plane provides the NoC location of each router and its local tile. Each
                tile comprises a set of cores, a set of memories, and a Network Adapter
                (NA). Different types of cores are distinguished by color.


placement of tiles with a mesh NoC topology is the predominant architectural structure
employed in many-core platforms, see Figure 2.1.
   In a two-dimensional mesh NoC, each router is connected to the NA on its local
tile and each of its adjacent routers in the cardinal directions so that it is connected
to 2–4 other routers subject to its location on the chip, see Figure 2.1. Each NoC
connection (connecting two adjacent routers or a router to its local NA) is realized via
two uni-directional links, one per direction of data transfer. Each NA has one input
and one output port. It comprises a Transmitter (TX) unit and a Receiver (RX) unit
which are responsible for the coordination of the outbound and incoming traffic of
the tile, respectively. Each TX unit (RX unit) is equipped with (i) a buffer for the
intermediate storage of the outgoing (incoming) traffic of the tile and (ii) the control
logic necessary for the coordination and transfer of the outgoing (incoming) traffic
between the local router and the on-tile memories. Accordingly, each router can have
up to five input and up to five output ports and is equipped with (i) an input buffer per
input port for intermediate storage of arriving data, (ii) a 5 × 5 crossbar connecting
each input buffer to each output port, and (iii) the control logic necessary for the
coordination and routing of traffic to adjacent routers or the local NA.

                                             2.1 Heterogeneous Many-Core Platforms



     core         core          core core core core             core     core    core


  NA         mem.               NA     mem.      mem.          NA      mem.     mem.

Figure 2.2: A detailed view of the internal architecture of the three tile types from Fig-
            ure 2.1. In each tile, the cores and the NA access the local memories via a
            set of (memory) buses. Core types are distinguished by color.


2.1.3 Memory Scheme
Scalability is a central goal in the design of many-core systems, and the memory
scheme in a many-core platform is developed such that system scalability is preserved.
To that end, many-core systems employ a distributed memory scheme without remote-
access privileges between tiles, while a shared memory scheme is used within each
tile. The distributed memory scheme between tiles, also referred to as a No Remote
Memory Access (NORMA) architecture [MDA+ 16], restricts the accessibility of the
memories on each tile to the resources located on that tile, namely, the local cores and
the NA. The memory space on each tile is generally shared among the local cores and
NA, while it can also be partitioned into regions with additional access restrictions.
For instance, a private memory space can be dedicated to each core. Likewise, the
dedicated memory space of each core can be further partitioned among the processes
executed on that core. Similarly, a dedicated memory space can be provisioned for
the storage of each outgoing (incoming) communication traffic of the tile, accessible
to the TX unit (RX unit) of the NA and the respective producing (consuming) core
or process. In this context, the operating system typically offers dynamic memory
allocation services for a dynamic partitioning of the memory space among the on-tile
resources and/or the processes running on the local cores.

2.1.4 Communication Scheme
Many-core systems employ two communication schemes for data transfers within
one tile (intra-tile communication) and between tiles (inter-tile communication) in
accordance with their shared intra-tile and distributed inter-tile memory schemes.

Intra-Tile Communications

The exchange of data within one tile, namely, an intra-tile communication, is typically
realized through the shared memories of that tile. To that end, the communication
source (also known as the producer) writes the data into an assigned memory space
within one of the on-tile memories, and the communication destination (also known as



                                                                                        15
2 Fundamentals


the consumer) reads the data from that memory space. This communication scheme
enables data transfers between two cores located on the same tile and can also be
employed for the exchange of data between two processes executed on the same core.

Inter-Tile Communications

The transfer of data between cores located on two different tiles, namely, an inter-tile
communication, is realized over the NoC. Due to the distributed memory scheme
between tiles, inter-tile communications are realized by means of explicit message
passing between the source and destination tiles. To that end, first (i) the communica-
tion source (producer) writes the data into an assigned space within one of the on-tile
memories. Then, (ii) the local NA reads the data from the memory, prepares it for
the transfer via the NoC, and dispatches the data to its local router. Once available
at the router, (iii) the data is forwarded through a chain of routers over the NoC in
a hop-by-hop manner towards the destination tile. At the destination tile, (iv) the
local router forwards the data to the RX unit of the NA which prepares the data and
writes it into an assigned space within one of the on-tile memories from where (v) the
communication destination (consumer) reads the data afterwards.

Network-on-Chip Operation

The realization of an inter-tile communication (steps (ii)–(iv) detailed above) strongly
depends on the switching strategy, routing algorithm, and the flow-control mechanism
of the NoC [BM06; DT04]. In the following, a summary of the current practices in
many-core NoC design w.r.t. these aspects is provided.

Switching Strategy       The switching strategy of a NoC specifies how data is trans-
ferred through the NoC. Most many-core NoCs employ a packet-switching strategy
in which data is partitioned into a set of packets that are transferred—in-order, in a
pipelined manner, and independently from each other—towards the destination tile.
In this line, wormhole switching [NM93] is a widely used packet-switching strategy
in which data packets are subdivided into fixed-length flow-control digits (flits) which
are transferred over the NoC. The flits associated with a data packet consist of (i) a
head flit carrying routing and sequencing information, (ii) a series of payload flits (also
called body flits) containing the actual data, and (iii) a tail flit signaling the end of the
packet and carrying, e.g., an error-checking code. The subdivision of packets into flits
(called packet flitization) before the transfer and the reconstruction of the packet from
flits (called packet assembly) after the transfer are performed by the TX unit of the
NA on the source tile and the RX unit of the NA on the receiver tile, respectively.

Routing Algorithm    The routing algorithm of a NoC determines the path (sequence
of routers) through which data is transferred between two tiles. NoC routing al-


16
                                   2.2 Real-Time Execution on Many-Core Platforms


gorithms are classified w.r.t. their routing decision procedure as either adaptive or
oblivious [WLC16]. An adaptive routing algorithm considers the network traffic in its
routing decisions to balance the NoC load at the cost of an increased router complex-
ity. Alternatively, oblivious routing algorithms disregard the network traffic in their
routing decisions to simplify the router design. In the scope of oblivious algorithms,
deterministic routing algorithms simplify the network design considerably by using a
fixed route for each pair of source and destination tiles and, thereby, eliminating the
routing decision process altogether. XY-routing is a widely-used deterministic routing
algorithm in which data is first routed in the X-direction and then in the Y-direction to-
wards the destination router [NM93]. XY-routing is also a minimal routing algorithm,
as it realizes the transfer of data with the shortest possible route length quantified by
the number of hops in the route.

Flow-Control Mechanism          The flow-control mechanism of a NoC coordinates the
forwarding of traffic flows in the network by steering the allocation of router buffers
and links to individual traffic participants. Credit-based flow control is predominantly
used in NoCs to steer the flow of traffic by means of so-called credits which sig-
nal the availability of input buffer space in each router to its respective predecessor
router [DT04]. In this scheme, data forwarding is orchestrated by the credits com-
municated between adjacent routers which also inherently realizes a back-pressure
mechanism. Virtual-channel flow control [Dal92] is another widely-used mechanism
in which the physical space of each buffer is logically partitioned into several so-called
virtual channels which can be exclusively assigned to different traffic participants. Us-
ing virtual channels, quality of service guarantees can be provided per communication
flow since they prevent concurrent flows which pass through the same router from
blocking each other, see, e.g., [HKK+ 13].


2.2 Real-Time Execution on Many-Core Platforms
Embedded applications often have certain requirements w.r.t. their non-functional prop-
erties of execution that must be satisfied throughout their lifespan. Examples of these
non-functional properties include timing properties (e.g., latency and throughput),
power and energy consumption, and dependability properties (e.g., safety, security,
and reliability). The choice and the number of non-functional requirements of an appli-
cation typically depend on the application and system domain. Timing requirements
are among the most common non-functional requirements in embedded systems.
   In this section, first, real-time applications and systems are introduced in Sec-
tion 2.2.1. Afterwards, an overview of timing verification procedure of many-core
systems is provided, and the timing analyses involved in timing verification processes
are described in Section 2.2.2. Thereafter, two key system properties crucial for the de-
sign of real-time systems, namely, timing predictability and timing composability, are



                                                                                      17
2 Fundamentals


introduced in Sections 2.2.3 and 2.2.4, respectively, and it is shown how they can be
established in a many-core system. Finally, Section 2.2.5 introduces the paradigm of
invasive computing as a promising solution for the design of hard real-time embedded
systems based on heterogeneous many-core platforms.

2.2.1 Real-Time Applications and Systems
A timing requirement generally specifies an upper and/or lower bound on the timing
properties of a process or an application, e.g., an upper bound on its execution latency
or a lower bound on its throughput. Timing requirements are relevant for systems in
which the correctness of their behavior depends not only on the logical results of their
computation but also on the physical instant (time) at which these results are ready,
e.g., the airbag control system in a car. By definition, an application with at least one
timing requirement is called a real-time application, and a system that contains at
least one real-time application is referred to as a real-time system [Kop11].
   Timing requirements of real-time applications can be classified as either soft or
hard subject to their strictness: In the case of a soft real-time requirement, occasional
timing violations are considered tolerable. Such violations, however, degrade the
system’s performance or the quality/usefulness of the obtained results. For instance,
real-time applications from the areas of multimedia, e.g., audio/video streaming and
image processing, typically have soft real-time requirements [BLA+ 05]. In the case of
a hard real-time requirement, on the other hand, any timing violation may lead to a
system failure, and therefore, timing violations are not tolerable at all.
   Recent years give evidence of a rapid increase in the number and diversity of
hard real-time applications in embedded systems. For instance, the introduction of
embedded systems in time-critical areas, e.g., automotive electronics, medical imaging,
avionics, telecommunications, industrial plant automation, and consumer electronics,
has given rise to a high volume of applications with hard timing constraints [DB11;
SEU+ 15; SDM+ 17]. In this line, a hard real-time system is defined as:

  Hard Real-Time System [Kop11]: A real-time computer system that must meet
  at least one hard deadline is called a hard real-time computer system or a safety-
  critical computer system.

   Here, the term safety-critical is often associated with systems in which a system
failure, e.g., consequent to the violation of a hard timing requirement, can put human
lives at risk [Kni02]. A real-time application may also have non-timing requirements,
e.g., in terms of safety or security. The methodologies presented in this work focus on
the timing aspects of execution for hard real-time applications in many-core systems.
These methodologies can be used orthogonally to or in combination with techniques
that address other non-functional requirements of applications.



18
                                   2.2 Real-Time Execution on Many-Core Platforms


2.2.2 Worst-Case Timing Verification
Hard real-time applications rely on timing verification techniques that examine the
satisfaction of their timing requirements. Verifying a seamless satisfaction of hard real-
time requirements is a challenging task since the timing behavior of an application
often strongly depends on (i) the input of the application and (ii) the state of its
execution environment (also called execution context). The latter denotes the state of
the system resources used by the application and the presence and behavior of other
applications in the system. The uncertainty in the input or execution environment of
an application typically results in a huge variability in the non-functional properties
of its execution, e.g., its timing behavior. In this context, Teich et al. introduce the
concept of *-predictability defined as follows:

  *-predictability [TGR+ 16]: Let o denote a non-functional property of a program
  (implementation) p and the uncertainty of its input (space) given by I and environ-
  ment by Q. The predictability (marker) of objective o for program p is described
  by the interval

                      o(p, Q, I) = [INF o (p, Q, I), SUPo (p, Q, I)]               (2.1)

  where INF o and SUPo denote the infimum and supremum [of property o for
  program p] under variation of state q ∈ Q and input i ∈ I, respectively.

   In line with this definition, techniques aiming at the verification of timing properties
of hard real-time applications mainly focus on deriving worst-case timing guarantees
for each application that hold under any variation of its input and execution environ-
ment. The main goal of such a technique is to derive safe and tight timing guarantees.
Here, safeness denotes that the timing property of interest does not violate the derived
timing guarantee under any variation of the input or environment state. Thus, a safe
timing guarantee specifies an upper/lower bound on the actual worst-case timing
behavior of the application. Tightness, on the other hand, denotes the closeness of
the derived guarantee to the actual worst-case timing behavior without jeopardizing
safeness. Timing verification of hard real-time applications is typically performed
in two steps, namely, (i) a context-independent timing analysis followed by (ii) a
context-aware timing analysis, which are detailed in the following.

Context-Independent Timing Analysis

The timing verification of a hard real-time application begins with a context-independent
timing analysis. In such an analysis, each task of the application is considered in-
dividually, and its Worst-Case Execution Time (WCET) is derived in full isolation,
i.e., without interruption and in the absence of any other concurrent processes in the



                                                                                       19
2 Fundamentals


                                 WCET guarantee

                   possible
                execution times                     maximum            execution
     density



                                                    observed              time
                                                                  WCET
                                                    execution            upper
                  observed                            time               bound
               execution times


                                          execution time

Figure 2.3: Worst-Case Execution Time (WCET) and the distribution of possible and
                 observable execution times of an illustrative task according to [WEE+ 08].


system [MRR+ 19]. Since the execution time of a task often varies with changes in
(i) its input(s), (ii) its internal state, and (iii) the timing behavior of the underlying
hardware resources, the goal of WCET analysis is to identify a combination of these
factors that results in the highest possible execution time for that task.
   For the vast majority of applications, an enumeration of all execution scenarios
of a task is computationally impractical due to the intractable number of possible
scenarios [KP08]. Therefore, WCET analyses aim at finding a so-called WCET
guarantee which specifies a safe and tight upper bound on the actual WCET of a task,
see Figure 2.3. To that end, compute-heavy analyses and processes are employed
including (i) control flow analysis to characterize the internal execution paths of the
task, (ii) hardware subsystem analysis to provide an abstract model of the timing
behavior of the underlying hardware resources, and (iii) estimate calculation which
uses the results of (i) and (ii) to bound the WCET of the task [WEE+ 08; CRR+ 14].
To tighten the obtained WCET guarantee, these analyses often employ and combine
measurement-based, simulation-based, statistical, and/or formal techniques.

Context-Aware Timing Analysis

The timing behavior of a task strongly depends also on its execution context, i.e., the
presence and the behavior of other processes in the system. Sharing resources among
concurrently executed tasks can lead to considerable variations in their timing behavior
since the accesses of one task to a shared resource may be blocked or delayed by
concurrent accesses from other tasks. Due to the imposition of such timing interference
scenarios, e.g., preemption delay on a shared core or delays imposed when accessing
memory via a shared bus, the maximum interval between the arrival time and the
completion time of a task, referred to as its Worst-Case Response Time (WCRT), often
exceeds the WCET guarantee derived for it in isolation [DB11].
   The second phase of the timing verification of a hard real-time application aims at



20
                                  2.2 Real-Time Execution on Many-Core Platforms


deriving an upper bound on the WCRT of each task in its execution context. Such
an upper bound accounts for both the execution time of the task and the maximum
possible amount of timing interference that may be imposed on its execution due to
the presence of concurrent processes in the system. To that end, WCRT analyses (also
called schedulability analyses) are used to derive a safe and tight WCRT guarantee for
each task based on (i) its WCET guarantee, (ii) the arbitration/scheduling policy of
the shared resources it uses, and (iii) the timing behavior of other users of the shared
resources [MRR+ 19]. In the scope of a many-core system, WCRT analysis mainly
involves bounding the preemption delays in the case of shared cores and the delays
imposed on the transfer of data to/from tile memories via shared buses.

Communication Timing Analysis

Different tasks of an application are often subject to data dependencies such that the
results produced by one task serve as input for another task. The exchange of data
between data-dependent tasks imposes a temporal overhead that must be taken into
account when deriving worst-case timing guarantees for the whole application. Note
that the WCRT of each task also accounts for reading/writing its input/output data
from/to the respective memories on the tile where the task is executed.
   As detailed in Section 2.1.4, the shared-memory scheme within each tile of a many-
core platform enables the intra-tile data exchanges between data-dependent tasks
executed on the same tile to happen implicitly through the shared memories of that
tile. Thus, the temporal overhead of intra-tile transfers is restricted to the time taken
for writing and reading the data to/from memory which is already accounted for in the
WCRT guarantee of the producer and consumer tasks.
   Due to the distributed (NORMA) memory scheme between different tiles, however,
inter-tile data exchanges necessarily depend on an explicit transfer of data via the
NoC between the source and destination tiles. The worst-case temporal overhead of
such transfer processes, namely, the Worst-Case Traversal Time (WCTT) of inter-tile
communications, is not incorporated in the WCRT of the producer and consumer
tasks and, therefore, must be bounded separately. Similarly to the worst-case timing
behavior of tasks, the WCTT of a data transfer consists of (i) context-independent
and (ii) context-dependent latency components that can be analyzed separately or in a
single step, introduced in the following.

Basic Transfer Time      The context-independent latency component of an inter-tile
data transfer is referred to as the basic transfer time which denotes the worst-case
transfer time of the data in full isolation, i.e., in the absence of any other concurrent
communication flows in the network. To bound the basic transfer time, context-
independent timing analysis is performed, taking into account (i) the maximum size
of the data to be transferred and (ii) the timing behavior of the resources involved in
the transfer process, namely, memories, buses, NAs, and routers [DT04].


                                                                                     21
2 Fundamentals


Worst-Case Network Jitter       Concurrent communication flows in a network can
interfere with the timing behavior of one another directly or indirectly if resources
are shared among multiple communication flows. The maximum amount of timing
interference imposed on the transfer of a data set is referred to as the network jitter.
The network jitter is analyzed using a context-aware timing analysis based on (i) the
previously derived basic transfer time of the data, (ii) the arbitration/scheduling
policy of the resources involved in its transfer, (iii) the timing behavior of concurrent
communication flows which use these resources, and (iv) the operational characteristics
of the NoC, e.g., switching strategy and flow-control mechanism [HRG+ 16].

Compositional Timing Analysis

In a many-core system, several resources are typically involved in the execution of
a task or the inter-tile transfer of data, each contributing a latency component to
the overall latency of the respective computation or communication process. For
instance, the execution time of a task consists of the time taken to read/write its data
from/to the memory over a bus and the time taken to perform the computation on the
core. Similarly, the inter-tile transfer latency of a communication flow consists of the
time taken to read the data from the memory over a bus, the time taken to transfer
the data over the network, and the time taken to write the data into the destination
memory. Any change in the context of each step, e.g., interfering processes, can lead
to a change in the latency of that step and, hence, the overall latency of the whole
process. To derive a safe worst-case timing guarantee during the WCRT analysis of a
task or the WCTT analysis of an inter-tile data transfer, all possible combinations of
timing interference scenarios imposed in the steps above must be taken into account to
identify the scenario that leads to the highest possible latency for the overall process.
   Due to the intractable number of possible combinations of interference scenarios in
different steps, an integrated consideration of them typically becomes computationally
infeasible [MTT18]. As a remedy, the complexity of the timing verification process
can be mitigated by means of compositional timing analyses. In such analyses, the
worst-case timing interference imposed on a process is decomposed into a set of timing
interference contributions, each incurred in one step of the process and/or on one or a
subset of involved resources [HRW15; HAE+ 17]. Timing decomposition is realized
in such a way that (i) each interference contribution can be analyzed and bounded
individually, and then, (ii) the derived bounds for different contributions can be
combined according to a so-called combination function of the timing decomposition
to obtain a globally safe timing guarantee for the whole process [HRW15]. Such
a timing decomposition is often applied during different steps of worst-case timing
analysis as a complexity-reducing mechanism. The timing analyses presented in this
work are developed in a compositional manner.




22
                                  2.2 Real-Time Execution on Many-Core Platforms


2.2.3 Timing Predictability
In many-core systems, performance-boosting features such as branch prediction, out-
of-order execution, and pipelining, have proven effective in enhancing the average-case
performance of applications. Such features, however, often manifest an unpredictable
timing behavior which can be fatal for hard real-time applications relying on worst-
case timing guarantees: The timing unpredictability of these features contributes
significantly to the uncertainty of the execution environment of applications, i.e., leads
to an expansion in the state space of the environment Q in Eq. (2.1). The inflated
uncertainty of the environment state often results in a significant increase in the worst-
case timing properties of execution and may lead to situations where derivable timing
guarantees are too large to be of any practical use [But11; TGR+ 16]. Some uncertain
resource behaviors, e.g., random resource arbitration or cache replacement policies,
even render an exact analysis of worst-case timing guarantees impossible. Moreover,
such features often give rise to timing anomalies, defined as follows:

  Timing Anomaly [LS99; RWT+ 06]: A timing anomaly refers to a situation in
  which the local worst-case timing scenario, e.g., a cache miss or a branch mis-
  prediction, does not entail the global worst-case timing scenario, e.g., maximum
  possible execution time.

   Timing anomalies render compositional timing analyses difficult (if not impossi-
ble) and complicate the formal analysis of worst-case timing guarantees [MTT18].
Therefore, timing predictability is an indispensable property of any platform used in
a hard real-time context so that practical bounds on the worst-case timing properties
of applications can be derived analytically: In a timing-predictable system (i) the
range of observable values for timing properties of applications is narrowed down
such that practical (useful) worst-case timing guarantees can be derived for them.
Moreover, the timing-predictable behavior of resources prevents timing anomalies and,
thereby, (ii) enables the use of formal and compositional timing analyses to derive
timing guarantees at an affordable computational overhead [AMH+ 11]. This work
considers timing predictable many-core systems in which each and every resource
has a predictable timing behavior devoid of randomness or timing anomalies. In the
rest of this work, the term predictability refers to timing predictability unless stated
otherwise.

2.2.4 Timing Composability
Composability is another key complexity-reducing system property which is of cru-
cial importance for the design of real-time embedded systems based on many-core
platforms. In general, composability outlines the ability to develop a system through
the integration of individually designed and verified components such that a property


                                                                                      23
2 Fundamentals


verified for a component individually remains unaffected in the presence of other
components after the system integration. Kopetz defines composability as follows:

  Composability [Kop11]: An architecture [system] is said to be composable w.r.t.
  a specified property if the system integration will not invalidate this property
  once the property has been established at the subsystem level. Examples of such
  properties are timeliness or testability. In a composable architecture [system], the
  system properties follow from the subsystem properties.

   Timing composability is a fundamental system property for the design of modern
real-time systems which host a large number of applications. In a timing-composable
system, concurrent applications are decoupled from each other w.r.t. their worst-case
timing behavior so that the timing verification of each application can be performed
individually, irrespective of the presence and/or behavior of the other applications in
the system [AMH+ 11]. By extension, timing composability also enables independent
timing analysis of individual tasks of an application where timing guarantees can be
derived for different tasks separately and then combined to bound the timing properties
of the whole application. In the rest of this work, the term composability refers to
timing composability unless stated otherwise.
   Composability may be established by means of spatial and/or temporal isolation
of applications from each other. While spatial isolation eliminates resource sharing
by assigning each resource exclusively to one application, temporal isolation allows
resources to be shared among applications in such a way that their worst-case tim-
ing behavior remains independent from each other. Establishing temporal isolation
between concurrent applications necessitates the practice of so-called composable
arbitration policies on shared resources which are discussed in the following.

Composable Resource Sharing

Temporal isolation between concurrent applications entails two essential corner-
stones: The practice of (i) composable arbitration policies on shared resources and
(ii) exclusive reservation of resource budgets per requestor of each shared resource. A
composable arbitration policy erects a temporal firewall between concurrent requestors
of a shared resource. This enables the worst-case timing behavior of each requestor
to be analyzed based on its reserved resource budget and the arbitration policy of the
shared resource regardless of the presence or the behavior of other requestors. In fact,
a composable arbitration policy ensures that temporal control resides within the shared
resource itself and not in its requestors [Kop11]. This renders event-driven and/or
priority-based arbitration policies, e.g., Round-Robin (RR) or First-Come-First-Serve
(FCFS), non-composable [AMH+ 11]. Contrarily, contention-less and time-triggered
arbitration policies are composable. In particular, Time-Division Multiple Access
