                                            2.2 Real-Time Execution on Many-Core Platforms


          Si            Di               Ki = 5                           Wiy = 3       Wix = 2

 ... x    x     y   y        y   x   x     y      y     y             y     y       y   x    x               ...

       Pi = Ki · (Si + Di )     arbitration period in                                 arbitration period in
                          the presence of x and y requests                      the presence of x requests only
                                                            arbitration period in
                                                      the presence of y requests only

Figure 2.4: Example of Time-Division Multiple Access (TDMA) arbitration policy of
               wheel size Ki = 5 for a resource i with two requestors, x and y, which have
               Wix = 2 and Wiy = 3 reserved time slots per arbitration period, respectively.


(TDMA) and Weighted Round-Robin (WRR) policies are well-established and widely
used composable arbitration policies [GAC+ 13; HGB+ 09; HKK+ 13].
   Note that composability and timing predictability are two orthogonal system proper-
ties where the presence or the lack of one does not necessarily confirm or invalidate the
other [AMH+ 11]. For instance, a shared resource with RR arbitration annihilates com-
posability, while leaving timing predictability intact. Likewise, caches with random
replacement policy annihilate timing predictability, while they leave system compos-
ability unharmed as long as they are not shared. In this work, each shared resource of
a given many-core architecture is assumed to have a composable arbitration policy,
e.g., TDMA or WRR, to establish timing composability. These policies are outlined
in the following.

Time-Division Multiple Access (TDMA) Arbitration

TDMA is a composable arbitration policy that is widely used in real-time systems.
In TDMA, physical time is divided into a series of time slots of equal length which
are periodically assigned to resource requestors. Each arbitration period, also called
TDMA round, consists of a fixed number of consecutive time slots referred to as wheel
size, see [JW04; HKK+ 13]. A requestor may reserve one or more periodic time slots.
Each pair of consecutive time slots may be separated by a fixed-length (relatively short)
control time slot used by the resource arbiter to switch between different requestors.
The length of each control slot is equal to the arbitration delay of the arbiter. For
instance, the arbitration delay of a shared core reflects the context-switch latency of
the operating system for switching between tasks.
   Figure 2.4 depicts an example of TDMA arbitration. The TDMA arbitration policy
of a resource i can be specified by three parameters: (i) the length Si of each arbitration
time slot, (ii) the length Di of each control slot, and (iii) the arbitration wheel size
Ki . Based on these parameters, the length of an arbitration period Pi of resource i is
calculated as Pi = Ki · (Si + Di ). Here, a requestor x which has reserved Wix ∈ N+     ≤Ki




                                                                                                             25
2 Fundamentals

          Si            Di               Ki = 5                   Wix = 2                        Wiy = 3

 ... x    x     y   y        y   x   x     y      y   y   x   x    x    x    y     y    y    y     y       y ...

       Pi = Ki · (Si + Di )     arbitration period in            arbitration period in
                          the presence of x and y requests the presence of x and y requests
                                                 arbitration period in                 arbitration period in
                                           the presence of x requests only       the presence of y requests only

Figure 2.5: Example of Weighted Round-Robin (WRR) arbitration policy of wheel
               size Ki = 5 for a resource i with two requestors, x and y, which have
               Wix = 2 and Wiy = 3 reserved time slots per arbitration period, respectively.


periodic slots on i receives a dedicated periodic resource time budget of (Wix · Si ) and,
hence, experiences an aggregate wait time of (Pi −Wix · Si ) per arbitration period Pi .
In this work, Wix is referred to as the arbitration weight of requestor x on resource i.
   TDMA is a non-work-conserving arbitration policy [JW04]. That means, a shared
resource under TDMA arbitration remains idle during the time slots that are not used
by their assigned requestors even if pending requests are present from other requestors.
This is visualized in Figure 2.4 where the white slots represent unused arbitration slots
in which i remains idle. Since resource requestors typically do not utilize each and
every one of their reserved slots, TDMA arbitration often results in a considerable
under-utilization of resources and a poor average-case performance. This renders
TDMA a rather unattractive arbitration policy for composable systems in which
resources may be shared among real-time and best-effort applications [KHM+ 13].

Weighted Round Robin (WRR) Arbitration

WRR has fundamental similarities to TDMA in that it divides time into a series of
equally long and periodically arbitrated time slots. However, unlike TDMA, WRR
is a work-conserving arbitration policy in which unused time slots (regardless of
them being reserved or not) are skipped to minimize the wait time of requestors
and the idle time of the resource, see [QLD10; HKK+ 13]. This scheme results
in a dynamic adjustment of the length of each arbitration period according to the
number of active requests in that period. Compared to TDMA, this adaptability
leaves the worst-case timing guarantees derived for real-time applications intact while
improving the average-case performance considerably, e.g., in favor of best-effort
applications [HKK+ 13].
   Figure 2.5 illustrates an example of WRR arbitration. The WRR arbitration policy
of a resource i can be described by three parameters: (i) the length Si of each arbitration
slot, (ii) the length Di of each control slot, and (iii) the maximum arbitration wheel
size Ki . Based on these parameters, the maximum length of an arbitration period Pi



26
                                   2.2 Real-Time Execution on Many-Core Platforms


is calculated as Pi = Ki · (Si + Di ). However, depending on the number of present
requests, the actual length of an arbitration period may be smaller than Pi .
   Note that under WRR, each requestor of resource i is guaranteed to receive its
reserved number of periodic time slots in any sequence of Ki consecutive time slots.
Therefore, worst-case timing guarantees can be derived for each requestor based on
its reserved number of periodic time slots and the arbitration parameters (Si , Di , and
Ki ) similarly to TDMA. Here, a requestor x which has reserved Wix ∈ N+        ≤Ki periodic
arbitration slots on resource i attains (upon request) an aggregate dedicated resource
time of at least (Wix · Si ) in each time window of length Pi . This results in a maximum
aggregate wait time of (Pi −Wix · Si ) per time window Pi for x on resource i.

2.2.5 Invasive Computing
Heterogeneous many-core platforms are widely recognized as viable solutions for the
design of emerging and future embedded systems. These systems typically encompass
a large number of applications with various non-functional requirements, including
hard timing constraints, and oftentimes exhibit dynamic execution and workload
profiles throughout their lifespan. A successful adoption of many-core platforms
for the design of such systems, however, necessitates a reconsideration of existing
design and programming practices and the introduction of novel design methodologies
to address the major design challenges of emerging embedded systems targeting
many-core platforms. In particular, programmability, adaptivity, scalability, power and
thermal constraints, real-time capability, reliability, and fault tolerance are envisioned
to constitute the major design challenges of contemporary and future embedded
systems targeting large-scale heterogeneous many-core platforms [THH+ 11].
   In response to these challenges, Teich [Tei08] has proposed a novel programming
paradigm called invasive computing which addresses the major aspects of the design
challenges outlined above by introducing the concept of resource-aware programming.
In invasive computing, applications are empowered to (i) negotiate their resource
requirements with the system manager online to attain their required resources in
view of a dynamic availability of resources, (ii) distribute their workload on the
resources provided to them, and (iii) release these resources upon the completion of
their execution. In this scope, Teich et al. define invasive programming as follows:

  Invasive Programming [THH+ 11]: Invasive programming denotes the capability
  of a program running on a parallel computer to request and temporarily claim
  processor, communication, and memory resources in the neighborhood of its
  actual computing environment, to then execute in parallel the given program using
  these claimed resources, and to be capable to subsequently free these resources
  again.




                                                                                       27
2 Fundamentals



          start         invade          infect          retreat           exit

     Figure 2.6: Execution phases of an invasive program according to [HRS+ 11].


   Based on this definition, an invasive computing scheme can be established by pro-
viding language and system support for invasive programming through three identified
basic programming constructs, namely, invade, infect, and retreat, to enable resource-
aware application programming. The execution phases of an invasive program in
terms of these three primitives are shown in Figure 2.6, adopted from [HRS+ 11]. As
illustrated, the execution of an invasive program begins with issuing an invade call to
request an initial claim of resources denoting a set of processing, communication, and
memory resources required for the execution of the program. Following a successful
invasion (allocation) of resources, the program can deploy and execute its code on the
claimed resources using an infect call. During its execution, it can infect its claimed
resources several times (also called re-infect), e.g., to perform various processes on
the claimed resources. Once the execution of the program is completed, it can release
the claimed resources using the retreat call to terminate and exit. A program may also
request an adaptation of its claimed resources during its execution to shrink (using
retreat) or expand (using invade) its set of claimed resources, e.g., in reaction to a
change in its workload.
   Invasive primitives, namely, invade, infect, and retreat, can be introduced into
many programming languages to attain resource awareness. For instance, the library
InvadeX10 [HRS+ 11] introduces these primitives into the X10 programming lan-
guage [CGS+ 05] from IBM. Provided with these language constructs, the application
programmer can specify the set of resources required for the satisfactory execution
of the application w.r.t. its non-functional requirements. Alternatively, invasive com-
puting also provides X10 language support for the programmer to specify the desired
range for each non-functional property of interest (e.g., timing, power consumption,
reliability, and security) in terms of so-called requirements [TGR+ 16] which denote
soft/hard upper- and lower bound constraints on the non-functional property in ques-
tion. Based on these user-specified requirements, a suitable claim of resources can be
derived, e.g., using system-level Design Space Exploration (DSE) techniques, and the
program can be subsequently augmented with the derived claim of resources.
   The description above elucidates the capability of invasive computing in establishing
an adaptive execution of applications on many-core platforms by means of resource-
aware programming. The exclusive reservation of resources (or resource budgets) per
application in invasive computing can be seen as an enabler of timing composability in
a system by enforcing spatial/temporal isolation between concurrent applications. This,
in turn, enables a scalable (per-application) design and a non-functional verification
scheme which is particularly crucial in the context of hard real-time applications and



28
                                                                          2.3 System Model


systems. In the scope of this work, invasive computing serves as a promising solution
to provide the features and fundamentals based on which the methodologies, analyses,
and techniques proposed in this work can be incorporated into the design practice of
emerging embedded systems to empower an adaptive and satisfactory execution of
hard real-time applications on heterogeneous many-core platforms.


2.3 System Model
This section introduces the system model used in this work. The system-level design
of an embedded system—consisting of a set of applications to be executed on a given
many-core platform—mainly involves the mapping of the applications in the system
onto the resources of the platform. The description of the applications and the platform
architecture are initially provided in a Systems-Level Design Language (SLDL), e.g.,
SysteMoC [FHT06]. This description is then transformed into a symbolic system
model that is used for the mapping of applications onto platform resources.
   In this work, the graph-based system model as introduced first in [BTT98] is used
which is referred to as the specification, see [LSG+ 09] for a SLDL-based transforma-
tion. The specification consists of graph-based representations of each application and
the platform architecture as well as a set of attributes reflecting certain non-functional
properties of application tasks and platform resources. This representation encapsu-
lates all necessary information for the mapping of applications onto a platform and the
evaluation of the quality of obtained mapping solutions w.r.t. a set of non-functional
properties of interest, e.g., timeliness and energy consumption.
   In the following, the building blocks of the specification used in this work are
presented as follows: In Sections 2.3.1 and 2.3.2, the application model and the
architecture model are presented, respectively, and their graph-based representations
used in this work are introduced. Afterwards, Section 2.3.3 introduces mapping edges
that link the application graph to the architecture graph and reflect the design space
for the application mapping process.

2.3.1 Application Model
In the context of multi/many-core systems, applications are typically partitioned into
a set of processing tasks that can be executed in parallel on different resources—
and exchange data with each other, if necessary—to exploit the parallel processing
potential of the underlying platform [Moy13; Mar06]. In the system model used in this
work, an application is specified by a so-called application graph Gapp (Vapp , Eapp ), also
referred to as problem graph [BTT98] or task graph [DRW98]. Here, Vapp = T ∪ M is
a bipartite set of vertices where T = {t 0 , ...,t |T |−1 } denotes the set of (processing) tasks
of the application, and M = {m0 , ..., m|M|−1 } denotes the set of messages exchanged
among the tasks. The notations |T | and |M| denote the cardinality of sets T and M,

          PLDm0 = 700
          PRDm0 = 1200 µs

Figure 2.7: Example of an application graph Gapp (Vapp , Eapp ) where Vapp = T ∪ M
            contains tasks T = {t 0 , ...,t 5 } and messages M = {m0 , ..., mm }, and edges

respectively. The directed edges Eapp ⊆ (T × M) ∪ (M × T ) in the application graph
specify the data dependencies (partial order) between tasks and messages. Each data
dependency e ∈ Eapp connects a task to a message or vice versa. An example of an
application graph is shown in Figure 2.7.
   In the applications considered in this work, each message m ∈ M has exactly one
predecessor (producing task) and one successor (consuming task). Here, convenience
functions IN(t) ⊆ M and OUT(t) ⊆ M are used to provide the set of input and output
messages of task t, respectively. The union set of input and output messages of task t
is provided by function IO(t) = IN(t) ∪ OUT(t) ⊆ M.
   This work considers applications with hard real-time constraints on their end-to-end
latency (makespan) referred to as the application deadline. A mapping of such an
application is considered useful only if it is guaranteed to meet the deadline of the
application when executing it on a given platform. In the application graph, tasks
and messages are annotated with a set of attributes presented in the following. The
attributes are used during the application mapping process and/or for the evaluation of
the non-functional properties of mappings, e.g., worst-case timing guarantees.

Task and Message Attributes

Each task t ∈ T in the application graph is annotated with its maximum memory
demand, i.e., the maximum number of single-word memory accesses it may perform
during its execution, denoted by MDt . Each task is also annotated with an execution
period PRDt which specifies the (minimum) inter-arrival time of its input message(s).
Similarly, each message m ∈ M is annotated with its maximum payload size PLDm (in
number of words) as well as its transfer period PRDm which denotes the (minimum)
inter-production time of that message.



30
                                                                          2.3 System Model


                r0                         r1                                 r2

    tile u0 n               tile u1        n1                 tile u2         n2
             0


      q0        b0            q1      b1        b2   q2        q3        b3        b4        q4


           c0        c1       c2      c3        c4   c5             c6        c7        c8

Figure 2.8: Example of an architecture graph for a heterogeneous many-core platform
                composed of three tiles of different types (from Figure 2.2) with a 3 × 1
                NoC organization. Core types are denoted by color (blue, green, and red).

2.3.2 Architecture Model
In the system model of this work, the architecture of a many-core platform is spec-
ified by a so-called architecture graph Garch (Varch , Earch ). Here, the set of vertices
Varch = C ∪ Q ∪ B ∪ N ∪ R represents the resources of the platform where each c ∈ C de-
notes a processing core, each q ∈ Q a memory, each b ∈ B a bus, each n ∈ N a Network
Adapter (NA), and each r ∈ R a router of the NoC. The edges Earch ⊆ Varch ×Varch
represent physical connections (links) between the resources. Non-router resources
of the architecture, i.e., cores, memories, buses, and NAs, are organized as a set U
of tiles where each tile u ∈ U consists of a NA, the buses connected to it, and all
cores and memories connected to these buses. Each non-router resource belongs to
exactly one tile, hence, tiles are (i) disjoint, i.e., ui ∩ u j = 0/ for all ui , u j ∈ U where
i ̸= j, and (ii) covering, i.e., ∪u∈U u = C ∪ Q ∪ B ∪ N. In this work, convenience func-
tion TILE(i) ∈ U is used to provide the tile u ∈ U on which the non-router resource
i ∈ C ∪ Q ∪ B ∪ N is located. Figure 2.8 illustrates an example of an architecture graph
for a heterogeneous many-core platform consisting of three tiles with a 3 × 1 NoC
organization. The graph-based representation of the tiles in Figure 2.8 corresponds to
the three tile architectures depicted in Figure 2.2.

Resource Attributes

Each resource in the architecture graph is annotated with a set of attributes that reflect
the necessary non-functional properties of that resource. In the system model of this
work, each memory q ∈ Q in the architecture graph is annotated with its basic service
time ST q which denotes the time required to perform a single-word memory operation
(read/write) in isolation. The service time ST q of a memory q ∈ Q comprises the
latency of address/data-exchange cycles and the intrinsic delay of the memory.
   Since this work considers composable systems, any shared resource must have a


                                                                                                  31
2 Fundamentals


composable arbitration policy, e.g., TDMA or WRR. As detailed in Section 2.2.4,
such an arbitration policy can be described by (i) the length S of each arbitration slot,
(ii) the maximum delay D of the arbiter, and (iii) the arbitration wheel size K. In this
line, the arbitration parameters of resources are provided as follows:

     • Each bus b ∈ B is annotated with its (maximum) arbitration wheel size Kb , slot
       length Sb , and arbitration delay Db .
     • Each core c ∈ C is annotated with its scheduling wheel size Kc , scheduling slot
       length Sc , and scheduling delay Dc . The latter corresponds to the context-switch
       overhead of the operating system. Each core c ∈ C is also annotated with its
       arbitration weight Wbc on each local bus b ∈ B over which c accesses memories.
     • Each NA n ∈ N is annotated with the arbitration parameters of its TX unit tx,
       namely, Ktx , Stx , and Dtx , and those of its RX unit rx, namely, Krx , Srx , and Drx .
       It is also annotated with the arbitration weight of its TX and RX units on each
       local bus b ∈ B used to access memories, denoted by Wbtx and Wbrx , respectively.
     • Each router r ∈ R is annotated with its routing delay Dr as well as the arbitration
       capacity Kr and slot length Sr of each of its output ports.

2.3.3 Mapping Edges
In the specification, the application and architecture graphs are connected by a set
of so-called mapping edges V ⊆ T ×C that reflect the design space of possible task-
to-core assignments. A mapping edge v = (t, c) ∈ V denotes that task t ∈ T can be
executed on core c ∈ C. Each mapping edge v = (t, c) ∈ V may also be annotated
with a set of attributes which reflect non-functional properties of task t when executed
on core c. In the system model of this work, each mapping edge v = (t, c) ∈ V is
annotated with the WCET of task t on core c, denoted by WCET t,c .
   Contrarily to tasks, messages are not subject to predetermined restrictions regarding
their choices of storage and communication resources. That means, each message
m ∈ M may be stored in any memory q ∈ Q in the architecture and, in the case of
an inter-tile message, it may be transferred via any NoC route between its source
and destination tiles. Therefore, it is not required to explicitly denote the mapping
possibilities of messages in the specification. As will be detailed in Section 2.4.1, the
choice of memory and (in the case of inter-tile communications) NoC route for each
message depends on the mapping of its producing and consuming tasks to cores and
the routing algorithm of the NoC, respectively.

2.3.4 Execution and Operation Model
In an application specified by application graph Gapp (T ∪ M, Eapp ), the execution of
tasks T and the transfer of messages M between them must follow the order reflected



32
                                     2.4 Application Mapping in Many-Core Systems


by the data dependencies Eapp . Each iteration of execution for a task t ∈ T can be
initiated only after one instance of each of its input messages m ∈ IN(t) is available in
the memories of the tile on which t is executed. For instance, the execution of task t 5
in Figure 2.7 can be initiated only if one instance of each input message m3 , m4 , and
m5 is locally available on the tile where t is to be executed. During its execution, a task
t produces one instance of each of its output messages m ∈ OUT(t) and stores them in
the memories of the tile on which it is executed. The maximum number of memory
operations performed for reading/writing input/output messages from/to the memory
is incorporated in the memory demand MDt attribute of task t. Memory operations are
considered to be blocking, thus, the execution of a task is stalled during its memory
operations. Each memory operation corresponds to a single-word memory access with
a service time of ST q for each memory q ∈ Q which is given as an attribute of q.
   In general, resources of a many-core platform may be shared among multiple
requestors unless exclusively reserved. Recall from Section 2.2.4 that a composable
arbitration of shared resources necessitates a time-triggered arbitration scheme. Hence,
to enable a composable arbitration of memory buses between the resources located on
each tile, burst memory operations are excluded which enables a semi-instantaneous
switching between different requestors. Here, for accessing a block of data, a requestor
may perform several consecutive single-word memory operations during its reserved
time slot(s) on the bus. Likewise, to preserve composability also when sharing cores
between multiple tasks, a preemptive execution model [DB11] is considered. This
enables the operating system to halt the execution of each task preemptively to have
its processing time confined within its reserved scheduling time slot(s). Similarly, to
preserve composability when sharing NoC routes between concurrent communication
flows, wormhole-switched NoCs with credit-based virtual-channel flow control are
considered. This allows per-link bandwidth reservation and time-triggered preemption
of each communication flow at each router [HKK+ 13]. In such NoCs, the arbitration
slot length Sr of each router r ∈ R is typically equal to the duration of one clock cycle
of the NoC which suffices for the transfer of one flit of data over a link.


2.4 Application Mapping in Many-Core Systems
During the design process of an embedded system based on a given many-core
platform, the applications in the system are mapped onto the resources of the platform.
This section provides an overview of the current practice of application mapping in
many-core systems as follows: First, Section 2.4.1 presents the standard procedure
of mapping an application on a many-core platform. Thereafter, Sections 2.4.2
and 2.4.3 provide an overview and classification of existing mapping schemes and
methodologies for multi/many-core systems consisting of several applications. Finally,
Section 2.4.4 introduces the mapping methodology used in this work, namely, Hybrid
Application Mapping (HAM), and elucidates its key characteristics which are of



                                                                                       33
2 Fundamentals


principal significance for the design of adaptive hard real-time embedded systems.

2.4.1 Application Mapping Procedure
The process of mapping an application onto a many-core platform is realized using the
system-level synthesis procedure introduced in [BTT98]. In line with this procedure,
the specification presented in Section 2.3 is used to compute a so-called mapping1 of
the application on the platform by conducting four sets of design decisions, namely,
binding, routing, allocation, and scheduling, as detailed in the following.

Binding

Binding design decisions determine the assignment of tasks T to cores C and the
assignment of messages M to memories Q. These assignments are provided by a
so-called binding β = β task ∪ β msg and are derived as follows: First, the binding β task
of tasks to cores, formally defined in Eq. (2.2), is determined by selecting exactly one
mapping edge2 v = (t, c) ∈ V for each task t ∈ T , denoting that task t is mapped to
core c ∈ C. In this work, convenience function CORE(t, β ) provides the core c ∈ C
to which task t ∈ T is mapped in binding β . After the binding β task of tasks to cores
has been determined, the binding β msg of messages to memories, formally defined
in Eq. (2.3), is determined in such a way that each message m ∈ M is mapped (i) to
exactly one memory on the tile where it is produced and (ii) to exactly one memory
on the tile where it is consumed. Here, (m, q) ∈ β msg denotes that message m ∈ M is
to be stored in memory q ∈ Q.

                           β task = (t, c) | t ∈ T ∧ c ∈ C
                                   
                                                                                    (2.2)
                             msg
                                   
                           β      = (m, q) | m ∈ M ∧ q ∈ Q                          (2.3)

Routing

Routing design decisions determine a NoC route for each inter-tile message, i.e.,
each message that is communicated between tasks which are mapped to different
tiles. The route of an inter-tile message m ∈ M establishes a direct path between the
source and destination tiles of m over the NoC and is denoted by a non-repeating
sequence or tuple (r̂) ⊆ R ∪ N of connected routers and NAs (r̂ ∈ R ∪ N). The route
of each inter-tile message is determined in accordance with the routing algorithm
of the NoC, e.g., XY-routing [NM93]. The routes of messages are provided as a
so-called routing ρ, formally defined in Eq. (2.4). In this work, convenience function
ROUTE(m, ρ) provides the NoC route of message m ∈ M in routing ρ. Moreover, the
sequence of routers located on the NoC route of a message m ∈ M (excluding the

any message m ∈ M produced and consumed on the same tile, ROUTE(m, ρ) = 0/ as m
does not require a NoC transfer.

Allocation

Allocation design decisions determine the set of resources used during the execution of
tasks T and/or transfer of messages M as a so-called allocation α ⊆ C ∪ Q ∪ B ∪ N ∪ R.
Based on the binding and routing decisions, the allocation α contains (i) the cores to
which tasks are mapped (specified by β task ), (ii) the memories to which messages are
mapped (specified by β msg ), and (iii) the NoC routers and NAs over which inter-tile
messages are routed (specified by ρ). In addition to that, the allocation α also includes
(iv) the buses connecting the allocated cores and NAs to the allocated memories on
their tile as these buses are used to access memories.

Scheduling

Scheduling design decisions specify the budget of each task/message on the (possibly
shared) resources it uses. Given the arbitration parameters of resources (provided as
resource attributes in the architecture graph), a so-called schedule σ = σ task ∪ σ msg is
specified: Here, the schedule σ task of tasks, formally defined by Eq. (2.5), denotes for
each task t ∈ T , the arbitration weight Wct , i.e., the number of periodic time slots, to be
reserved for task t on the core to which t is mapped, i.e., c = CORE(t, β ). Likewise, the
schedule of messages σ msg , formally defined by Eq. (2.6), denotes for each inter-tile
message m ∈ M, the arbitration weight W(r̂)  m of m on each NoC resource (router or NA)

Mapping Feasibility

The result of the binding, routing, allocation, and scheduling design decisions is
a mapping µ of the application on the given many-core architecture, specified as
µ = (βµ , ρµ , αµ , σµ ). A mapping µ is feasible if its constituting components, namely,
βµ , ρµ , αµ , and σµ , satisfy the following conditions:

    • Binding (βµ ): Each task t ∈ T must be mapped to exactly one core c ∈ C
      according to t’s mapping possibilities denoted by the mapping edges in the


                                                                                             35
2 Fundamentals


       specification. Each message m ∈ M must be mapped to exactly one memory
       q ∈ Q on each tile where m is produced or consumed.
     • Routing (ρµ ): Each inter-tile message m ∈ M must have an acyclic NoC route
       that connects m’s source tile to its destination tile.
     • Allocation (αµ ): The following resources must be allocated: Every core c ∈ C
       with at least one task mapped to it, every memory q ∈ Q with at least one
       message mapped to it, every NoC router r ∈ R and every NA n ∈ N with at least
       one message routed over it, and every bus b ∈ B that connects the allocated
       cores and NAs to the allocated memories on the respective tile.
     • Schedule (σµ ): Each task t ∈ T must have a positive arbitration weight Wct on
       the core c ∈ C to which it is mapped. The overall arbitration weight assigned
       to the tasks that are mapped to a core c ∈ C shall not exceed c’s arbitration
       capacity Kc . Each inter-tile message m ∈ M must have a positive arbitration
                  m on each router and each NA (TX/RX unit) located on its route
       weight W(r̂)
       (r̂) ⊆ R ∪ N. The overall arbitration weight assigned to messages routed over a
       router or NA (TX/RX unit) i ∈ R ∪ N shall not exceed i’s arbitration capacity Ki .

where tasks t 0 , t 1 , t 2 , and t 3 have arbitration weights of 2, 4, 3, and 8 on their respective
cores, and m0 has an arbitration weight of 5 on each resource on its NoC route. The
mapping can be formally described as µ = (βµ , ρµ , αµ , σµ ) where:

2.4.2 Mapping Schemes for Multi-Application Systems
Emerging embedded systems typically comprise several applications to be executed
on a many-core platform. In this context, the goal of the design process is to find an
optimal mapping of all applications on the platform. This, however, is a challenging
task: As shown in [BTT98], even finding one feasible mapping of a single application
on a many-core platform is an NP-complete problem. Consequently, finding an
optimal mapping of an application on a many-core platform embodies an NP-hard
optimization problem [GJ79]. The progressive increase in the number of applications
integrated into a modern embedded system contributes further to the complexity of
the design process as the space of possible mapping solutions grows exponentially
with the overall number of tasks in the system.
   Recent years also give evidence of a rapid increase in the dynamism of application
workloads in embedded systems which also contributes considerably to the complexity
of the system design process: In a dynamic system, each application may be launched
and terminated on-demand, independently from other applications, and oftentimes
in reaction to unforeseeable external events, e.g., user requests. In such systems,
often only a fraction of all applications are simultaneously active at each point in time.
Finding an optimal design in this context entails the consideration of all possible system
use-cases—each denoting one combination of concurrently executed applications. The
number of possible system use-cases, however, grows exponentially with the number
of independent applications in the system and can become intractable in modern
embedded systems which comprise a large number of applications [AMH+ 11].
   Over the past decade, various methodologies have been introduced for the design of
multi-application embedded systems based on many-core platforms. These methodolo-
gies can be classified into two general categories: (i) integrated mapping approaches
which are inherited from the multi-core mapping paradigm and (ii) constructive map-
ping approaches which have emerged to alleviate the shortcomings of the former in


                                                                                                        37
2 Fundamentals


addressing the design complexity encountered in the context of mapping on many-core
platforms. In the following, these classes are introduced.

Integrated Mapping Schemes

The process of mapping the applications of an embedded system onto a given target
platform is traditionally realized based on an integrated mapping scheme. In such a
scheme, all applications are considered at the same time within one mapping process,
and mapping decisions are conducted for all applications simultaneously. This inte-
grated scheme performs well for small systems consisting of one or only a few small
applications with a relatively static workload targeting a platform with a relatively
small number of resources, e.g., a multi-core platform. However, due to its poor
scalability w.r.t. the number of applications in the system, the number of resources
in the platform, and the number of system use-cases, this scheme is not considered
viable for the design of emerging embedded systems comprising a large number of
applications with a dynamic workload profile targeting many-core platforms.

Constructive Mapping Schemes

With the introduction of many-core platforms in embedded domains, the traditional
integrated mapping scheme has been gradually replaced by a constructive scheme
to alleviate the design complexity [AMH+ 11]. In a constructive mapping approach,
the mapping process of the system is partitioned into two phases: First, (i) a per-
application mapping computation phase is performed in which one or a set of high-
quality mappings are computed for each application individually. Thereafter, in
(ii) a system integration phase, the precomputed mappings of the applications are
used to assemble the whole system. In systems designed based on a constructive
approach, system composability ensures that the non-functional properties of each
mapping evaluated during the per-application mapping computation phase also hold
in the presence of the other applications in the system after the integration [Kop11;
AMH+ 11], see Section 2.2.4.
   A constructive mapping scheme, on the one hand, reduces the design complexity
of a multi-application system to the complexity of individual application mapping
processes. On the other hand, this simplification allows parts of the resulting less-
demanding design steps to be conducted at run time. Such a shift enables an adaptive
mapping scheme in which run-time situations, e.g., workload fluctuations, are taken
into consideration during the mapping process of applications [SSK+ 13]. This poten-
tial has led to the emergence of different classes of many-core application mapping
methodologies which are outlined in the following.




38
                                    2.4 Application Mapping in Many-Core Systems


2.4.3 Application Mapping Methodologies
Application mapping is one of the most crucial aspects of system design based on
many-core platforms [MOP+ 09]. As mentioned before, optimally mapping an applica-
tion onto a given many-core platform has been shown to be an NP-hard optimization
problem. Using an exact optimization approach, e.g., Integer Linear Programming
(ILP), to find optimal mappings is, thus, impractical due to the intractable design
space of possible mappings. In this context, Design Space Exploration (DSE) schemes
based on meta-heuristic optimization techniques, e.g., evolutionary algorithms [Dav91;
FF95], simulated annealing [KGV83], or particle swarm optimization [Ken10], have
become the de facto standard approaches for many-core mapping optimization as
they have proven effective in delivering high-quality mappings at the expense of an
affordable computational effort, see [BTT98].
   In addition to their choice of the optimization technique, application mapping
methodologies employed for the constructive design of embedded systems can be
classified based on when they perform the mapping optimization and/or the system
integration. In this scope, [SSK+ 13] classifies multi/many-core application mapping
approaches into two categories: (i) static (also called offline or design-time) mapping
methodologies and (ii) dynamic (also called online or run-time) mapping methodolo-
gies. In the following, an overview of these methodologies is provided before the
application mapping methodology used in this work is presented in Section 2.4.4.

Static Application Mapping

In a static application mapping approach, all mapping decisions are conducted at
design time: First, DSE is performed to find an optimal mapping for each application,
and then, system integration is performed using the mappings computed for the
applications. This design scheme results in a fixed (static) mapping of applications
onto the platform resources once the offline design process is finished [SSK+ 13]. Since
they conduct all design decisions offline, static mapping approaches require global
knowledge about all applications in the system and can exploit this holistic system
view to find an optimal mapping for each application.
   The offline design scheme of static mapping approaches enables the use of (i) com-
putationally intensive mapping optimization procedures, see, e.g., [BBL+ 10; HM05;
MBS+ 05; LWH+ 05], to find high-quality mappings as well as (ii) complex non-
functional verification techniques to evaluate the quality of each mapping w.r.t. the
non-functional properties of interest. In an embedded system, such a verification
process is often crucial for the quality assessment of mappings, e.g., timing verifica-
tion for hard real-time applications. Due to the inflexibility of their design solutions,
however, static mapping approaches perform well only in the case of systems with
a relatively static workload. Therefore, they lose practicality in the case of dynamic
systems or for systems where the whole set of applications is not known statically.



                                                                                     39
2 Fundamentals


Dynamic Application Mapping

Dynamic application mapping approaches alleviate the inflexibility of static mapping
approaches by conducting mapping decisions at run time when an application must
be launched. To that end, a so-called Run-time Platform Manager (RPM) is present
in the system (e.g., as part of the operating system) which supervises the system by
(i) launching each application on-demand according to the availability of resources,
(ii) removing a running application upon a termination request, and (iii) adapting
the mapping of running applications (if necessary), see, e.g., [KBL+ 11]. A dynamic
mapping scheme reduces the complexity of application mapping by eliminating the
need for a prior consideration of all possible system use-case and resource-availability
scenarios in the mapping procedure since the system state is known when mapping
decisions take place. By taking into account the current system state in their mapping
decisions, dynamic mapping approaches enable an adaptive mapping scheme for the
design of dynamic many-core systems [SSK+ 13].
   This advantage, however, comes at the expense of increased time pressure for
the application mapping process since the overhead of this process directly affects
the system’s performance. Due to the limited time and computational capacity at
run time, dynamic mapping methodologies cannot afford compute-intensive mapping
optimization procedures. Instead, they must resort to lightweight heuristics to compute
mappings in an iterative and/or incremental (task-by-task) manner which typically
results in sub-optimal mappings, see, e.g., [COM08]. Furthermore, they also cannot
afford compute-heavy procedures for the non-functional verification of their obtained
mappings and, therefore, lose applicability for applications that depend on such
verification processes, e.g., hard real-time applications.

2.4.4 Hybrid Application Mapping (HAM)
In recent years, a new class of application mapping methodologies has emerged which
combines static and dynamic mapping methodologies in a hybrid (offline/online)
mapping scheme to exploit the advantages of each, while disposing of their shortcom-
ings, see [SSK+ 13; WWG+ 18b; PGH+ 20] for an overview. This so-called Hybrid
Application Mapping (HAM) scheme employs (i) DSE at design time to find a set of
(Pareto-)optimal mappings for each application, followed by (ii) system management
at run time where a suitable precomputed mapping is selected dynamically when an
application must be launched. The design flow of HAM is illustrated in Figure 2.10
and elucidated in the following.

Design-Time Design Space Exploration (DSE)

In HAM, mapping optimization is performed for each application separately at design
time using DSE, see Figure 2.10 (top). For each application, the DSE takes as input the



40
                                        2.4 Application Mapping in Many-Core Systems


specification (consisting of the application graph, the architecture graph, and the map-
ping edges3 as detailed in Section 2.3. The DSE embodies a multi-objective mapping
optimization process in which mappings are (i) generated using the application map-
ping procedure detailed in Section 2.4.1 (by conducting binding, routing, allocation,
and scheduling decisions). The quality of each generated mapping is then (ii) evaluated
w.r.t. multiple design objectives such as timing (see, e.g., [WGW+ 14; WWG+ 18a]),
reliability (see, e.g., [HWZ+ 15; WWG+ 18b]), security (see, e.g., [WWG+ 16]), energy
consumption, and resource usage. Non-functional requirements of each application,
e.g., real-time constraints, are expressed as constrained objectives whereby map-
pings with unsatisfactory behavior w.r.t. the application requirements are marked as
infeasible and dismissed during the optimization process. Subject to the choice of
non-functional qualities of interest, the evaluation can be realized using measurement-
based, simulation-based, analytical, or statistical techniques.
   During the optimization process, the DSE collects a set of mappings with Pareto-
optimal qualities w.r.t. the design objectives. These mappings will be provided to
the RPM to be used at run time, e.g., to launch applications on-demand. An efficient
DSE of mappings can be achieved using the so-called constraint graphs introduced
in [WGW+ 14]. A constraint graph represents a class of mappings abstractly by
specifying (i) a partitioning of the tasks into a set of task clusters, (ii) an assignment
of each task cluster to a core type, (iii) the maximum distance (NoC hops) allowed
between each pair of tasks clusters containing data-dependent tasks, and (iv) the
required NoC budget between data-dependent task clusters. Each constraint graph
may have several concrete mapping realizations on a given many-core architecture.
In [SWG+ 17], based on constraint graphs, a reformulation of the mapping problem
into a problem of task clustering and resource-type mapping is introduced which
enables an efficient DSE of applications on many-core architectures by eliminating
redundant, symmetric mappings from the search space.

Run-Time System Management

In HAM, the set of Pareto-optimal mappings computed for each application during
the offline DSE are provided to a Run-time Platform Manager (RPM) which launches
and terminates applications dynamically on-demand and coordinates the deployment
of applications in the system, see Figure 2.10 (bottom). To that end, the RPM employs
a (i) management policy that steers its decisions based on the current state of the
systems, e.g., the availability of resources, and non-functional requirements of running
and incoming applications, e.g., real-time constraints. Whenever an application shall
be launched, the RPM considers the precomputed mappings of that application to
select (or, optionally, rank and filter) mapping candidates w.r.t. the criteria above and
 3 For clarity of visualization, in Figure 2.10, all mapping edges of one task to any core of the

   architecture are summarized as an abstract mapping edge marked with the types (colors) of target
   cores. For instance, task t 1 can be mapped onto green or blue cores.



                                                                                                41
2 Fundamentals


in line with its management policy. The admissibility of each mapping candidate in
view of the current system state and application requirements is examined using a set
of (ii) admission checks in terms of, e.g., the availability of required resources for each
mapping, its energy/power consumption, timeliness, etc. Note that in the case of a
constraint-graph-based representation of mapping classes, the embedding feasibility of
each constraint graph can be checked using exact constraint-solving approaches, e.g.,
SAT [SWG+ 17], or by means of heuristics, e.g., backtracking algorithms [WGW+ 14;
WWG+ 18a]. Once a suitable mapping is identified which passes all admission checks,
the RPM uses that mapping to launch the application. It may also change the mapping
of a running application during its execution in a similar procedure, e.g., to evacuate
an overheated core. Once the execution of an application is finished, the RPM removes
it from the platform and releases its resources to be used later by other running or
incoming applications.

HAM for Hard Real-Time Applications

The shortcomings of static and dynamic application mapping methodologies render
these approaches unfit for the design of dynamic embedded systems with hard real-time
constraints targeting (heterogeneous) many-core platforms: Static mapping approaches
fail in offering the necessary flexibility and mapping adaptability for systems with
dynamic workload profiles, and dynamic mapping approaches fail in providing the
necessary timing guarantees for hard real-time applications.
   The hybrid design flow of HAM methodologies combines the advantages of both
static and dynamic methodologies while disposing of their major shortcomings: Simi-
larly to static mapping approaches, the offline DSE in HAM can benefit from computa-
tionally intensive optimization techniques and non-functional verification procedures.
While the former enables finding high-quality mappings, the latter is crucial for appli-
cations that rely on complex verification processes to obtain non-functional guarantees,
e.g., hard real-time guarantees. On the other hand, similarly to dynamic mapping
approaches, HAM approaches determine (select) the mapping to be used for each
application at run time which empowers them to incorporate the available information
about the current system state, e.g., resource availability, into their mapping selection
procedure. This enables an adaptive application mapping and resource management
scheme which is crucial for systems with dynamic workload profiles.
   The features and characteristics of HAM render it a particularly promising scheme
for the design of emerging embedded systems with hard real-time applications and
dynamic workload profiles targeting heterogeneous many-core platforms. On this
account, the methodologies, techniques, and analyses proposed in this work are
developed based on this hybrid mapping scheme. The contributions of this work
enable a timing-predictable, resource-efficient, thermally safe and composable, and
adaptable execution of hard real-time applications with strict timing guarantees in
heterogeneous many-core systems.

                                                                                           3
           Optimization and Timing Analysis

Timing composability and timing predictability are crucial system properties neces-
sary for the adoption of many-core platforms in embedded systems with real-time
constraints. As detailed in Chapter 2, in a timing-composable system, concurrent
applications are isolated from each other by means of exclusive resource (or resource-
budget) reservation per application. In a timing-predictable system, every resource
has a predictable behavior which enables the use of formal analyses for the timing
verification of real-time applications. Providing timing guarantees for hard real-time
applications in such systems necessitates a proper choice of an (i) inter-application
isolation scheme which specifies rules as to how resources must be reserved per
application so that concurrent applications are temporally and/or spatially isolated
from each other. In light of that, a (ii) worst-case timing analysis is also required to
formally derive timing guarantees for each application based on its reserved resources
(or resource budgets).
   In the context of application mapping on many-core platforms, state-of-the-art
design practices address the two aspects of inter-application isolation and worst-case
timing analysis only to a limited extent: To provide worst-case timing guarantees
for an application, a timing analysis technique must be able to safely bound the
delay components and timing interferences that contribute to the worst-case timing
properties of that application. The timing analyses used in the scope of many-core
systems, however, often cannot analyze all possible timing interferences that can
arise in a many-core system in the general case. Thus, for providing safe worst-case
timing guarantees, these analyses necessarily rely on some restrictive assumptions,
e.g., about the platform architecture and/or the deployment of applications on it, to
ensure that those timing interferences that they cannot analyze also never occur1 . Such
assumptions result in a fixed inter-application isolation scheme (i.e., a fixed choice
of temporal/spatial isolation scheme for each resource) that is applied throughout the

 1 For instance, some analyses rely on the assumption that the architecture consists of single-core

   tiles only (due to their lack of bus and NA interference analysis) or the assumption of single-tile
   mappings only (due to their lack of NoC latency analysis). See Section 3.5 for a detailed discussion.



                                                                                                    45
3 Isolation-Aware Mapping Optimization and Timing Analysis


system and, thus, imposes a fixed resource-reservation policy on each mapping of each
application. Such a fixed policy often results in a mismatch between the amount of
resources reserved per application and its actual performance and timing requirements.
In turn, this mismatch manifests itself either in unsatisfactory timing guarantees (in
case reserved resources are insufficient) or in an under-utilization of resources (in case
reserved resources surpass the application’s demand).
   As a remedy, this chapter presents two contributions. Foremost, a (i) worst-case
timing analysis is proposed for (heterogeneous) many-core systems. It can formally
bound the worst-case timing properties of real-time applications in many-core systems
without being subject to the restrictive assumptions of the existing analyses. In light of
this timing analysis, the system is no longer restricted to a fixed inter-application iso-
lation scheme and resource-reservation policy. Instead, a flexible resource-reservation
policy can be employed in which the choice of temporal/spatial isolation scheme
applied to each resource in each mapping of each application can be adjusted flexibly.
This enables adjusting the amount of resources reserved per application according
to its performance and timing requirements. To that end, an (ii) isolation-aware
mapping optimization approach is presented which enables the mapping optimizer
to explore the choices of temporal/spatial isolation scheme for each resource used
in each mapping of each application. For mappings which employ a combination of
different isolation schemes on their allocated resources, the proposed timing analysis
is used to provide worst-case timing guarantees. By incorporating (i) and (ii) into
the Design Space Exploration (DSE) in Hybrid Application Mapping (HAM), the
mapping optimizer will automatically find mappings in which the amount of reserved
resources is optimized according to the performance and timing requirements of the
application so that its real-time constraints are satisfied in a resource-efficient manner.
   The remainder of this chapter is organized as follows2 . In Section 3.1, an overview
of the current practice of many-core application mapping w.r.t. inter-application
isolation schemes is provided, and the advantages of an isolation-aware application
mapping approach are outlined using an illustrative example. Then, in Section 3.2, the
proposed isolation-aware mapping optimization approach is presented2 , followed by
the proposed worst-case timing analysis presented in Section 3.3. The effectiveness of
mapping optimization based on the proposed approaches is demonstrated through a
series of experimental results presented2 in Section 3.4. Finally, an overview of related
work is provided in Section 3.5, followed by a summary of the chapter in Section 3.6.




 2 The contents of this chapter are based on and partly published in [PSW+ 19] which has appeared in

     the Proceedings of the 31st Euromicro Conference on Real-Time Systems (ECRTS). In particular,
     major parts of Sections 3.3 and 3.4 are published in [PSW+ 19].



46
                                                           3.1 Inter-Application Isolation


3.1 Inter-Application Isolation
Isolation between concurrent applications is a key principle in the design of real-time
systems as it enables establishing system composability, see Section 2.2.4. A sepa-
ration between concurrent applications w.r.t. the resources they use can be realized
by means of (i) spatial isolation where each resource is exclusively reserved for
one application. Alternatively, (ii) temporal isolation can be exerted which allows
resources to be shared among applications. The latter, however, necessitates a compos-
able arbitration policy, e.g., Time-Division Multiple Access (TDMA) or Weighted
Round-Robin (WRR), to regulate the maximum amount of temporal interference that
can be experienced by each requestor of a shared resource due to the presence of
other requestors. Through this regulation, a composable arbitration policy renders the
worst-case timing behavior of each requestor of a shared resource independent from
the presence and the behavior of other requestors, see Section 2.2.4.
   The choices of temporal and/or spatial isolation exerted between applications
on different resources within a system manifest the so-called (inter-application)
isolation scheme of that system. The isolation scheme of a system specifies a resource-
reservation policy which must be followed by each application in that system. It
regulates the amount of resources that must be reserved per running application and,
therefore, has a strong impact on the worst-case timing behavior of each application,
the resource utilization of the system, and other qualities that depend on the timing
and/or resource usage of each application, e.g., energy consumption. This impact
renders the choice of the inter-application isolation scheme a crucial design decision.

3.1.1 Inter-Application Isolation Schemes
The inter-application isolation schemes typically employed in many-core systems can
be categorized into three classes: Core-Sharing (CS), Core-Reservation (CR), and
Tile-Reservation (TR) isolation schemes. These schemes differ from each other w.r.t.
the choice of temporal/spatial isolation exerted on different resources located on each
tile of the underlying many-core platform. Noteworthy, sharing the Network-on-Chip
(NoC) between concurrent applications can hardly be avoided as it establishes the
necessary connectivity between different tiles of the platform [MTT18]. Therefore,
concurrent applications are typically temporally isolated from each other on the NoC.
    Figure 3.1 illustrates the three classes of many-core isolation schemes for an exem-
plary application with three tasks (t 0 , t 1 , and t 2 ) mapped to two cores (c0 and c1 ) of a
three-core tile on a many-core platform. For all cores c ∈ C, the arbitration capacity
as defined in Section 2.2.4 is assumed to be Kc = 5 which is visualized by the five
periodic scheduling time slots depicted per core. For a timely execution, t 0 requires a
minimum of three slots on c0 while t 1 and t 2 each require a minimum of two slots on
c1 . This resource demand can be derived by means of timing analysis. The reservation
state of each scheduling slot of each core is highlighted by color according to the color

3 Isolation-Aware Mapping Optimization and Timing Analysis

code in Figure 3.1 (bottom). In the following, the three classes of isolation schemes
are explained using this example.

Core-Sharing (CS)

In systems designed based on the so-called Core-Sharing (CS) isolation scheme,
each core may be shared among multiple applications, see Figure 3.1b. Here, the
core scheduling slots required for each task are reserved for the application at its
launch time while the remaining slots of each core can be used by other applications.
In Figure 3.1b, the application in question reserves three slots on core c0 for task t 0
and four slots on core c1 for tasks t 1 and t 2 . Hence, the remaining two slots on c0 , one
slot on c1 , and all five slots on c2 can be used by other applications.
   Note that the buses located on each tile enable memory access and, therefore, are
shared between the cores and the Network Adapter (NA) located on that tile. Moreover,
the NA on each tile is shared between all incoming and outgoing messages of that
tile. Hence, under the CS isolation scheme where multiple applications can use the
cores on a tile, they are automatically entitled to use the buses and the NA of that
tile in a shared manner. The CS isolation scheme, therefore, establishes temporal
isolation between concurrent applications on all resources. This renders CS the least
restrictive isolation scheme in many-core systems which results in a high degree of
inter-application timing interference, but also enables a high utilization of resources.




48
                                                                   3.1 Inter-Application Isolation


Core-Reservation (CR)

In systems designed based on the so-called Core-Reservation (CR) isolation scheme,
each core is exclusively reserved for one application, see Figure 3.1c. Compared to
CS, this reservation scheme eliminates inter-application timing interferences which
arise due to execution preemption on cores. Hence, it leads to tighter Worst-Case
Response Time (WCRT) bounds for tasks and, thereby, enhanced timing properties
for applications. Nonetheless, as it disregards the actual compute-power demand
of tasks, the CR isolation scheme often leads to an over-reservation and, thereby,
under-utilization of cores. In Figure 3.1c, cores c0 and c1 will be exclusively reserved
for the application in question at its launch time while core c2 can be used by other
applications. Here, c0 and c1 are utilized up to 60% and 80%, respectively.
   The CR isolation scheme still allows different cores of a tile to be allocated by dif-
ferent applications. Hence, while each core is exclusively assigned to one application,
the buses and the NA located on each tile are shared among the applications which
use the cores located on that tile. Therefore, CR establishes inter-application spatial
isolation on cores and temporal isolation on other resources.

Tile-Reservation (TR)

In systems designed based on the so-called Tile-Reservation (TR) isolation scheme,
each tile of the many-core platform is reserved for one running application exclusively,
see Figure 3.1d. TR is, therefore, the most restrictive isolation scheme in practice
which eliminates all inter-application timing interferences on intra-tile resources
altogether. This advantage, however, comes at the expense of a typically significant
over-reservation of resources. The TR isolation scheme establishes spatial isolation
between concurrent applications at the tile level.

Inter-Application Isolation in Practice

In the state-of-the-art practices of application mapping in composable many-core
systems, e.g., [SKS13; WGW+ 14; WWG+ 18a], a fixed choice of inter-application
isolation scheme (CS, CR, or TR) is exerted onto every mapping of every application
in the system3 . The inter-application isolation scheme of a system specifies the
resource-reservation policy followed by applications in that system and, therefore, has
a strong impact on its utilization of resources and the (worst-case) timing properties of
applications. This impact is demonstrated in Figure 3.2 for a mapping of an application
deployed on two adjacent tiles of an exemplary many-core platform that uses WRR for
the arbitration of shared resources. The mapping is visualized under the three isolation
schemes CS, CR, and TR in Figures 3.2b to 3.2d, respectively. For each isolation
scheme, the resource cost and the worst-case end-to-end latency (makespan) of the
mapping are reported below the respective sub-figure. Resource cost is calculated as

which, however, comes at the expense of a notably high end-to-end latency of 79.5 ms.
The TR isolation scheme in Figure 3.2d, on the other hand, exclusively employs spatial
isolation between concurrent applications, resulting in the lowest end-to-end latency

3.1.2 Isolation-Aware Application Mapping: Motivation
A single and fixed isolation scheme strongly restricts the design space of possible
mappings of an application on a given platform as it imposes a constant resource-
reservation policy on each and every mapping of each application in the system.
This practice often leads to mappings where the amount of reserved resources is not
properly adjusted to the actual performance and timing requirements of the application.
The resulting mismatch between the reserved and required computational power, in
turn, harms resource utilization if the reserved resources for an application exceed its
demand (over-reservation) which is often observed under the TR isolation scheme.
Less restrictive isolation schemes, namely, CS and CR, mitigate this utilization penalty
by allowing all or a subset of resources to be shared among multiple applications.
Nonetheless, this often results in a high degree of inter-application timing interference
which could lead to mappings with degraded or even unacceptable timing guarantees.
   These shortcomings can be addressed by applying a combination of different inter-
application isolation schemes on different resources used by a mapping. Figure 3.3
illustrates the advantage of applying isolation schemes in combination for the ex-
emplary mapping previously studied under fixed isolation schemes in Figure 3.2.
Applying isolation schemes in combination enables 12 additional isolation scenarios
for this mapping—in addition to the fixed scenarios depicted in Figure 3.2—three of
which are shown in Figure 3.3. In the first combined scheme shown in Figure 3.3a, CS

Contributions Overview

The practice of fixed inter-application isolation schemes in real-time many-core sys-
tems mainly roots in the lack of timing analyses that can capture all possible timing
components contributing to the execution latency of applications: Existing timing
analyses employed in a many-core context often cannot capture all timing contribu-
tions that may arise during the execution of applications, in the general case. To be
able to provide safe worst-case timing guarantees, these analyses necessarily rely on
fixed inter-application isolation schemes to ensure that those timing contributions
that they cannot analyze will also never occur4 . As a remedy, this chapter presents
two contributions which enable flexible combinations of the above introduced inter-
application isolation schemes (CS, CR, and TR): (i) a worst-case timing analysis for
(heterogeneous) many-core architectures which neither depends on a fixed choice
of inter-application isolation scheme nor is subject to the restrictive assumptions5
of existing timing analyses, (ii) an isolation-aware mapping optimization approach
which enables exploring combinations of isolation schemes on the resources used by
each mapping generated during the mapping optimization process.
   Together, the proposed mapping optimization approach and worst-case timing

    generally not practical due to the different (and possibly conflicting) assumptions of these analyses
    regarding the platform architecture and/or the arbitration scheme of resources.
 5 See Footnote 1 on Page 45 for some examples.

              specification (application, architecture, mapping edges)

                           Design Space Exploration (DSE)

      – binding and routing
      – isolation and allocation                     – worst-case timing analysis

                                        qualities


              Pareto-optimal mappings

Figure 3.5: An overview of the proposed isolation-aware mapping optimization ap-
            proach (left) and worst-case timing analysis (right) incorporated into the
            design-time DSE in HAM. The contributions are highlighted in blue.


analysis enable an adaptive resource-reservation policy in which the amount of re-
sources reserved per application is adjusted to its actual performance and timing
requirements to enhance the timing and resource utilization trade-off. In the resulting
isolation-aware DSE scheme, the mapping optimizer automatically finds mappings
with superior quality trade-offs (including verified worst-case timing guarantees) com-
pared to mappings derived in a fixed-isolation-scheme setting. An overview of the
proposed contributions incorporated into the DSE of HAM is illustrated in Figure 3.5.
These contributions will be presented in detail in Sections 3.2 and 3.3. Note that any
combination of CS, CR, and TR isolation schemes can be expressed in terms of the
number of core scheduling slots to be reserved on a set of cores. For instance, TR
corresponds to the reservation of all scheduling slots of all cores on the respective tile.
Therefore, the imposition of isolation schemes in combination can be realized using
the existing resource-reservation mechanisms in composable many-core systems.


3.2 Isolation-Aware Mapping Optimization
This section presents the proposed isolation-aware mapping optimization approach. In
this approach, for each mapping generated during the DSE in HAM, the choices of
isolation scheme for each allocated resource are explored. As detailed in Section 2.4.1,
the generation of a mapping involves four sets of design decisions, namely, bind-
ing, routing, allocation, and scheduling. For the exploration of the design space of
mappings, the mapping optimization problem is typically encoded using (i) a set of
decision variables whose assignment is decided by the optimizer and (ii) a set of con-
straints formulated based on the decision variables to restrict the solution space of the
optimizer to valid (feasible) mappings, see, e.g., [BTT98]. In the proposed mapping



54
                                           3.2 Isolation-Aware Mapping Optimization


optimization approach, a set of new design decisions are introduced and the encoding
of (some) design decisions is adapted to enable the optimizer to explore the choices of
isolation scheme for each resource allocated in each mapping, see Figure 3.5 (left).
   The set of resources used by a mapping are determined by the binding and routing
decisions. The former specify the assignment of tasks to cores and messages to mem-
ories, and the latter specify a NoC route for each inter-tile message, see Section 2.4.1.
While the encoding of the binding and routing decisions remains agnostic to the
exploration of isolation schemes, the allocation of resources and the scheduling of
tasks and messages strongly depend on the choices of isolation scheme made by the
optimizer and, therefore, must be adapted.
   For the optimization of mappings, this work uses the SAT-Decoding [LGH+ 07]
optimization approach which employs an evolutionary algorithm coupled with a
Pseudo-Boolean (PB) solver for an efficient DSE of discrete constrained optimization
problems. In SAT-Decoding, a set of PB constraints are used to specify the design
space of valid solutions, see also [GTL+ 17]. For the mapping optimization problem at
hand, the PB constraints from [LSF14] are used to explore the binding of tasks to cores.
Without loss of generality, the encoding of routing decisions is realized based on the
XY-routing algorithm [NM93]. To explore isolation schemes, we introduce additional
decision variables and constraints. In the following, first, Section 3.2.1 presents the
encoding of isolation schemes and the corresponding adaptation of the allocation.
Thereafter, the isolation-aware scheduling of mappings is presented in Section 3.2.2.

3.2.1 Isolation-Scheme Encoding and Resource Allocation
To explore the space of possible combinations of isolation schemes (CS, CR, and
TR) for the resources used by each mapping, we provide the mapping optimizer with
two binary design variables for each core c ∈ C with at least one task t ∈ T mapped
to it in the mapping under generation. By adjusting the assigned values of these
binary variables, the optimizer can realize various combinations of isolation schemes
within the mapping. The assigned values of these variables for each core denote the
isolation-scheme preferences of that core in the mapping in question which we refer
to as the preferred isolation scheme of that core. Once the preferred isolation scheme
of all cores has been determined, the so-called activated isolation scheme of each core
is derived by resolving the possible conflicts between the preferred isolation schemes
of the cores located on the same tile. This procedure is detailed in the following.
   Each core c ∈ C with at least one task mapped to it is associated with two binary
design variables νcTR and νcCR . The first variable νcTR ∈ {0, 1} determines whether
the tile on which core c is located is to be allocated exclusively. Hence, νcTR = 1
denotes the preference of the TR isolation scheme for core c. The second variable
νcCR ∈ {0, 1} determines whether c is to be allocated exclusively and comes into effect
if TR is not preferred for c, i.e., νcTR = 0. Thus, CR is preferred for core c if condition
¬νcTR ∧ νcCR = 1 holds. Finally, if neither TR nor CR is preferred for core c, i.e.,


                                                                                       55
3 Isolation-Aware Mapping Optimization and Timing Analysis


¬νcTR ∧ ¬νcCR = 1, the preferred isolation scheme of c is taken to be CS.
   After the preferred isolation scheme for each core is decided, the activated isolation
schemes in the mapping are decoded as follows: TR is activated on a tile u ∈ U iff
(i) u has at least one core c ∈ u with a TR preferred isolation scheme or (ii) all cores
on u have a CR preferred scheme. Once TR is activated on a tile u, it is also activated
on each and every core of that tile, regardless of their preferred isolation scheme. CR
is activated on a core c iff (i) CR is preferred for c, and (ii) c is not located on a tile
with an activated TR scheme. Finally, CS is activated on a core c iff (i) CS is preferred
for c and (ii) c is not located on a tile with an activated TR scheme.
   Once the activated isolation schemes have been derived, the resources to be allocated
are specified. To that end, all resources located on tiles with an activated TR isolation
scheme are allocated in addition to the other resources used by the mapping which are
outlined in the description of the allocation decisions in Section 2.4.1.

3.2.2 Isolation-Aware Scheduling
In the scheduling step, the so-called schedule of tasks and messages on the resources
used by them is specified. That is, for each task/message x ∈ T ∪ M that uses resource i,
an arbitration weight Wix ∈ [1, Ki ] is determined, denoting the number of periodic time
slots to be reserved for x on resource i. Here, Ki represents the arbitration capacity
(total number of periodic time slots) of i, see the definition of schedule in Section 2.4.1.
   In the proposed isolation-aware mapping optimization approach, the schedule
of tasks and messages on their respective resources is determined based on (i) the
activated isolation scheme of the resources in the mapping (decided by the optimizer as
detailed in Section 3.2.1) and (ii) the arbitration policy of each resource (provided in the
architecture graph). To that end, first, the arbitration weight Wix of each task/message
x ∈ T ∪ M on each respective resource i used by x is calculated. Subsequently, a set
of so-called arbitration tuples are constructed based on them which summarize all
necessary scheduling information for the worst-case timing analysis of the mapping.
In the following, first, the scheduling procedure for tasks and messages, i.e., the
calculation of their arbitration weights, is presented. Following that, the construction
of arbitration tuples based on calculated arbitration weights is detailed.

Scheduling of Tasks and Messages

The schedule of a task/message x ∈ T ∪ M on each resource i used by x is derived by
determining the arbitration weight Wix of x on i. To that end, we present an iterative
scheduling approach based on the proposed timing analysis. Details of the timing
analysis will be presented in Section 3.3. At this point, suppose that the timing analysis
can be used to safely bound the WCRT of each task and the WCTT of each inter-tile
message based on its arbitration tuples which summarize all necessary inputs for the
timing analysis. In general, the arbitration tuple of each requestor x of resource i



56
                                          3.2 Isolation-Aware Mapping Optimization


is constructed as (Si ,Wix , Pix ) where Si ∈ R+ denotes the length of each arbitration
time slot of i, Wix ∈ N+                                           x   +
                       ≤Ki the arbitration weight of x on i, and Pi ∈ R the worst-case
arbitration period perceived by x on i. Here, Ki ∈ N+ denotes the arbitration capacity
of resource i. The scheduling of tasks and messages is performed as follows.

Task Scheduling        For each task t ∈ T with execution period (implicit deadline)
PRDt (specified by the application) mapped to core c ∈ C, the arbitration weight Wct
of t on c is calculated using the following iterative task scheduling procedure: Starting
with the lowest arbitration weight Wct = 1:

   1. Arbitration tuple (Sc ,Wct , Pc ) is constructed and used as input to the timing
      analysis to calculate the WCRT of task t on core c. The WCRT analysis is
      performed using Eqs. (3.2) to (3.5) which will be presented later in Section 3.3.

   2. If the WCRT of t does not exceed its given deadline PRDt , the arbitration weight
      Wct will be used for t on c as the minimum arbitration weight which satisfies the
      timing constraint of t. Otherwise, Wct is incremented by one, and the procedure
      returns to step 1.

Message Scheduling       For each inter-tile message m ∈ M with production period (im-
