Schedulability Check       After the arbitration weights have been derived for all tasks
and messages on each resource they use, the schedulability of the mapping is evaluated
as follows. If the overall arbitration weight demanded by all tasks T̂ ⊆ T mapped to
a core c ∈ C exceeds the arbitration capacity of c, i.e., ∑t∈T̂ Wct > Kc , the mapping
is considered unschedulable and is discarded. Likewise, if the overall arbitration
weight demanded on a NoC router or the Transmitter (TX) or Receiver (RX) unit
of a NA exceeds its arbitration capacity, the mapping is considered unschedulable


                                                                                      57
3 Isolation-Aware Mapping Optimization and Timing Analysis


and is discarded. If no capacity violations are observed, the mapping is considered
schedulable, and the derived arbitration weights will be used to construct the arbitration
tuples as presented in the following.

Calculation of Arbitration Tuples

As mentioned before, for each requestor (user) x of each resource i, the arbitration tuple
of x on i is constructed as (Si ,Wix , Pix ) where Si denotes the length of each arbitration
time slot of i, Wix the arbitration weight for x on i, and Pix the worst-case arbitration
period perceived by x on i. The arbitration tuples of a mapping summarize all the
necessary scheduling information for the worst-case timing analysis of the mapping
and serve as input for the proposed timing analysis. While Si is provided as an attribute
of i in the architecture graph and Wix is calculated as detailed above regardless of the
choices of isolation schemes, Pix is the key parameter that captures the impact of the
choices of isolation schemes and provides isolation awareness to the proposed timing
analysis: As detailed previously in Section 2.2.4, the worst-case arbitration period
Pi of a resource i under a composable arbitration policy, e.g., TDMA or WRR, can
be calculated as Pi = Ki · (Si + Di ) where Ki denotes the arbitration capacity of i, Si
the arbitration slot length, and Di the arbiter delay. Here, Pi provides an upper bound
on the worst-case arbitration period Pix perceived by any requestor x of i. However,
subject to the choice of the isolation scheme applied on i, a tighter but still safe upper
bound on Pix can be derived as follows.
   Under a work-conserving arbitration scheme, e.g., WRR, the arbitration period
Pi = Ki · (Si + Di ) is observed only if all Ki time slots of resource i are assigned to
requestors and are used. An imposition of CR or TR isolation scheme on i can,
however, result in scenarios in which some slots of i are reserved by the mapping
under scheduling but not used by any of its tasks or messages. Such a scenario is
illustrated in Figure 3.6 in which resource i with an arbitration capacity of Ki = 5
is used by requestors x and y with arbitration weights of Wix = 2 and Wiy = 1 on i,
respectively. If the choice of isolation scheme activated on i allows the remaining
two time slots of i to be used by other applications, then, in the worst case, x and
y can perceive an arbitration period of Piy = Pix = Pi = 5 · (Si + Di ) when accessing i.
However, if the applied isolation scheme results in an exclusive reservation of i for the
mapping under scheduling, then it can be safely assumed that the two remaining time
slots of i which are not used by x or y will not be used at all and, hence, are always
skipped by the arbiter. Thus, the worst-case arbitration period perceived by x and y
when accessing i can be safely tightened as Piy = Pix = 3 · (Si + Di ) < Pi .
   The refinement of the worst-case arbitration period Pix perceived by requestor x on
resource i enables eliminating a considerable degree of pessimism from the worst-case
timing analysis of the mapping and, thus, deriving tighter but still safe worst-case
timing guarantees. For each requestor x of an exclusively allocated resource i with a
work-conserving arbitration policy, Pix can be calculated by disregarding the reserved

             Si               Di            Ki = 5            Wix = 2    Wiy = 1

           Pi = Ki · (Si + Di )

Figure 3.6: Arbitration of a resource i with arbitration capacity Ki = 5. Requestors x
              and y are assigned to i with arbitration weights of Wix = 2 and Wiy = 1 per

slots of i which are not assigned to any requestors in the mapping under scheduling.
For instance, for an exclusively allocated core c ∈ C with tasks T̂ ⊆ T mapped to it,
the worst-case arbitration period perceived by each task t ∈ T̂ can be calculated as
Pct = ∑t∈T̂ Wct · (Si + Di ). Similarly, for each bus b ∈ B in an exclusively allocated tile,
the idle bus masters will be excluded when calculating the worst-case bus arbitration
period Pbi perceived by each non-idle bus master i. Idle bus masters are idle cores (i.e.,
cores that do not host any tasks) and idle TX/RX unit of the NA (in case no messages
are injected from/to the tile to/from the NoC).
    Note that, for each resource i with the set X of requestors, it is guaranteed that
Pix ≤ Pi for each requestor x ∈ X since it has already been verified in the schedulability
test that ∑x∈X Wix ≤ Ki . Also note that the refinement of the worst-case arbitration
period as discussed above can be applied only in the case of work-conserving arbi-
tration policies, e.g., WRR. Otherwise, no period reductions may take place. After
the worst-case arbitration period perceived by each requestor of each resource in the
mapping is calculated, the set of arbitration tuples are constructed which serve as input
for the timing analysis of the mapping. To that end, the following arbitration tuples
are constructed per mapping which are also exemplified for a mapping in Figure 3.7.

Task ▶ Core For each task t ∈ T mapped to core c ∈ C, the arbitration tuple
(Sc ,Wct , Pct ) of t on c is constructed. Here, Sc is provided by the architecture, Wct
is calculated as part of t’s schedule, and Pct is calculated as detailed above.

Message ▶ TX/RX            For each inter-tile message m ∈ M routed through TX unit
tx on m’s source tile and RX unit rx on m’s destination tile, the arbitration tu-

vided by the architecture, Wtxm and Wrxm are calculated as part of m’s schedule where
Wtxm = Wrxm = W(r̂)m , and Pm and Pm are calculated as detailed above.

denoted as (Sb ,Wbtx , Pbtx ). Likewise, for the RX rx unit of each NA n ∈ N that routes
at least one incoming message into its tile, the arbitration tuple on each on-tile bus
b ∈ B is calculated and denoted as (Sb ,Wbrx , Pbrx ). Here, Wbtx , Wbrx , and Sb are provided
by the architecture, and Pbtx and Pbrx are calculated as detailed above.

Computational Complexity

The isolation-aware scheduling approach presented above involves (i) the scheduling
of tasks and messages, (ii) the schedulability check of the mapping, and (iii) the
calculation of the arbitration tuples. In the following, the computational complexity
of each step (i)–(iii) and the overall computational complexity of the isolation-aware
scheduling approach is analyzed.

Scheduling of Tasks and Messages            The scheduling of tasks and messages is
performed based on the WCRT and WCTT analyses which will be presented in Sec-
tion 3.3. As will be shown later, both analyses are constant-time non-iterative analyses
each with a computational complexity of O(1). For each task t ∈ T mapped to core
c ∈ C with arbitration capacity Kc , the scheduling procedure (i.e., the calculation
of the arbitration weight Wct of t on c) may involve a maximum of Kc iterations of
WCRT analysis, thus, resulting in a computational complexity of O(Kc ). Therefore,
the scheduling of all tasks introduces a computational complexity of O(Kcore     max |T |)
         max = max
where Kcore         c∈C Kc denotes the maximum arbitration capacity among all cores
c ∈ C in the architecture, and |T | denotes the total number of tasks in the application.
Similarly, the scheduling procedure of each inter-tile message m ∈ M may involve a
maximum of KNoC max iterations of WCTT analysis where K max = max
                                                              NoC       i∈N∪R Ki denotes
the maximum arbitration capacity among (TX/RX units of) all NAs n ∈ N and all
routers r ∈ R in the architecture. Thus, the scheduling of all inter-tile messages has
a computational complexity of O(KNoC   max |M|) where |M| denotes the total number of

messages in the application. Therefore, the overall computational complexity of the
scheduling of tasks and messages will be O(Kcore max |T | + K max |M|).
                                                             NoC


Schedulability Check      The schedulability check for each mapping (after the schedul-
ing of tasks and messages) involves an enumeration of the tasks mapped to each
allocated core c ∈ C as well as an enumeration of the inter-tile messages routed over
each allocated NA n ∈ N and router r ∈ R. In the general case, a nested loop over the
allocated cores c ∈ C (outer loop) and the set of tasks t ∈ T mapped to each core (inner
loop) introduces a computational complexity of O(|C||T |). However, since each task is
necessarily mapped to exactly one core, the total number of iterations for the inner loop
will never exceed |T |, which reduces the computational complexity of the nested loop
to O(|C| + |T |). The computational complexity of the enumeration of the inter-tile
messages m ∈ M routed over each allocated NA n ∈ N and router r ∈ R can be derived
as O(|N||M|) and O(|R||M|), respectively. Therefore, the schedulability check intro-
duces an overall computational complexity of O((|C| + |T |) + (|N||M|) + (|R||M|))
which can be simplified to O(|C| + |T | + |R||M|) given that |N| = |R|.

Calculation of Arbitration Tuples   The calculation of the arbitration tuples involves
the three following enumerations: (i) an enumeration of the tasks mapped to each


                                                                                      61
3 Isolation-Aware Mapping Optimization and Timing Analysis


allocated core c ∈ C which has a computational complexity of O(|C| + |T |) as shown
above, (ii) an enumeration of the inter-tile messages routed over each allocated
NA n ∈ N and router r ∈ R which has a computational complexity of O(|R||M|)
as shown above given that |N| = |R|, and (iii) an enumeration of the bus masters (i.e.,
the on-tile cores and the single NA) connected to each allocated bus b ∈ B which has a
computational complexity of O(|B||C|). Thus, the calculation of the arbitration tuples
introduces an overall computational complexity of O(|C| + |T | + |R||M| + |B||C|)
which can be simplified to O(|T | + |R||M| + |B||C|).

Overall Computational Complexity          The overall computational complexity of the
proposed isolation-aware scheduling approach can be derived by combining the
computational complexity of its individual steps analyzed above. Overall, the pro-
posed isolation-aware scheduling approach introduces a computational complexity of
     max |T | + K max |M| + |R||M| + |B||C|) which grows linearly with the number |T |
O(Kcore          NoC
of tasks and the number |M| of messages in the application.


3.3 Worst-Case Timing Analysis7
This section presents the proposed worst-case timing analysis for (heterogeneous)
many-core systems. Given the arbitration tuples of a mapping, the proposed timing
analysis formally derives an upper bound on the Worst-Case Response Time (WCRT)
of each task t ∈ T and on the Worst-Case Traversal Time (WCTT) of each inter-tile
message m ∈ M. The obtained WCRT and WCTT bounds are then used to bound
the worst-case end-to-end latency (makespan) and throughput of the mapping. The
proposed timing analysis is performed compositionally, i.e., the worst-case timing
behavior of each task or message is decomposed into several timing contributions on
different resources which are analyzed separately and, then, are combined to obtain
globally safe timing guarantees. For further details on compositional timing analyses,
see Section 2.2.2.

3.3.1 Worst-Case Response Time Analysis
The Worst-Case Response Time (WCRT) of a task t denotes the worst-case time
interval between t’s arrival time and completion time on the core to which it is mapped.
The WCRT of t accounts for the context-independent worst-case latency of t and the
worst-case timing interferences that may be imposed on t in its execution context,
i.e., by concurrent processes in the system when t accesses shared resources. In
each iteration of its execution, task t reads its required data and input messages from
the on-tile memory associated with it, performs its processing, and writes its output

 7 Major parts of this section have been published in [PSW+ 19].




62
                                                           3.3 Worst-Case Timing Analysis


messages into its associated memory. In general, t may be exposed to two sources of
timing interference in each iteration of its execution: (i) interference on the bus when
accessing memory and (ii) preemption delays on the core. Therefore, the WCRT of a
task t ∈ T can be formally bounded as:

    WCRT(t, c, q, b) = WCET t,c + MDt · ST q + I bus (t, c, q, b) + I core (t, c, q, b)      (3.2)

where WCET t,c denotes the worst-case execution time of t on core c in isolation,
assuming zero delays for bus and memory accesses and an uninterrupted execution
of t on c. MDt denotes the maximum memory demand (total number of single-word
memory accesses) of t. ST q denotes the service time of memory q, i.e., the turnaround
delay of a single-word memory access in the absence of interferences on the bus. Thus,
(MDt · ST q ) provides the overall latency of t’s memory accesses when performed in
isolation. I bus (t, c, q, b) denotes the worst-case delay imposed on t’s execution due
to interferences on bus b over which memory q is accessed. Finally, I core (t, c, q, b)
denotes the worst-case preemption delay imposed on t’s execution on core c. Here,
WCET t,c is provided as an attribute of the mapping edge (t, c) in the specification,
MDt is an attribute of t in the application graph, and ST q is an attribute of q in the
architecture graph, see Section 2.3. Interference delays I bus (t, c, q, b) and I core (t, c, q, b)
can be formally bounded as follows.

Worst-Case Bus Interference

Suppose that task t mapped to core c accesses memory q over bus b. The worst-case
bus interference I bus (t, c, q, b) imposed on t’s execution for its memory accesses can
be formally bounded based on the memory demand MDt of t, the service time ST q
of memory q, and the arbitration tuple (Sb ,Wbc , Pbc ) of core c on bus b. To bound
I bus (t, c, q, b), first, we derive the maximum number of bus arbitration slots that t may
require for performing MDt memory operations.
    The maximum number of bus arbitration slots required by t is denoted as Φ(t, c, q, b)
and is subject to two upper bounds: On the one hand, Φ(t, c, q, b) cannot exceed MDt ,
since each bus slot is necessarily long enough to allow performing at least one single-
word memory access. Thus, it is guaranteed that Φ(t, c, q, b) ≤ MDt . On the other
hand, Φ(t, c, q, b) cannot exceed the maximum number of bus slots that may pass
during t’s execution in isolation, i.e., ⌈(WCET t,c + MDt · ST q )/Sb ⌉. Thus, Φ(t, c, q, b)
can be bounded using Eq. (3.3) where the two arguments of the min{} operation
correspond to the two upper bounds outlined above.
                                                                      
                                                  WCET t,c + MDt · ST q
                      Φ(t, c, q, b) = min MDt ,                                        (3.3)
                                                           Sb

   In the worst case, the memory accesses of t are distributed across its execution
time in such a way that the Φ(t, c, q, b) required bus slots for t fall into separate bus


                                                                                               63
3 Isolation-Aware Mapping Optimization and Timing Analysis


arbitration periods. As a result, t may experience the maximum wait time for attaining
each of its required bus slots. In each bus arbitration period perceived by t on b, a total
of Wbc bus slots are dedicated to t executed on core c. Given the length Sb of each bus
slot of b and the worst-case bus period Pbc perceived by t on b, a worst-case wait time
of (Pbc −Wbc · Sb ) may be imposed on t’s execution per bus arbitration period before
it acquires the bus ownership for each of its required bus slots. Thus, the overall bus
interference imposed on t’s execution can be bounded as:
                      I bus (t, c, q, b) = Φ(t, c, q, b) · (Pbc −Wbc · Sb )           (3.4)
where the first factor Φ(t, c, q, b) denotes the maximum number of bus slots required
by t, and the second factor (Pbc −Wbc · Sb ) denotes the maximum wait time imposed on
t for attaining a dedicated bus slot per bus arbitration period Pbc .
   In some cases, a memory access is initiated so late that it cannot be completed
before the end of the current bus arbitration slot. To compensate for this misalignment,
the arbitration delay Db of each bus b ∈ B is provisioned with a guard time equal to
the service time ST q of its associated memory q. This practice (i) enables memory
accesses that are initiated too late to be completed before the next bus slot is assigned
to its respective requestor and, thereby, (ii) eliminates memory interferences due to
overlapping memory accesses from different bus masters in consecutive bus slots. Note
that if task t accesses multiple memories, the bus-interference analysis above must be
applied for each respective memory bus bi over which t performs MDti single-word
accesses to memory qi with a service time of ST qi .

Worst-Case Core Preemption Latency

Supposing that task t mapped to core c accesses memory q over bus b, the worst-
case delay imposed on t’s execution due to the preemption of its execution on c
can be formally bounded based on the arbitration tuple (Sc ,Wct , Pct ) of t on c and the
arbitration tuple (Sb ,Wbc , Pbc ) of c on the bus b over which memory q is accessed. In
each iteration of its execution, task t requires an overall dedicated processor time of
(WCET t,c + MDt · ST q + I bus (t, c, q, b)) on core c in order to complete its processing
and perform its memory accesses to q over the shared bus b. Given that t has a total of
Wct dedicated core scheduling slots per scheduling period of c, it receives an overall
dedicated processor time of (Wct · Sc ) per perceived core scheduling period Pct . Hence,
a worst-case periodic wait time of (Pct −Wct · Sc ) can be imposed on t’s execution due
to preemption. Therefore, the overall preemption delay imposed on t’s execution can
be formally bounded as:
                        WCET t,c + MDt · ST q + I bus (t, c, q, b)
                                                                  
     core
                                                                     · Pct −Wct · Sc
                                                                                     
    I (t, c, q, b) =                         t
                                                                                       (3.5)
                                           Wc · Sc
where the first factor bounds the number of core scheduling periods over which the
execution of t may span, and the second factor gives the worst-case wait time imposed


64
                                                        3.3 Worst-Case Timing Analysis


on t’s execution per core scheduling period. Note that also here, a memory access
may be initiated too late to be completed before the end of the current core scheduling
slot. To compensate for this misalignment, the context-switch latency Dc of each core
c ∈ C is provisioned with a guard time equal to the maximum service time among all
memories accessible to c. As a result, any memory access started in the current core
scheduling slot is guaranteed to be completed before the next context switch of c.

3.3.2 Worst-Case Traversal Time Analysis
The Worst-Case Traversal Time (WCTT) of an inter-tile message m denotes the worst-
case latency of the transfer process of m from the memory on the source tile where m
is produced to the target memory on the destination tile where m will be consumed. As
elaborated in Section 2.1.4, the transfer process of an inter-tile message m is realized in
three steps: Once m is written by its producer task in the source memory q, (i) the TX
unit tx of the local NA reads m from memory q via q’s associated bus b, decomposes
m into flow-control digits (flits), and injects the flits into the NoC. On the NoC, (ii) the
flits of m are routed over the sequence of routers ρm ⊆ R on m’s NoC route towards
the destination tile. Once the flits arrive at the destination tile, (iii) the RX unit rx
of the local NA reconstructs m from its flits and writes it into m’s dedicated space in
the target memory q̂ over q̂’s associated bus b̂. In line with the three steps above, the
WCTT of an inter-tile message m can be formally bounded as:

  WCTT(m, tx, q, b, ρm , rx, q̂, b̂) = DTX (m, tx, q, b) + DNoC (m, ρm ) + DRX (m, rx, q̂, b̂)
                                                                                         (3.6)
         TX                 NoC                    RX
where D (m, tx, q, b), D (m, ρm ), and D (m, rx, q̂, b̂) denote the worst-case la-
tency of the transfer steps (i)–(iii) above, respectively. These latency contributions can
be bounded formally using the following analysis.

Worst-Case Network Adapter Latency

Suppose that TX unit tx shall read inter-tile message m from memory q over bus b for
injection into the NoC. This process is subject to two sources of interference: (i) bus
interference since reading m from memory is performed over a shared bus and (ii) TX
interference since tx may be shared among multiple outbound messages transmitted
concurrently from the tile. In the following, we bound the worst-case latency of this
process, denoted by DTX (m, tx, q, b), using the arbitration tuple (Sb ,Wbtx , Pbtx ) of tx on
b and the arbitration tuple (Stx ,Wtxm , Ptxm ) of m on tx. The maximum payload size PLDm
of m is also known which is provided by the application graph.
   To bound DTX (m, tx, q, b), first, we formally bound the maximum number of bus
arbitration slots that can be required for reading m from memory q, denoted as
Ω (m, q, b): Generally, in each bus arbitration slot, a total of ⌈Sb /ST q ⌉ consecutive
accesses to memory q can be performed via its associated bus b. Therefore, given



                                                                                           65
3 Isolation-Aware Mapping Optimization and Timing Analysis


the maximum payload size PLDm of m in number of single-word memory accesses,
Ω (m, q, b) can be bounded as:
                                                 
                                                Sb
                         Ω (m, q, b) = PLDm                            (3.7)
                                               ST q
  Given Ω (m, q, b) and provided that tx has a reserved arbitration weight of Wbtx
on bus b, the overall bus delay imposed on tx for reading m from memory q can
be bounded using Eq. (3.8). There, ⌈Ω (m, q, b)/Wbtx ⌉ bounds the number of bus
arbitration periods that may pass before tx attains all Ω (m, q, b) dedicated bus slots
required for reading m, and (Pbtx −Wbtx · Sb ) bounds the worst-case wait time imposed
on tx per bus arbitration period.
                                                  
                    bus                Ω (m, q, b)
                                                     · Pbtx −Wbtx · Sb
                                                                       
                   I (m, tx, q, b) =           tx                                 (3.8)
                                           Wb

    Finally, DTX (m, tx, q, b) can be formally bounded using Eq. (3.9). There, the first
summand (PLDm · ST q ) denotes the latency of reading m from memory q in isola-
tion, the second summand I bus (m, tx, q, b) derived by Eq. (3.8) denotes the worst-
case bus interference imposed on tx when reading message m from memory q via
shared bus b, and the third summand bounds the worst-case TX interference imposed
when reading m from the memory. Here, ⌈(PLDm · ST q + I bus (m, tx, q, b))/(Wtxm · Stx )⌉
bounds the maximum number of TX arbitration periods involved for reading m and
(Ptxm −Wtxm · Stx ) bounds the worst-case wait time imposed per TX arbitration period
Ptxm on the process of reading m. The RX latency, i.e., DRX (m, rx, q̂, b̂) in Eq. (3.6) can
be bounded similarly to the analysis of the TX latency in Eqs. (3.7) to (3.9).
     DTX (m, tx, q, b) = PLDm · ST q + I bus (m, tx, q, b)
                             PLDm · ST q + I bus (m, tx, q, b)                            (3.9)
                                                              
                         +                 m                     · (Ptxm −Wtxm · Stx )
                                        Wtx · Stx

Worst-Case Network-on-Chip Latency

Suppose that message m is transferred over the sequence of NoC routers ρm ⊆ R
connecting its source and destination tiles. The worst-case latency of this transfer
process, denoted by DNoC (m, ρm ), can be formally bounded based on the arbitration
tuple (Sρm ,Wρmm , Pρmm ) of m on the routers in ρm . Note that, in a wormhole-switched
NoC (as considered in this work), the length Sρm of one arbitration slot of a router is
equal to the NoC clock cycle denoted by τ NoC , hence, Sρm = τ NoC . The worst-case
latency of transferring m over router sequence ρm can be formally bounded as:
             DNoC (m, ρm ) = (Fm − 1 + |ρm | · Drouter ) · Sρm
                                F                                                  (3.10)
                                    m                          m    m
                             +           − 1  + |ρ m |    ·  P   −W   · S ρm
                                  Wρmm                        ρm   ρm




66
                                                    3.3 Worst-Case Timing Analysis


which is adopted from [WGW+ 14]. Here, Fm denotes the number of flits associated
with m which is calculated based on m’s payload size PLDm and the NoC bit width.
Moreover, |ρm | denotes the length of the router sequence ρm in number of hops, τ NoC
denotes the duration of one NoC clock cycle, and Drouter denotes the latency of a
router in number of clock cycles. The first summand in Eq. (3.10) derives the latency
of transferring Fm flits over the router sequence ρm in isolation, i.e., in the absence
of any interferences on ρm . The second summand in Eq. (3.10) formally bounds the
worst-case interference imposed on the transfer of m over ρm in the presence of other
communication flows on the NoC. Here, the first factor (⌈Fm /Wρmm ⌉ − 1 + |ρm |) bounds
the maximum number of router arbitration periods in which the flits of m may be stalled
due to the transfer of concurrent flows. The second factor (Pρmm −Wρmm · τ NoC ) bounds
the worst-case wait time imposed per router arbitration period Pρmm , cf. [WGW+ 14].

3.3.3 Application Timing Guarantees
Let WCRT(t) denote the WCRT of task t ∈ T and WCTT(m) the WCTT of inter-tile
message m ∈ M analyzed as presented in Sections 3.3.1 and 3.3.2, respectively. Based
on the derived bounds on the WCRT of tasks and WCTT of messages, the worst-
case throughput TH app of the mapping can be formally bounded using Eq. (3.11).
Likewise, the worst-case end-to-end application latency (makespan) Lapp can be
bounded using Eq. (3.12) where Π denotes the set of all end-to-end paths in the
application graph.
                           n                          o−1
             TH app = max max WCRT(t) , max WCTT(m)                             (3.11)
                          t∈T             m∈M
                        n                                   o
             Lapp = max ∑t∈(T ∩π) WCRT(t) + ∑m∈(M∩π) WCTT(m)                    (3.12)
                    π∈Π


Computational Complexity

The WCRT analysis of each task t ∈ T realized using Eqs. (3.2) to (3.5) and the
WCTT analysis of each inter-tile message m ∈ M realized using Eqs. (3.6) to (3.10)
are constant-time non-iterative analyses with a computational complexity of O(1).
Hence, the throughput analysis of an application using Eq. (3.11) introduces a com-
putation complexity of O(|T | + |M|) where |T | and |M| denote the number of tasks
and messages in the application graph, respectively. Having the application graph
provided as an adjacency list, the calculation of the worst-case end-to-end application
latency in Eq. (3.12) can be realized using a Depth-First Search (DFS) algorithm with
a computational complexity of O(|T | + |M|).

3 Isolation-Aware Mapping Optimization and Timing Analysis


3.4 Experimental Results8
This section presents the results of a series of experiments conducted to evaluate the
impact of the proposed isolation-aware mapping optimization and timing analysis
approach on the quality of the obtained mappings. To that end, the effectiveness of this
approach is evaluated in comparison with existing mapping optimization approaches
based on a fixed isolation scheme.

3.4.1 Experiments Setup
Architectures     The experiments are performed based on three heterogeneous many-
core architectures with 4 × 4, 5 × 5, and 6 × 6 tiles, respectively. Each architecture
is composed of three core types (IBM PowerPC, AMD K6-2, and AMD K6-III)
provided by the Embedded System Synthesis Benchmarks Suite (E3S) [Dic10]. Each
tile of each architecture is composed of four homogeneous cores such that the three
architectures encompass 64, 100, and 144 cores, respectively, with diverse numbers
of instances from the core types above. In addition to four cores, each tile also
contains one shared memory, one NA with separate TX and RX units, and a shared bus
providing memory connectivity for cores, the TX, and the RX. Every shared resource
has a WRR arbitration policy configured as follows: For each core c, an arbitration
capacity of Kc = 10 slots, a scheduling slot length of Sc = 50 us, and a context-switch
overhead of Dc = 10 us is considered. On each bus b, each master i (TX, RX, and
the four cores) has an arbitration weight of Wbi = 1 slot, resulting in a bus arbitration
capacity of Kb = 6 slots. The length of each bus slot Sb is set equal to the memory
service time which is 7 clock cycles. For each NoC router r, an arbitration capacity of
Kr = 10 slots and an arbitration slot length of Sr = τ NoC = 10 ns are considered. For
each TX tx and RX rx, an arbitration capacity of Ktx = Krx = 10 slots and a slot length
equal to the bus arbitration period is considered. The NoC employs the XY-routing
algorithm [NM93].

Applications     For the experiments, four real-time applications are used from the
embedded domains of networking (7 tasks, 9 messages), consumer (11 tasks, 12
messages), telecommunications (14 tasks, 20 messages), and automotive (18 tasks,
21 messages). The applications are provided as benchmarks by E3S [Dic10]. For
each application, E3S [Dic10] also provides the Worst-Case Execution Time (WCET)
of each task on each core type, the memory demand of each task, the payload size
of each message, and the execution/transfer period of each task/message which are
required for the timing analysis of mappings.



 8 Major parts of this section have been published in [PSW+ 19].




68
                                                            3.4 Experimental Results


Design Objectives       The optimization of mappings for each application on each
architecture is performed w.r.t. three design objectives that are commonly considered
in the DSE of real-time applications in composable many-core systems: (i) worst-
case application latency (makespan) derived using the proposed timing analysis,
(ii) resource cost calculated as the sum of the core scheduling slots reserved on each
allocated core divided by its arbitration capacity which is set to Kc = 10 for all cores
c ∈ C, and (iii) energy consumption, calculated based on the WCRT of tasks, the core
power-consumption parameters provided by [Dic10] for each task of each investigated
benchmark application, the payload size of each message provided by [Dic10], and
the NoC/bus energy model from [WSK+ 05] considering a link length of 2 mm for
NoC links and 5 mm for buses.

Design Space Exploration (DSE)        For the optimization of mappings, the OpenDSE
framework [RLG+ 18] and the NSGA-II [DPA+ 02] multi-objective evolutionary algo-
rithm provided by the optimization framework O PT 4J [LGR+ 11] are used. During
DSE, the mapping optimizer follows the course of several optimization iterations to
generate new mappings and evaluate them w.r.t. the given design objectives. It collects
a population of mappings which is updated per iteration and is used for the generation
of mappings in the next iteration. The optimizer also retains the set of Pareto-optimal
mappings found so far. The DSE is configured to perform 4,000 optimization iterations
based on a population of 100 mappings where 25 mappings are generated per iteration.
The experimental results reported in this section represent an average over 20 runs of
the DSE for each application on each architecture.

Investigated Approaches       In the experiments, the proposed isolation-aware map-
ping optimization approach is compared with mapping optimization approaches
based on one of the three fixed isolation schemes, namely, Core-Sharing (CS), Core-
Reservation (CR), and Tile-Reservation (TR), introduced in Section 3.1.1. For each
optimization approach and each given pair of application and architecture, the DSE
delivers a set of Pareto-optimal mappings w.r.t. the design objectives introduced above.
The investigated approaches are compared with each other in terms of the quality of
mappings obtained using each.

Quality Metric     For the quality comparison of the mappings obtained by the in-
vestigated approaches, the well-established ε-dominance metric [LTD+ 02] from the
domain of multi-objective optimization is used. ε-dominance is a scalar value re-
flecting the quality of solutions in the space of multiple objectives and is defined as
follows: Let Γ represent a set of Pareto-optimal mappings obtained by an optimization
approach in the space of a set of design objectives O to be minimized. Also, let Γ ref
represent a reference set of mappings containing the true Pareto-optimal mappings for
the optimization problem at hand. To assess the quality of mappings in Γ w.r.t. Γ ref ,



                                                                                    69
3 Isolation-Aware Mapping Optimization and Timing Analysis


ε-dominance provides a scalar indicator εΓ ∈ [0, 1) calculated as:
         n                                                                   o
 εΓ = min ε ∈ [0, 1) | ∀µ ′ ∈ Γ ref , ∃µ ∈ Γ ∋ ∀o ∈ O : (1 − ε) · µ o ≤ µ ′o    (3.13)

where µ o and µ ′o denote the quality of mappings µ ∈ Γ and µ ′ ∈ Γ ref , respectively,
w.r.t. design objective o ∈ O. A smaller value for εΓ indicates a smaller relative
distance between the mappings in Γ and those in the reference set Γ ref in the space of
design objectives which, in turn, denotes a higher quality of mappings in µ. Due to
the exponential complexity of the many-core mapping problem, finding the set Γ ref
of true Pareto-optimal mappings is computationally infeasible. Therefore, similarly
to the common practice in the multi-objective optimization domain, the reference
set Γ ref in our experiments is constructed as the union set of the Pareto-optimal
mappings obtained by the four optimization approaches under investigation, denoting
the collection of the best reachable mappings.

3.4.2 Results Discussion
Figures 3.8 and 3.9 illustrate the ε-dominance indicator of the proposed isolation-
aware approach and the three fixed-isolation-scheme approaches, namely, CS, CR and
TR, across their exploration (optimization) run time. The results in Figure 3.8 corre-
spond to the smaller applications, namely, networking and consumer, (plot columns)
on the three architectures (plot rows) while the results in Figure 3.9 correspond to
the bigger applications, namely, telecom and automotive, (plot columns) on the three
architectures (plot rows).
   In general, the quality of mappings obtained by each approach improves as the
exploration progresses, resulting in a decrease in the ε-dominance indicator of each
approach throughout the course of its optimization iterations. Also, the overall ex-
ploration time of each approach grows with the application size (compare the plots
in the same row in Figures 3.8 and 3.9) and architecture size (compare the plots in
the same column in Figures 3.8 and 3.9). The results consistently demonstrate that,
regardless of the choice of application and architecture, (i) the proposed isolation-
aware approach always outperforms the fixed-isolation-scheme approaches. This is
indicated by its lower ε-dominance indicator at the end of its exploration in all plots
in Figures 3.8 and 3.9. Moreover, (ii) the proposed isolation-aware approach exhibits
an exploration run time within the same range as the other approaches and, hence, does
not impact the scalability of the mapping optimization process adversely. Among the
fixed-isolation-scheme approaches, CR generally performs better than CS and TR for
most cases, while CS exhibits the worst quality of mappings for all applications and
architectures. In summary, over all 12 combinations of applications and architectures,
the proposed isolation-aware approach achieves an ε-dominance improvement of up
to 67% (compared to CS) with an average improvement of 26% compared to the three
fixed-isolation-scheme approaches.

on the other hand, allocates tiles exclusively (resulting in a relatively high resource
cost) which eliminates all on-tile inter-application interferences (resulting in relatively
low latency and energy consumption). When considering all design objectives col-
lectively for calculating their ε-dominance indicators according to Eq. (3.13), both
CS and TR need a large scaling factor ε to compensate for their respective inferior
objective values, explaining their poor (high) ε-dominance indicators in Figures 3.8
and 3.9. A compromise between CS and TR is achieved by CR which limits the
exclusive reservation of resources to cores only. This establishes a moderate trade-off
between resource cost, worst-case latency, and energy consumption which results in a
better (lower) ε-dominance indicator.



                                                                                                                                                                         73
3 Isolation-Aware Mapping Optimization and Timing Analysis


   The fixed isolation schemes of the approaches above (CS, CR, and TR) significantly
restrict the mapping design space available to the optimizer to only a small part of the
actual design space. This, in turn, restricts their coverage of the solution and objective
space to considerably smaller sub-spaces. Contrarily, the proposed isolation-aware
approach provides the mapping optimizer with a design space that not only covers
the design space of each of the three fixed-isolation-scheme approaches but also
extends beyond that by enabling the optimizer to also explore the choices of isolation
schemes in combination within each mapping. This enables the wide spread of the
solutions found under the isolation-aware approach in Figure 3.10. As a result, using
the proposed approach, the optimizer is empowered to find mappings of high quality
(w.r.t. the design objectives) which are inaccessible to the other approaches. Thereby,
it outperforms the other approaches as confirmed by its lower ε-dominance indicator
in Figures 3.8 and 3.9 for each application on each architecture.


3.5 Related Work
The worst-case timing analysis of applications in a many-core system is typically
performed in two steps as detailed in Section 2.2.2. First, context-independent timing
analysis is performed in which the WCET of each task is bounded for its execution
in isolation, i.e., in the absence of any timing interferences. Authors of [WEE+ 08]
provide an extensive overview of the tools, methods, and techniques used for context-
independent WCET analysis of tasks. After WCET analysis, schedulability analysis is
performed in the execution context of the application to bound the additional delays
that may be imposed on shared resources due to external interferences.
   The majority of timing analyses for multi/many-core systems mainly focus on
worst-case interference (schedulability) analysis based on the context-independent
timing properties of tasks and messages. For instance, the timing analyses presented
in [ADI+ 15; DNA16; DAI+ 17; GLS+ 12; GSH+ 16; RMM+ 16; RDP17; SS16] derive
upper bounds on the WCRT of tasks in a multi-core context in which several cores are
connected to a set of memories over a set of shared buses, similarly to the organization
of resources within a tile of a many-core platform. Authors of [GLS+ 12; GSH+ 16]
analyze WCRT based on a non-preemptive Fixed-Priority (FP) core scheduling policy
and a Round-Robin (RR) memory bus arbitration. Authors of [ADI+ 15; DAI+ 17]
present a framework for multi-core WCRT analysis based on a preemptive FP core
scheduling policy in conjunction with multiple bus arbitration policies, e.g., FP, RR,
and TDMA. In [RMM+ 16; SS16], a WCRT analysis is presented based on a non-
preemptive FP core scheduling policy and a multi-level bus arbitration scheme which
combines FP and RR policies. In [RDP17], bus interference is analyzed by means
of an Integer Linear Programming (ILP)-based timing analysis for a RR arbitration
policy. Authors in [DNA16] present an analysis of bus interference under various
arbitration policies, e.g., FP and TDMA. Regarding the interference on the NoC,



74
                                                                     3.5 Related Work


[ZSO+ 13] and [ZB08] analyze the NoC transfer delays under RR and FP arbitration
policies, respectively.
   The analyses introduced above predominantly consider contention-oriented and/or
non-preemptive arbitration/scheduling policies, e.g., priority-based policies. For such
policies, timing guarantees can be derived only if the complete set of applications
that access the shared resources is known to the analysis. The advantage of a com-
posable system, however, is that mapping optimization and timing evaluation of each
application can be performed independently from other applications. Thus, the mix
of concurrently executed applications that share resources with an application is not
required to be known at the time of its timing analysis, see Section 2.2.2. Hence,
contention-oriented policies can be used in a composable system only as long as the
respective resource is exclusively used by one application. That means, contention-
oriented policies can be used in a composable system only if each mapping of each
application (i) employs a TR isolation scheme only, (ii) is confined within one tile
only, and (iii) does not depend on any inter-tile communications, e.g., I/O transfers. In
practice, however, due to the small number of cores in each tile [Til12; HDH+ 10], the
high performance demand of modern applications necessitates the incorporation of
several tiles in the mapping of some applications to meet their real-time requirements.
For such applications, the involved parts of the NoC could be (in theory) exclusively
allocated so that an independent timing analysis of multi-tile mappings becomes pos-
sible, e.g., using the NoC analyses in [ZSO+ 13; ZB08]. In practice, however, sharing
the NoC among applications can hardly be avoided [MTT18].
   Contention-free arbitration policies that operate preemptively based on time slicing,
e.g., TDMA and WRR, enable inter-application resource sharing without violating
system composability. For instance, provided a NoC [HKK+ 13] with WRR arbitration
policy, authors of [WGW+ 14; WWG+ 18a] analyze NoC delays based on the reserved
budget for each message, independently of the other communication flows that may
share the same NoC links. For WCRT analysis, a preemptive, priority-based, RR
core scheduling policy is considered in [WGW+ 14] which, however, restricts the
applicability of its analysis to mappings in which cores are allocated exclusively.
Authors of [WWG+ 18a], consider a similar scheduling policy but restrict the maximum
number of tasks that can be mapped on one core. Thereby, their timing analysis can
also be adopted (with slight modifications) to bound the WCRT of tasks under a WRR
scheduling policy to cover preemption delays that may be imposed by other (currently
unknown) applications. Both analyses, [WGW+ 14] and [WWG+ 18a], however, do
not consider the NA and bus interference delays that arise in multi-core tiles, e.g.,
in [dDAB+ 13; HDH+ 10]. The timing analysis presented in this work is developed in
compliance with composable arbitration policies, e.g., TDMA and WRR, and captures
timing interferences that may arise on cores, buses, NAs, and the NoC in a many-core
system. This renders it applicable to many-core systems with multi-core tiles and
mappings that may span across multiple tiles without relying on restrictive assumption
about the platform architecture or the deployment of applications on it.


                                                                                     75
3 Isolation-Aware Mapping Optimization and Timing Analysis


   Existing timing analyses used in the context of many-core systems are tailored to a
fixed choice of inter-application isolation scheme and, hence, cannot provide worst-
case timing guarantees if multiple isolation schemes are applied in combination. For
instance, the analyses in [ADI+ 15; DNA16; DAI+ 17; GLS+ 12; GSH+ 16; RMM+ 16;
RDP17; SS16] are applicable only under the TR isolation scheme. The analyses
presented in [WGW+ 14; WWG+ 18a] are applicable to many-core systems with single-
core tiles under a TR/CR isolation scheme only. However, the analysis in [WWG+ 18a]
can also be adapted to be used for systems with single-core tiles under a CS isolation
scheme. Contrarily to these analyses, the timing analysis proposed in this work does
not depend on a fixed inter-application isolation scheme and, hence, can provide
worst-case timing guarantees for mappings which employ a combination of different
isolation schemes on their allocated resources.
   To the best of our knowledge, existing DSE and mapping optimization approaches
used in the context of HAM, e.g., [KSK+ 13; MAV+ 10; PP12; SS17; SKS13; WGW+ 14;
WWG+ 18a; YAM+ 11], consider (or, implicitly assume) a fixed inter-application isola-
tion scheme that is applied exclusively to every explored mapping for every application
in the system. For instance, the resource-reservation scheme in the DSE approaches
in [PP12; MAV+ 10; YAM+ 11] manifest the CR isolation scheme, [KSK+ 13; SKS13;
WGW+ 14; WWG+ 18a] follow the TR isolation scheme, while [WWG+ 18a] covers CS
as well. Contrarily to the approaches above, the isolation-aware mapping optimization
and timing analysis proposed in this work enable arbitrary combinations of isolation
schemes to be employed within one mapping by controlling the choice of the isolation
scheme applied per allocated resource for each generated mapping. This enables the
optimizer to automatically find high-quality mappings in which the amount of reserved
resources is fine-tuned according to the performance and timing requirements of the
application.


3.6 Summary
The state-of-the-art practice of application mapping for real-time composable systems
targeting many-core platforms is restricted to a fixed inter-application isolation scheme
that is applied exclusively on each and every mapping of every application in the
system, excluding numerous promising mappings from the mapping solution space as
elaborated in Section 3.1. This restriction mainly roots in the inability of the existing
timing analyses applied in a many-core context in capturing all timing components
contributing to the execution latency of an application in a many-core system, in the
general case.
   As a remedy, this chapter presented a (i) worst-case timing analysis specifically
developed for many-core systems and, enabled by it, an (ii) isolation-aware mapping
optimization approach for the offline DSE in HAM. The isolation-aware mapping
optimization approach presented in Section 3.2 enables the mapping optimizer to



76
                                                                     3.6 Summary


explore combinations of inter-application isolation schemes applied on different re-
sources within each mapping generated during the mapping optimization process. For
each thereby obtained mapping, the timing analysis presented in Section 3.3 formally
bounds the worst-case timing properties based on the choices of isolation schemes
in that mapping. Together, the isolation-aware mapping optimization approach and
the timing analysis enable the optimizer to find high-quality mappings in which the
amount of reserved resources is well-adjusted to the performance and timing require-
ments of the application. A series of experimental results presented in Section 3.4
demonstrated the effectiveness of the proposed approach in enhancing the quality
of the obtained mappings in comparison to the current practices of inter-application
isolation in many-core systems.




                                                                                 77
           Thermal Composability in Hybrid
                                                                             4
                     Application Mapping

The ongoing advancements in the semiconductor industry have enabled a continuous
process technology downsizing which empowers the massive integration of resources
in many-core chips. Although many-core chips address the high computational-power
demand of modern embedded systems and applications, their dense integration of
resources gives rise to high temperatures on the chip which can jeopardize the system’s
thermal integrity and lead to permanent transistor failures or even chip burn-down. To
ensure thermal safety, many-core systems employ Dynamic Thermal Management
(DTM) mechanisms that monitor the thermal state of the chip steadily and counteract
hot spots autonomously using power-control mechanisms, e.g., power gating, to reduce
the temperature of the overheated regions into a safe range.
   Although they preserve the thermal integrity of the systems, DTM countermeasures
interfere with the execution of the applications which are running in the overheated
regions and, hence, may lead to deadline violations in a real-time context. In the case
of hard real-time applications, the deadline violations consequent to the activation of
DTM countermeasures can jeopardize the functional integrity of the whole system and
even put human lives at risk in safety-critical systems, e.g., in automotive or medical
domains. To prevent such unforeseeable interferences, the occurrence of thermal
violations must be prevented such that DTM countermeasures are never triggered in
the first place. State-of-the-art Hybrid Application Mapping (HAM) methodologies
do not consider this critical aspect in their mapping and management processes for
real-time applications and, thereby, indeed fail in providing safe real-time guarantees.
   As a remedy, this chapter presents a thermally composable HAM methodology
which introduces a (i) thermal-safety analysis into the design-time Design Space
Exploration (DSE) in HAM and provides the Run-time Platform Manager (RPM)
with a lightweight (ii) thermal-safety admission check to be used when launching
applications. The thermal-safety analysis derives an abstract thermal-safety index for
each mapping offline which is then used for the online thermal-safety admission check
on-the-fly. Using (i) and (ii), the proposed methodology enforces thermal safety at all
times proactively (by performing (ii) at the launch time of applications) so that DTM



                                                                                    79
4 Thermal Composability in Hybrid Application Mapping


countermeasures are never triggered and real-time guarantees are preserved for all
running applications.
   The remainder of this chapter is organized as follows1 . In Section 4.1, the concepts
of thermal safety and thermal composability are elucidated, and the contribution of
this chapter is motivated. Section 4.2 presents the proposed thermally composable
HAM methodology. In Section 4.3, the effectiveness of the proposed approach is
demonstrated through a series of experimental results. Finally, an overview of related
work is provided in Section 4.4, followed by a summary of the chapter in Section 4.5.


4.1 Thermal Safety and Thermal Composability
4.1.1 Many-Core Thermal Problem: Cause and Control
The consumption of power on a chip generates heat which, in turn, leads to a gradual
increase in temperature. Figure 4.1 illustrates the thermal impact (left) of application
execution in a many-core chip (right). As illustrated, the execution of the application
leads to a temperature increase in the region used by the application (due to power
consumption) as well as in the regions nearby the region used by the application
(due to heat transfer between neighboring regions). The magnitude and the spread of
temperature observed on a chip are directly related to the density of power consumption
and its distribution on the chip [PCS+ 18].
   The massive density of transistors integrated within many-core chips (enabled by
the continuous technology downsizing) results in a noticeably high density of power
consumption and, therefore, may also lead to a significant increase in the range of
observable temperatures on the chip. In this scope, the cooling solution of the chip,
namely, the combination of the thermal compound, heat spreader, heat sink, etc., is
designed so as to dissipate the generated heat rapidly and, thereby, prevent overheating.
However, due to the technological limitations of the cooling subsystem and chip
packaging, the generated heat in a many-core chip cannot be dissipated fast enough to
prevent excessively high temperatures that may harm the thermal integrity of the chip
by causing permanent transistor failures [SGH+ 14].
   In this context, many-core systems rely on DTM mechanisms. Such mechanisms
steadily monitor the temperature of different regions of the chip and use power-control
mechanisms such as power gating, clock gating, or Dynamic Voltage and Frequency
Scaling (DVFS) to autonomously counteract overheating in regions where the observed
temperature exceeds a predefined critical temperature threshold [HC17; KPS+ 17]. Al-
though they preserve the thermal integrity of the chip, such countermeasures interfere
with the execution of the applications that are running in the thermally affected regions
and may lead to deadline violations for real-time applications which is not tolerable
 1 The contents of this chapter are based on and partly published in [PSK+ 19] which has appeared in

     the Proceedings of the 40th IEEE Real-Time Systems Symposium (RTSS).



80
                                       4.1 Thermal Safety and Thermal Composability

                                                                                      80°C



                                                                                      60°C

   application
                                                                                      40°C



                                                                                      20°C
                          many-core platform                 heatmap of the chip

     Figure 4.1: Thermal impact of application execution on a many-core chip.


in a hard real-time context. To prevent such unforeseeable interferences without
jeopardizing the thermal integrity of the chip, it must be proactively ensured that the
applications in the system have a thermally safe behavior such that thermal violations
are eliminated altogether and, hence, DTM countermeasures are never triggered.
   To verify the thermal safety of different mappings of an application, worst-case
thermal analysis can be used. Due to their relatively high computational overhead,
thermal analyses are not suited for online use and, therefore, must be performed offline.
This restriction can, however, be properly addressed in the context of HAM since, in
HAM, the set of mappings for each application is computed offline and, therefore,
the thermal analysis of each mapping may also be performed offline to identify and
discard mappings with unsafe thermal behavior.

4.1.2 Thermal Composability
Using thermal analysis, unsafe mappings can be identified in which the density of
power consumption results in the violation of the critical temperature threshold of
the chip. However, the sheer exclusion of such mappings does not guarantee thermal
safety: When applications are executed concurrently, the heat transfer between the
neighboring regions of the chip used by different applications can change the observed
temperature distribution within the region used by each application. As a result,
thermal violations may occur within the regions used by one or multiple running
applications, even though the mapping used by each of the applications has been
verified to have a thermally safe behavior in solitude.
   Such a situation is exemplified in Figure 4.2 where a new application is to be
launched in a system where two other applications are already running, see Figure 4.2a.
The initial thermal state of the chip prior to the launch of the new application, illustrated
in Figure 4.2b, gives evidence that the two deployed applications are running thermally
safe. Note here also the temperature induced by the running applications on the region
to be used by the new application. Now, subject to the thermal profile of the mapping
considered for launching the new application, two possible thermal scenarios may



                                                                                             81
4 Thermal Composability in Hybrid Application Mapping


                                                               application 1
                                                                 (running)
             application 3
                (new)
                                                               application 2
                                                                 (running)

                                  (a) many-core platform

                           80°C                        80°C                         80°C


                           60°C                        60°C                         60°C



                           40°C                        40°C         ••              40°C


                           20°C                        20°C                         20°C

     (b) initial state (safe)      (c) scenario 1 (safe)       (d) scenario 2 (unsafe)

Figure 4.2: Example of possible thermal scenarios consequent to the concurrent execu-
             tion of applications in a many-core system. Due to heat transfer between
             neighboring regions of the chip, launching a new application may indeed
             cause thermal violations (marked cores in Figure 4.2d) even though each
             of the involved mappings is verified to be thermally safe in solitude.


arise: (i) The system may remain in a thermally safe state after launching the new
application, see Figure 4.2c. Here, the thermal behavior of the new mapping leaves a
temperature headroom large enough for the heat induced by the other applications on
the regions used by the new application. Likewise, the already running applications
also have a large enough temperature headroom for the heat induced by the new
application on their respective regions such that the system remains in a thermally safe
state after launching the new application. However, (ii) launching the new application
may also lead to thermal violations in the system if the heat induced by the concurrent
applications on the region used by an application exceeds the temperature headroom
of the respective region, see Figure 4.2d. Hence, even though each mapping is verified
to be thermally safe in solitude, the combination of active mappings in the system may
still cause thermal violations, see the overheated (marked) cores in Figure 4.2d.
   In the context of HAM, to prevent such thermal violation scenarios, not only
(i) each and every mapping must be verified to have a thermally safe behavior but
also (ii) thermal composability must be established in a many-core system. Thermal
composability ensures that none of the applications in the system is exposed to thermal
violations induced due to the presence or the thermal activity of other applications
in the system. Only through thermal composability, it can be ensured that the timing



82
                           4.2 Thermally Composable Hybrid Application Mapping


guarantees of real-time applications are not at risk due to unforeseeable interferences
from DTM countermeasures triggered in reaction to thermal violations caused by other
running applications. State-of-the-art HAM methodologies do not consider thermal
composability in their mapping and management processes for real-time applications
and, hence, indeed fail in providing safe real-time guarantees.
   Establishing thermal composability in a system, however, is challenging since it is
generally not feasible to control the heat transfer between neighboring regions on a
chip. In this scope, admission control mechanisms are required to preserve thermal
composability by verifying the post-launch thermal safety of the system prior to
launching new applications. Such mechanisms rely on a worst-case thermal analysis
which takes the actual distribution of the applications in the system and their thermal
profile into account to provide worst-case thermal-safety guarantees, similarly to the
thermal-safety analysis of a mapping. This, however, necessitates the thermal analysis
to be performed online (at the launch time of applications) when the set of running
applications and their deployment of the chip are known. However, performing thermal
analysis online is not practical due to its high computational overhead.

Contribution Overview

This work presents a thermally composable HAM methodology which enforces thermal
safety proactively at the launch time of an application without relying on an online
worst-case thermal analysis of the system. The proposed methodology introduces
a (i) thermal-safety analysis into the DSE in HAM and provides the RPM with a
lightweight (ii) thermal-safety admission check to be used when launching applica-
tions. The thermal-safety analysis computes an abstract thermal-safety index for each
mapping which expresses its thermal safety as a function of the number of simulta-
neously active cores in the system. At run time, the thermal-safety admission check
uses the precomputed thermal-safety indices of mappings for a lightweight examina-
tion of the system’s thermal safety when launching applications without involving
a time-consuming worst-case thermal analysis of the system. Using the admission
check, the RPM can identify and avoid mapping candidates which may lead to thermal
violations for the given system load and, thereby, preserves thermal safety proactively
and establishes thermal composability. As a result, without jeopardizing the system’s
thermal integrity, the exposure of real-time applications to DTM countermeasures is
eliminated and, hence, real-time guarantees remain intact.


4.2 Thermally Composable Hybrid Application
    Mapping
This section presents the proposed thermally composable HAM methodology. Fig-
ure 4.3 provides an overview of the proposed methodology where the novel compo-

nents and steps are highlighted in blue and outlined in the following. The proposed
approach introduces an offline (i) thermal-safety analysis and an online (ii) thermal-
safety admission check into the standard flow of HAM. The offline thermal-safety
analysis investigates the thermal behavior of each generated mapping. It (i) verifies the
thermal safety of each mapping in solitude and (ii) derives an abstract thermal-safety
index for each mapping, termed Thermally Safe Utilization (TSU). The TSU of a
mapping expresses the thermal safety of that mapping as a function of the number
of simultaneously active cores in the system. By incorporating the thermal-safety
analysis as an evaluator into the offline DSE (in addition to worst-case timing analy-
sis), a thermal-aware DSE scheme is established in which not only the satisfaction of
real-time requirements is verified, but also mappings are optimized w.r.t. their thermal
safety so that Pareto-optimal mappings with desirable thermal behavior are obtained.
At run time, when launching an application, the RPM uses the proposed thermal-safety
admission check to examine the thermal safety of each mapping candidate for the
current system load based on the precomputed TSU of mappings without involving a
time-consuming worst-case thermal analysis.
   In the following, first, the proposed offline thermal-safety analysis is presented
in Section 4.2.1. Then, the online thermal-safety admission check is presented in Sec-
tion 4.2.2. Finally, the proposed thermally composable HAM methodology is pre-



84
                           4.2 Thermally Composable Hybrid Application Mapping


sented in Section 4.2.3 which incorporates the thermal-safety analysis and admission
check into the offline and online phases of HAM, respectively, to guarantee the thermal
safety of the system proactively and, thereby, establish thermal composability.

4.2.1 Thermal-Safety Analysis of Mappings
The proposed offline thermal-safety analysis derives an abstract thermal-safety index,
namely, Thermally Safe Utilization (TSU), for each mapping by means of worst-case
thermal analysis. The TSU of each mapping not only can be used to examine the
thermal safety of that mapping in solitude but also facilitates the online thermal-
safety admission check. In fact, it enables the RPM to examine the thermal safety of
the system based on the TSU of active mappings and the number of cores that are
simultaneously active in the system.
   Thermal analysis of mappings and systems can be performed based on temperature
itself or based on power consumption and power density as the main cause of heat
generation in the system. In the context of HAM, the worst-case thermal-safety
analysis of mappings based on a constraint on temperature itself is not practical. That
is because, in a thermal analysis based on a temperature constraint, the temperature of
each core can be derived only if global knowledge about the thermal activity of all
cores in the system is available to evaluate the heat transfer between cores [HGV+ 06].
In HAM, however, the information about the system load will be known only at run
time when all applications running in the system are known and, hence, is not available
offline at the time of thermal analysis of individual mappings.
   In contrast to temperature, the density of power consumption in each mapping can
be derived offline since it merely depends on the execution profile and activity of the
application on the allocated resources in that mapping. Therefore, the work at hand
uses the thermal analysis approach presented in [KPS+ 17; PKC+ 17] which derives a
constraint on the density of power consumption in the chip. This so-called thermally
safe power-density constraint is guaranteed to enforce thermal safety w.r.t. a given
critical temperature threshold. It is derived based on (i) the thermal model of the chip
(including its floorplan, RC thermal network, cooling subsystem, etc.) and (ii) the
system utilization denoting the number of simultaneously active cores in the system.
In its model, the thermal analysis from [KPS+ 17; PKC+ 17] also takes into account the
heat transfer between the cores on the chip, and its power-density constraint is verified
to guarantee thermal safety as demonstrated in [PKC+ 17].
   In the proposed thermal-safety analysis of mappings, the worst-case thermal analysis
from [KPS+ 17; PKC+ 17] is used to derive a thermally safe power-density constraint
for each system utilization level (number of active cores). Using the power-density
constraints derived for different system utilization levels, a so-called Thermally Safe
Power-density Dictionary (TSPD) is constructed as a thermal-safety reference for the
chip. The TSPD is then used for the thermal-safety analysis of each mapping at design
time to derive an abstract thermal-safety index (TSU) for that mapping based on the


                                                                                     85
4 Thermal Composability in Hybrid Application Mapping


peak power density observed on the cores allocated by that mapping. In the following,
first, the computation of TSPD is elucidated. Then, the calculation of the TSU of
mappings based on TSPD is detailed.

Thermally Safe Power-Density Dictionary (TSPD)

For the thermal-safety analysis of mappings, the worst-case thermal analysis presented
in [KPS+ 17; PKC+ 17] is used to calculate a thermal-safety reference for the chip
based on power density, referred to as the Thermally Safe Power-density Dictionary
(TSPD) which is defined as:
                   TSPD = {⟨n, PD⟩ | n = 1, 2, . . . , |C| , PD ≥ 0}              (4.1)
Each entry ⟨n, PD⟩ of the TSPD corresponds to the case of having n cores simultane-
ously active in the system and provides a power-density constraint PD that guarantees
thermal safety of the system as long as the density of power consumption on each and
every one of the n active cores does not exceed PD. In Eq. (4.1), |C| denotes the total
number of cores on the platform. The TSPD is computed once for a many-core chip
based on its thermal model (including the floorplan, RC thermal network, etc.) and is
then used for the thermal-safety analysis of any mapping generated for that platform.
   For the calculation of the ⟨n, PD⟩ entries of the TSPD, the thermal-safe power-
density analysis from [KPS+ 17; PKC+ 17] is used. For each n = 1, 2, . . . , |C|, this
analysis can identify the worst possible selection of n simultaneously active cores
which results in the highest possible temperature in the system. For this selection of n
active cores, the analysis derives a power-density constraint PD that ensures thermal
safety. Here, ⟨n, PD⟩ constitutes one entry of the TSPD. Since PD is derived based
on the worst possible selection of n simultaneously active cores, it is guaranteed to
ensure thermal safety for any selection of n simultaneously active cores in the system,
i.e., n active cores from any core type and at any location on the chip. This enables
abstracting away from the type and the location of active cores on the chip during the
calculation of the TSPD which considerably facilitates the TSU analysis of mappings
(at design time) and the thermal-safety admission check (at run time).
   To calculate the TSPD, first, we derive the thermal model of the chip and its cooling
subsystem using the HotSpot simulator [HGV+ 06]. This information serves as input
for the analysis from [KPS+ 17; PKC+ 17]. Using this analysis, we derive PD for each
possible number n = 1, 2, . . . , |C| of simultaneously active cores in the system and,
thereby, construct all ⟨n, PD⟩ entries of the TSPD. Figure 4.4 visualizes the TSPD
calculated for a 52-core many-core chip (which will be used later in this chapter for
the experimental evaluations). The illustrated TSPD denotes that, for instance, for a
total of n = 16 simultaneously active cores in the system, the thermal safety of the
system is guaranteed as long as the density of power consumption per active core does
not exceed PD = 1.49 W/mm2 . After all entries of the TSPD are calculated, it will be
used for the thermal-safety analysis of mappings as presented in the following.
