2 (div − 1)(div) < k, resulting in div =      2      − 1, see line 2 of the algorithm.
   After the grid granularity (parameter div) has been determined, each mapping
µ ∈ Γ will be associated with its respective quality region (cell), see lines 3–7 of
the algorithm. Here, the function CellID(µ) returns the grid coordinates (i, j) of the
quality region celli, j that encompasses mapping µ in the hyper-grid.
   In the second step of the algorithm (lines 8–21), the distillation of representative
mappings takes place. To that end, first, the Pareto ranking of mappings in the space
of resource objectives Ores is performed as described in Section 5.2.2 using function
ParetoRank (line 8). Subsequently, representative mappings are distilled from quality-
dominant regions (lines 9–16), and eventually, resource-efficient mappings are distilled
(lines 17–21). For the distillation of quality-dominant mappings (lines 9–16), the
quality-dominant regions (cells) are explored in the order of dominance ≻ specified
by Eq. (5.1). From each region (cell), one mapping µ is distilled using function Select
(line 12) and is added to the initially empty set of distilled mappings Γ ∗ (line 13).
Function Select will be discussed later in detail.

                                                  celli, j ≻ celli′ , j′ ⇐⇒ i ≤ i′ ∧ j ≤ j′                  (5.1)


                                                                                                              115
5 Automatic Mapping-Set Distillation


   Since quality trade-offs are approximated by the hyper-grid, a distilled mapping µ
may dominate further mappings µ ′ ∈ Γ based on the approximated quality trade-offs3 .
Here, a distilled mapping µ dominates mapping µ ′ ∈ Γ if (i) cell(µ) ≻ cell(µ ′ )
according to Eq. (5.1), and (ii) µ dominates µ ′ w.r.t. the resource objectives Ores ,
denoted by ≻Ores . During the distillation procedure, once a mapping µ has been
distilled, the remaining mappings µ ′ ∈ Γ are examined, and those that are dominated
by µ according to (i) and (ii) are eliminated from Γ together with µ (line 14) before
proceeding to the next cells. After the distillation of mappings from quality-dominant
regions (lines 9–16) has been performed as detailed above, resource-efficient mappings
are distilled from Γ (lines 17–21), in a similar procedure.

Function “Select”

In the proposed distillation algorithm, function Select is used to distill a mapping
from a given set of mappings, see lines 12 and 18 in Algorithm 1. For this purpose,
it ranks the given mappings based on an estimate of their embeddability and, then,
distills the mapping that has the highest rank, i.e., the highest estimated embeddability.
Note that, embeddability denotes the probability of a mapping being embeddable at
different run-time resource availability scenarios which can be accurately assessed
only at run time when the resource availability scenarios become known. Hence, an
exact assessment of embeddability for each mapping is not feasible offline at the time
of distillation. As a remedy, we use a heuristic to obtain an estimate of the relative
embeddability of the given mappings based on which a preference scheme can be
established between different mappings w.r.t. embeddability.
   For the ranking of mappings, function Select compares them according to their
(i) Pareto rank w.r.t. resource objectives and (ii) overall resource cost. The overall
resource cost of a mapping is calculated as its accumulative utilization rate of different
core types, where the utilization rate of a core type by a mapping is defined as the
proportion of the number of instances of that core type allocated by the mapping.
Here, a mapping with a lower Pareto rank w.r.t. resource objectives (primarily) and a
lower resource cost (secondarily) is taken to have a higher relative embeddability and,
hence, is prioritized for distillation.
   If two mappings offer the same relative embeddability estimate according to
(i) and (ii), function Select considers two additional criteria to reflect the quality
measures of mappings in their ranking: (iii) the distance of the quality region of each
mapping from the best quality region (cell1,1 ), calculated as (i − 1) + ( j − 1) for
celli, j , and (iv) the number of already distilled mappings from each respective quality
region. Here, under identical rankings based on (i) and (ii), a mapping from a region
(cell) with a better quality trade-off (primarily) and fewer representative mappings in
 3 Note that any pair of mappings µ ∈ Γ and µ ′ ∈ Γ are Pareto optimal based on their accurate quality

   measures. However, the approximation of quality trade-offs (by means of the hyper-grid) may alter
   the dominance relation between µ and µ ′ such that µ dominates µ ′ or vice versa.



116
                                                              5.3 Experimental Results


the distilled set (secondarily) is prioritized for distillation. Note that, since mappings
from the same quality region share the same preference w.r.t. (iii) and (iv), these
metrics come into effect only for the distillation of resource-efficient mappings where
the set of considered mappings belong to different quality regions.

Computational Complexity

The proposed distillation algorithm is established based on functions ParetoRank and
Select as well as a set of for and while loops as shown in Algorithm 1. Pareto rank-
ing of a set of mappings Γ w.r.t. a set of resource objectives Ores has a computational
complexity of O(|Γ |2 |Ores |) where |Γ | and |Ores | denote the cardinality of sets Γ
and Ores , respectively, cf. [ZTC+ 15]. Hence, function ParetoRank has a maximum
computational complexity of O(|Γ |2 |Ores |). Function Select embodies a find-min
procedure with an iteration space of |Γ | mapping evaluations, each involving an enu-
meration of the mapping qualities w.r.t. resource objectives Ores and quality objectives
Oqlt . Thus, function Select introduces a maximum computational complexity of
O(|Γ |(|Ores | + |Oqlt |)).
   The iteration space of the quality-partitioning loops in Algorithm 1 (lines 3–7)
and the overall iteration space of the distillation loops (lines 9–21) each scale with
div2 . Given that div2 ∝ k (see line 2), the proposed distillation algorithm introduces
an overall computational complexity of O(k|Γ | + |Γ |2 |Ores | + k|Γ |(|Ores | + |Oqlt |))
which can be simplified as O(|Γ |2 |Ores | + k|Γ |(1 + |Ores | + |Oqlt |)). Given that, in
general, |Ores | ≈ |Oqlt | and k ≤ |Γ |, the computational complexity of Pareto ranking
dominates the other terms, and thus, the overall computational complexity of the
proposed distillation algorithm becomes O(|Γ |2 |Ores |) which is certainly affordable
as the distillation takes place at design time.


5.3 Experimental Results
This section presents the results of a series of experiments that assess the effective-
ness of the proposed distillation methodology compared to multi-objective truncation
techniques that are typically used for mapping-set distillation in the context of HAM.
The experiments entail DSE of several applications to obtain Pareto-optimal map-
pings for distillation, followed by a series of run-time embedding trials to assess the
embeddability of the distilled mappings in view of a dynamic availability of resources.

5.3.1 Experiments Setup
Applications and Architectures     As platform, a 36-core heterogeneous many-core
architecture with a 6 × 6 NoC organization and single-core tiles is used. The platform
comprises diverse numbers of instances from three types of cores (IBM PowerPC,



                                                                                      117
5 Automatic Mapping-Set Distillation


AMD K6-2, and AMD K6-III) whose specifications are provided by Embedded
System Synthesis Benchmarks Suite (E3S) [Dic10]. As applications, four real-time
applications are used from the embedded domains of networking (7 tasks, 9 messages),
consumer (11 tasks, 12 messages), telecommunications (14 tasks, 20 messages), and
automotive (18 tasks, 21 messages). The applications are provided as benchmarks by
E3S [Dic10]. For each application, the benchmarks suite also provides the payload
size of each message and the Worst-Case Execution Time (WCET) and the power
consumption of each task on each core type.

Design Objectives     The offline optimization of mappings for each application is
performed w.r.t. two quality objectives, namely, end-to-end latency (makespan) and
energy consumption, as well as three resource objectives denoting the number of
allocated cores per core type. The worst-case latency of each mapping is derived
using the timing analysis from Chapter 3. The energy consumption of each mapping
is evaluated based on the Worst-Case Response Time (WCRT) of its tasks, their
power consumption, the payload size of each message, and the NoC/bus energy model
from [WSK+ 05] considering a length of 2 mm for NoC links and 5 mm for buses.

Design Space Exploration (DSE)        For the optimization of mappings, the DSE ap-
proach from [WGW+ 14] is employed which uses the NSGA-II [DPA+ 02] multi-
objective evolutionary algorithm that is provided by the optimization framework
O PT 4J [LGR+ 11]. The DSE is configured to perform 2,000 optimization iterations
with a population size of 100 mappings where 25 mappings are generated per itera-
tion. During the optimization, a per se not bounded set of Pareto-optimal mappings
(archive) is collected and updated which will be the output of the DSE. After the DSE,
the constraint graph of each obtained Pareto-optimal mapping is constructed as de-
tailed in [WGW+ 14]. The Pareto-optimal mappings serve as input to the investigated
mapping-set distillation methodologies introduced in the following.

Investigated Distillation Approaches      In the experiments, the proposed distillation
methodology is compared with three standard truncation techniques, namely, Crowd-
ing Distance (CD) [DPA+ 02], Nearest Neighbor (NN) [ZLT01], and Adaptive Grid
(AG) [KC03], from the domain of multi-objective optimization. For the distillation
of mappings, a distillation capacity of k = 12 mappings is considered to achieve an
affordable RPM overhead according to the overhead measures given in Table 5.1 when
scaled to an embedded setting. In addition to the proposed distillation methodology
and the multi-objective truncation techniques above, we also incorporate the set of
Pareto-optimal mappings w.r.t. quality objectives only, referred to as quality front,
and the set of Pareto-optimal mappings w.r.t. resource objectives only, referred to as
resource front, as references of comparison in the experiments.




118
                                                           5.3 Experimental Results


Run-Time Embedding and Feasibility Check          For the run-time embedding of map-
pings, the backtracking-based algorithm from [WWG+ 18a] is employed for the em-
bedding feasibility check of the constraint graph of each mapping. To emulate the
dynamism in resource availability at run time, various resource availability scenarios
are constructed as follows: Starting with an empty platform, i.e., 100% resource
availability, cores are preoccupied one by one, iteratively, and at random. At each
iteration, a run-time embedding experiment is performed in which the embeddability
of the mappings distilled by each approach is assessed for the current availability of
resources using the backtracking feasibility check. As part of this assessment, the
following data is recorded: (i) the number of embeddable mappings from the set of dis-
tilled mappings provided by each distillation approach and the best quality achievable
by the embeddable subset of mappings w.r.t. (ii) latency, (iii) energy consumption,
and (iv) Energy-Latency Product (ELP). The iterations terminate when all cores are
preoccupied.

5.3.2 Results Discussion
The following experimental results provide a comparative investigation of the effec-
tiveness of the considered distillation methodologies in terms of the embeddability
of distilled mappings and the diversity of the quality trade-offs offered by them. The
provided results represent an average over the 10 runs of DSE per application and the
five trials of the embedding experiment per DSE run to incorporate diverse mixes of
preoccupied cores for the embedding feasibility checks at each utilization level.

Run-Time Embeddability

The run-time embeddability of the mappings provided by each distillation methodology
is evaluated using two parameters: success rate and embeddability rate. For a given
set of mappings and a set of available resources, success rate denotes whether at least
one mapping can be feasibly embedded on the given set of available resources. Thus,
success rate reflects whether the application in question can at all be launched using
the provided set of distilled mappings. Embeddability rate, on the other hand, denotes
the proportion of embeddable mappings and, thus, reflects the flexibility of the RPM in
choosing between multiple embeddable mappings, e.g., to enhance energy efficiency.
   The success rate and the embeddability rate observed in the run-time embedding
experiments are provided in Figure 5.5 and Figure 5.6, respectively, for various
distilled and reference sets of mappings. In each figure, each plot corresponds to
one benchmark application specified by the plot title. In Figure 5.5, the original set
denotes the initial set of Pareto-optimal mappings and, hence, serves as the best-case
reference for the maximum achievable success rate. In Figure 5.6, the resource front
denotes the set of Pareto-optimal mappings w.r.t. resource objectives and, thus, serves
as a (near-)optimal reference for embeddability rate.

   As illustrated in Figures 5.5 and 5.6, while the ranking of the three standard multi-
objective truncation techniques, namely, CD, NN, and AG, varies per application
and criterion (success- or embeddability rate), the proposed distillation methodology
seamlessly outperforms the standard truncation techniques for all applications w.r.t.
both success rate and embeddability rate, especially at higher system utilization levels
where only a limited set of resources are available. For each application and criterion,
the proposed methodology improves the success rate by 8% on average (up to 45%)
and embeddability rate by 16% on average (up to 45%) compared to the standard
truncation technique with the best average results for the respective application and
criterion. Note that while the enhanced success rate is crucial to bring applications
to execution, the enhanced embeddability rate extends the options for the RPM to
select between different embeddable mappings. Also note that, when merely the
quality-dominant mappings are retained (quality front), embeddability is drastically
degraded as the retained mappings often impose a high demand for resources.

Quality Trade-Offs

To assess the diversity and efficiency of the distilled mappings w.r.t. their quality
trade-offs, the well-established ε-dominance indicator [LTD+ 02] from the domain of
multi-objective optimization is used. ε-dominance provides a scalar value ε ∈ [0, 1)
which reflects the quality of a set of solutions Γ relative to a given reference set Γref of
best solutions in a given space of multiple objectives. Formally, the ε-dominance of
set Γ w.r.t. reference set Γref provides the smallest value of ε for which all solutions
in Γref will be dominated by the solutions in Γ if the latter are scaled at a rate of
(1 − ε) w.r.t. all objectives, see Eq. (3.13) for the formal definition. Thus, a lower
ε-dominance indicator for Γ denotes a higher resemblance between Γ and Γref and,
hence, a higher quality of mappings in Γ .
   Figure 5.7 provides the ε-dominance of the distilled sets of mappings obtained
by each investigated approach w.r.t. the quality objectives. For each application,
the size of the original set of mappings is given in parentheses. Recall that each

  ε-dominance

                0.2
                0.1
                 0
                      networking (151)   consumer (262)       telecom (603)      automotive (422)

Figure 5.7: ε-Dominance of the distillation approaches (distillation capacity k = 12)
                        w.r.t. quality objectives for different applications, where the original num-
                        ber of Pareto-optimal mappings is given in parentheses [PGT17].


distillation approach retains a total of only k = 12 mappings. For the evaluation
of ε-dominance, the quality front is used as the reference set since it comprises
the Pareto-optimal mappings w.r.t. quality objectives. Resource-efficient mappings
retained in the resource front typically exhibit inferior quality measures as reflected by
the high ε-dominance of the resource front in Figure 5.7. As illustrated, the proposed
distillation methodology outperforms the three standard truncation techniques as it
exhibits the lowest ε-dominance for nearly all applications. In comparison to the
standard truncation approaches, the proposed methodology exhibits an ε-dominance
reduction of 0.04–0.06 on average over all applications, which translates into a relative
improvement of 35% on average (up to 42%).
   In the case of the consumer application, the proposed distillation methodology
exhibits a higher ε-dominance compared to standard techniques. For this application,
the distribution of mappings in the space of quality objectives is such that a small
number of quality regions contain a huge number of mappings while other regions
are empty. Hence, the majority of distilled mappings are resource-efficient mappings
while only a small portion of the distilled mappings belong to the quality-dominant
regions, resulting in a degraded ε-dominance. Note that, for this application, the
proposed approach significantly improves the embeddability rate, cf. Figure 5.6.
   While Figure 5.7 provides a measure for the distribution of the quality trade-offs
of distilled mappings, the quality trade-offs that can be practically achieved by a
set of mappings can be assessed only in conjunction with embeddability since a
trade-off becomes reachable only if the respective mapping is indeed embeddable.
To assess the reachable quality trade-offs for each set of distilled mappings, during
the run-time embedding experiment, the best quality achievable by the embeddable
subset of mappings in terms of (i) end-to-end latency, (ii) energy consumption, and
(iii) Energy-Latency Product (ELP) is recorded as well. ELP provides a measure of

the efficiency of quality trade-offs w.r.t. latency and energy consumption.
   Figure 5.8 provides the obtained results for the exemplary application of telecom.
Similar results are obtained for other applications as well. Here, the original set
provides the best-case reference in all three aspects. As illustrated, the quality front
performs similarly to the original set at low system utilization levels since, there,
nearly all mappings are embeddable and, hence, the quality trade-offs of the distilled
mappings become the decisive factor of the best achievable quality. However, as the
system utilization increases, the mappings in the quality front lose their embeddability
rapidly due to their high demand for resources. In fact, none of the mappings in the
quality front are embeddable at a system utilization level of 70% or higher. On the
other hand, the mappings in the resource front exhibit low energy consumption but
suffer from high latency, resulting in a degraded ELP of the resource front as well.
   Note the decrease in the energy consumption and ELP plots at high system uti-
lization levels in Figure 5.8. This decrease contradicts the expected increasing trend
observed at low system utilization levels and occurs for the following reason: The
results in Figure 5.8 represent an average over 10 runs of the DSE. Mappings from
different DSE runs lose their embeddability at different levels of system utilization.
At a high system utilization, the—otherwise increasing—minimum energy (and, thus,
minimum ELP) measures indeed decrease since, for some DSE runs, none of the
mappings is embeddable anymore. This results in a lower average energy consumption



                                                                                                                           123
5 Automatic Mapping-Set Distillation


(and, thereby, lower ELP) of embeddable mappings, compare to the case where some
mappings are still embeddable but exhibit a high energy demand (resulting in a higher
average energy demand). In the case of a single DSE run, however, the measures
monotonically increase. The same observation also applies where the minimum qual-
ity measure of a distilled set falls below that of the original set which will never occur
if the results are investigated per DSE run.
   As illustrated in Figure 5.8, the proposed methodology exhibits a high efficiency of
achievable quality trade-offs indicated by its low ELP measures. In fact, compared
to the standard truncation technique with the best ELP, the proposed methodology
achieves an average ELP improvement of 7% (up to 18%) for system utilization levels
of up to 80%. Taking the quality properties individually into account, the proposed
approach achieves efficient measures w.r.t. both latency and energy consumption,
whereas each standard truncation technique exhibits degraded achievable quality
measures in one or even both criteria.


5.4 Related Work
In general, Hybrid Application Mapping (HAM) methodologies which consider
mapping-set distillation rely on standard truncation techniques for the selection of
representative mappings. In the optimization paradigm, the multi-objective truncation
problem is well-studied [VDP15; HBK05], and several de facto standard truncation
techniques are widely used. These techniques aim at retaining a diverse set of so-
lutions by maximizing the spacing4 among the distilled solutions [HBK05]. For
instance, the so-called Adaptive Grid (AG) truncation technique in [KC03] places
a multi-dimensional grid in the objective space and prunes crowded grid cells iter-
atively to attain a uniform spread of the retained solutions. In [ZLT01], solutions
with the smallest distance to their Nearest Neighbor (NN) are eliminated iteratively to
maximize the distances among the remaining solutions. The approach in [DPA+ 02]
introduces a dispersion metric termed Crowding Distance (CD) to identify crowded
regions of the objective space which are pruned accordingly for a uniform spread of
retained solutions.
   In recent years, several truncation techniques have also emerged which prioritize
the efficiency of retained trade-offs over their diversity. They aim at identifying the
so-called knee-point solutions which offer the smallest trade-off loss w.r.t. all objec-
tives compared to their neighboring solutions (which often incur a large loss in at least
one objective to attain a small improvement in another objective) [CYJ16; BSR+ 17;
YJO18]. As discussed earlier in this chapter, multi-objective truncation techniques
indeed fail in preserving embeddability when adopted for mapping-set distillation

 4 Spacing provides a measure of how evenly the retained solutions are spread over the objective space

   covered by the original set where a higher spacing implies a better diversity of retained solutions.



124
                                                                         5.5 Summary


since they regard all objectives independently and, therefore, cannot consider the inter-
dependence between resource objectives and the impact of their collective efficiency
on embeddability. Thus, in the context of HAM, novel distillation methodologies
are required which are specifically designed to preserve both the diversity of quality
trade-offs and embeddability of the original mapping set.
    The HAM methodologies in [SKS13; KSK+ 13] introduce problem-specific dis-
tillation schemes customized for an optimization of mappings w.r.t. throughput and
energy consumption as quality objectives. In [SKS13], first, the mapping with the
highest throughput and the one with the lowest energy consumption are identified
for each combination of the number of allocated instances per core type. Then, from
the identified mappings, the Pareto-optimal ones in the space of energy, throughput,
and resource objectives are distilled. In [KSK+ 13], for each resource combination,
the mapping with the highest ratio of throughput/energy is retained. In spite of their
consideration of distillation, both approaches retain representative solutions for every
possible resource combination which can grow prohibitively with the application’s
size (number of tasks) and platform’s heterogeneity (number of resource types). To
alleviate the RPM’s overhead, both approached [SKS13; KSK+ 13] provide the RPM
with a statically sorted set of mappings to eliminate the need for thorough feasibility
checks. Nonetheless, numerous mappings may still have to be checked before the
first embeddable one is found. Furthermore, a static prioritization of mappings for all
possible online scenarios may degrade the efficiency of the RPM, cf. [WGT14].
    The distillation methodology proposed in this work addresses the shortcomings
of the approaches discussed above. It offers a (i) configurable distillation capacity
and distills mappings with (ii) a high diversity of quality trade-offs while retaining
mappings with (iii) efficient yet diverse resource requirements to enhance run-time
embeddability.


5.5 Summary
The offline DSE in HAM incorporates several quality objectives, e.g., latency, through-
put, and energy consumption, and several resource objectives, one per core type in the
architecture, for the optimization of mappings. As a result, a huge set of Pareto-optimal
mappings is typically obtained from the DSE which may impose an intolerable evalu-
ation and decision-making overhead on the RPM and even put real-time constraints at
the risk of violation by degrading the responsiveness of the RPM. Thus, only a small
subset of the mappings may be practically considered for online use. The process of
selecting a representative subset of mappings is referred to as mapping-set distillation.
State-of-the-art HAM methodologies either disregard distillation altogether or rely on
problem-specific distillation schemes or generic multi-objective truncation techniques
that suffer from limited applicability and poor distillation performance, respectively.
   As a remedy, this chapter presented in Section 5.2 an automatic and configurable



                                                                                    125
5 Automatic Mapping-Set Distillation


distillation methodology tailored to the distillation of Pareto-optimal mappings in
the context of heterogeneous many-core systems. By distinguishing resource-related
design objectives from quality-related objectives and proper handling of each set, the
proposed methodology retains a configurable number of Pareto-optimal mappings,
offering diverse quality trade-offs and, at the same time, modest resource demands to
enhance the run-time embeddability of the application. A series of experimental results
presented in Section 5.3 demonstrated the effectiveness of the proposed methodology
in comparison to well-established multi-objective truncation techniques.




126
              Hard Real-Time Online Mapping
                                                                             6
                                  Adaptation

In line with the principles of Hybrid Application Mapping (HAM), the methodologies
presented in Chapters 3 to 5 enable a timing-predictable and deadline-adhering execu-
tion of real-time applications in dynamic embedded systems targeting heterogeneous
many-core platforms. In light of these methodologies, real-time applications can be
launched and terminated dynamically on-demand using per-computed mappings with
worst-case timing guarantees and at an affordable online mapping and management
overhead. In this context, each application is launched using a mapping that is feasible
w.r.t. the current situation, i.e., relies only on available resources and satisfies the
non-functional requirements of the application and system, e.g., real-time constraints.
   In many-core systems, the dynamism created by the state of resources and the
workload of the applications puts the feasibility of mappings in use by running
applications at a steady risk of annihilation: On the one hand, the relentless shrinking
of feature size in semiconductor technologies invested in many-core platforms renders
them increasingly susceptible to resource failures, e.g., due to hardware faults or
overheating. Such situations annihilate the feasibility of active mappings that depend
on the failed resources. On the other hand, changes in the non-functional requirements
and/or workload of an application may lead to situations in which the mapping in use
can no longer satisfy the given requirements, and hence, its feasibility is obliterated.
To preserve the intended execution of applications in spite of such unforeseeable
events, it becomes inevitable to adapt their mappings on-the-fly during their execution.
Mapping adaptation is particularly crucial for hard real-time applications as a violation
of their timing constraints is not tolerable and may even lead to system failure.
   Mapping adaptation is realized using task migration and may involve the relocation
of several (if not all) tasks of the application which entails a temporal overhead. To
ensure a seamless satisfaction of hard timing constraints, it must be guaranteed that
both the temporal overhead of the adaptation process and the timing behavior of the
post-adaptation mapping of the application respect the given timing constraints. This,
in turn, necessitates a timing-predictable adaptation mechanism so that the worst-
case latency of the adaptation process can be analyzed. Moreover, timing analysis



                                                                                    127
6 Hard Real-Time Online Mapping Adaptation


is also required to derive the necessary timing guarantees. To that end, this chapter
presents two timing-predictable mapping adaptation methodologies for hard real-
time applications in heterogeneous many-core systems: a (i) mapping reconfiguration
methodology and a (ii) task migration methodology.
   In the proposed mapping reconfiguration methodology, online mapping adaptations
are realized in the form of a timing-predictable reconfiguration (switch) between
the statically computed mappings of applications with worst-case timing guarantees.
This methodology consists of a timing-predictable (i) reconfiguration mechanism
to be used online by the Run-time Platform Manager (RPM) to perform mapping
reconfigurations in a timing-predictable manner, a (ii) reconfiguration analysis which
is performed offline or hybrid (offline/online) to derive the necessary information
(including timing guarantees) for each reconfiguration option to be provided to the
RPM, and a lightweight (iii) reconfiguration admission check to be used by the RPM
to examine the admissibility of each reconfiguration option based on its previously
derived information and current timing constraints of the application.
   The proposed task migration methodology enables a finer granularity of mapping
adaptation by empowering the RPM to migrate any subset of the application’s tasks
to any destination cores with worst-case timing guarantees without relying on an
offline analysis or being restricted to a precomputed set of options. Thus, compared to
mapping reconfiguration, it enhances the flexibility of the RPM in choosing the degree
of modification to be exerted on a mapping. The proposed task migration methodology
consists of three components used online by the RPM: a timing-predictable (i) migra-
tion mechanism which enables the RPM to perform task migrations timing-predictably,
a lightweight (ii) migration analysis to formally bound the worst-case latency of
migration processes, and a lightweight (iii) migration admission check to examine the
admissibility of the migration latency w.r.t. the timing constraints of the application
and the fluctuations in its timing behavior during and after the migration processes.
   The remainder of this chapter is organized as follows1 . Section 6.1, elucidates the
role of mapping adaptation in many-core systems and outlines its major challenges.
In Section 6.2, the proposed mapping reconfiguration methodology is presented
and its effectiveness is demonstrated using experimental evaluations. Following
that, Section 6.3 presents the proposed task migration methodology and provides
an experimental evaluation of it in comparison to mapping reconfiguration. Finally,
an overview of related work on mapping adaptation in multi/many-core systems is
provided in Section 6.4, followed by a summary of the chapter in Section 6.5.


 1 The contents of this chapter are based on and partly published in [PWG+ 17; PWG+ 19; PSW+ 20]. In

   particular, the contents of Section 6.2 are based on [PWG+ 17] which has appeared in the Proceedings
   of the 25th International Conference on Real-Time Networks and Systems (RTNS) and [PWG+ 19]
   published in the Springer Journal of Real-Time Systems (RTS). The contents of Section 6.3 are
   based on [PSW+ 20] which has appeared in the Proceedings of the workshop on Next Generation
   Real-Time Embedded Systems (NG-RES).



128
                                    6.1 Mapping Adaptation in Many-Core Systems


6.1 Mapping Adaptation in Many-Core Systems
Dynamic embedded systems rely on adaptive application mapping and management
schemes, e.g., HAM, to achieve a high utilization of system resources in view of the
dynamism in the mix and workload of the concurrently executed applications. In
these systems, the mapping decisions made by the RPM are carried out based on the
currently available information about the state of the system resources and demands of
applications without prior knowledge about the future events which are typically not
known in advance and arrive in an unpredictable manner, e.g., launch request issued
by the user. In this context, it may be beneficial (e.g., for load balancing) or even
inevitable (e.g., in the case of hardware faults) to revise and adapt the mapping of
applications launched at an earlier stage according to an updated view of the state of
system resources and active applications.
   The online adaptation of the mapping of a running application is subject to several
challenges and obstacles. For instance, since the adaptation process modifies the
location of tasks in the system, an explicit synchronization between communicating
tasks becomes necessary to prevent data-flow inconsistencies consequent to out-
of-order delivery or even loss of messages. In fact, the mapping adaptation of an
application must happen transparently to the application itself. However, this is
hard to achieve since, especially in the case of heterogeneous many-core platforms,
mapping adaptation often relies on source code modification (or at least augmentation).
Moreover, it entails a temporal overhead which may be threatening for real-time
applications. This section provides an overview of the role of mapping adaptation in
many-core systems in Section 6.1.1, followed by a summary of major challenges of
mapping adaptation in the context of heterogeneous many-core systems and hard real-
time applications in Section 6.1.2. This overview provides the fundamentals required
for the presentation of the proposed mapping reconfiguration and task migration
methodologies in Sections 6.2 and 6.3.

6.1.1 Importance of Mapping Adaptation
The online adaptation of mappings in many-core systems involves the relocation
of tasks between cores and, thus, relies on task migration support [MDP+ 00]. The
initial practices of task migration date back to nearly three decades ago where it was
used in the scope of distributed systems and cluster computers for load balancing,
see, e.g., [CO95; LL94]. With the advent of embedded multi/many-core platforms,
task migration has also been adopted widely in embedded systems to adapt the
mapping of running applications, primarily for load balancing [HIB10] and energy
management [HV12]. In recent years, task migration has also been used for power
management and temperature balancing in many-core systems [RPM+ 19; LTH+ 14].
Moreover, in systems with a distributed memory scheme, task migration is used to
improve data locality and, hence, mitigate the load on the interconnection media, e.g.,



                                                                                  129
6 Hard Real-Time Online Mapping Adaptation


the Network-on-Chip (NoC) [KZL+ 11].
   As outlined above, mapping adaptation and task migration have proven effective
in enhancing the performance, resource utilization, and energy efficiency of many-
core systems. A mapping adaptation process, however, entails an inherent temporal
overhead which may become threatening for real-time applications, especially in the
case of hard timing constraints. Therefore, its practice is principally restricted to
best-effort applications. Recent years, however, give evidence of a shift in the role of
mapping adaptation in many-core systems such that it is increasingly becoming an
inevitable mechanism to preserve the desired execution of applications rather than
only a supplementary means for beneficial yet optional improvements. The ongoing
reduction in technology feature size brings about an unprecedented increase in the rate
of transient/permanent resource failures in many-core systems, e.g., due to hardware
faults or overheating. To preserve the execution of applications in view of such mostly
unpredictable events, mapping adaptation becomes necessary to evacuate the affected
resources and relocate their mapped tasks to other resources, see, e.g., [SPM09].
   In modern embedded applications, both the workload and the non-functional re-
quirements may change dynamically and unforeseeably, e.g., upon user requests.
Sometimes, the mapping in use by an application can no longer fulfill its requirements
in view of a change in its workload and/or requirements. Therefore, mapping adapta-
tion becomes inevitable to achieve a seamless satisfaction of the requirements, e.g., by
moving the critical tasks to powerful processing resources. Note that a mapping that
satisfies the most stringent requirements under all possible workload scenarios can, in
principle, lift the need for mapping adaptation consequent to workload/requirement
fluctuations. However, such mappings (if at all available) typically exhibit an immense
over-reservation and under-utilization of resources for the vast majority of possible
run-time situations. Therefore, in practice, they are typically avoided whenever pos-
sible. As a result, mapping adaptation remains the key solution to cope with the
dynamism in the workload and/or requirements of applications.
   Mapping adaptation is crucial for HAM methodologies in particular: In HAM,
applications are launched on-demand by selecting a feasible precomputed mapping,
i.e., a mapping which relies only on the currently available resources and satisfies the
non-functional requirements of the application. In some cases, an incoming application
cannot be launched at all, since, for none of its precomputed mappings, all required
resources are available. In such situations, mapping adaptation becomes crucial to
release the resources required for launching the incoming application by reorganizing
the already launched applications without disrupting their execution.

6.1.2 Challenges of Mapping Adaptation
Establishing the support for online mapping adaptation and task migration in many-
core systems is subject to several major challenges that are outlined in the following.




130
                                     6.1 Mapping Adaptation in Many-Core Systems


Context Relocation

The memory scheme of a system has a strong impact on the complexity of establishing
mapping adaptation support in it. In the case of a shared memory scheme, the
migration of tasks is often realized implicitly without requiring an actual relocation
of their context between different memories. Here, the context of a task denotes
the minimum set of data required for a seamless resumption of its execution after
migration [Smi88]. It consists of the address space of the task (i.e., code, data, and
stack) as well as its Process Control Block (PCB). The PCB of a task reflects its state
comprising the process identifier, contents of core registers, period, deadline, etc. In
the scope of many-core systems, the shared memory scheme within each tile enables
task migrations between different cores of the same tile, i.e., intra-tile migrations,
to be realized implicitly. Contrarily, due to the distributed memory scheme between
different tiles of a many-core platform, task migrations between different tiles, i.e.,
inter-tile migrations, involves an explicit relocation of the context of each migrating
task between its source and destination tiles. Here, an explicit reservation of a NoC
route for the relocation of the context becomes necessary to preserve composability.

Synchronization and Data-Flow Consistency

Inter-tile migrations also necessitate an explicit synchronization of data-dependent
tasks of the application to preserve its data-flow consistency. For this purpose, the
predecessor tasks of a migrating task (which send messages to it) must be notified about
the relocation so that new messages are sent to the new location thereafter. Moreover,
when migrating a task, a number of unprocessed input messages from its predecessors
may already exist or later arrive at the source location. These messages must be made
available at the destination location by the time the execution of the migrating task is
resumed there. In fact, to preserve data-flow consistency, all input messages of the
migrating task must be provided to it in the original order they would have reached it in
the absence of the migration. Likewise, output messages of migrating tasks must reach
their target tasks in their originally intended order. Improper handling of messages
during migration processes may give rise to an inconsistent execution behavior of the
application and/or erroneous computation results. When performing task migrations,
a synchronization between data-dependent tasks often relies on message-forwarding
and buffer-reordering service routines which are, however, typically not available in
embedded systems. This complicates inter-tile migrations and can potentially restrict
the practice of mapping adaptation to intra-tile migrations only.

Execution Preemption

Before the relocation process of a migrating task is initiated, its execution must be
suspended to ensure that its whole context is available in the memory to be relocated




                                                                                    131
6 Hard Real-Time Online Mapping Adaptation


to the destination location. Execution suspension may be realized preemptively, non-
preemptively, or cooperatively. These schemes offer a trade-off between the initiation
delay and the context size of the migration and are introduced in the following.
   In a preemptive suspension scheme, the operating system may stop the execution of
the task at any point during its execution. This enables an immediate initiation of the
migration at the cost of a relatively large context size. Contrarily, in non-preemptive
suspension, the execution of the task may be suspended only after its current iteration
of execution (job) is completed. This often leads to an inflated initiation delay for the
migration, but benefits from a relatively small context size to be relocated. Finally, in
cooperative suspension, the execution may be suspended only at so-called migration
points specified in the source code of the task [SH98]. Migration points are inserted
(by the programmer) at code locations where the context size is particularly small
so that a moderate trade-off between migration initiation delay and context size is
achieved. The choice of suspension scheme for mapping adaptation strongly depends
on the heterogeneity of the source and destination cores of a migration as discussed in
the following.

Resource Heterogeneity

The complexity of a task migration strongly depends on the heterogeneity of its source
and destination cores. In the case of task migrations between homogeneous cores, the
architectural and micro-architectural compatibility between the source and destination
cores enables all three variants of suspension schemes to be used. In the case of
heterogeneous cores, however, an adaptation of the context may become necessary
subject to the Instruction Set Architecture (ISA) of the source and destination cores.
   For task migrations between heterogeneous cores with the same ISA, the micro-
architectural differences between the two cores necessitate state-transformation ser-
vice routines under a preemptive or a cooperative suspension scheme to adapt the
process state (w.r.t. the contents of core registers) of the task to the micro-architecture
of the destination core. Since often state-transformation services are too complex to be
provided in an embedded setting, the practice of preemptive or cooperative suspension
schemes in many-core systems is typically restricted to homogeneous migrations
only. Contrarily, since the process state becomes irrelevant under a non-preemptive
suspension scheme, a migration of tasks between heterogeneous cores does not depend
on state-transformation services under a non-preemptive suspension scheme.
   For task migrations between heterogeneous cores with different ISAs, binary-
translation service routines become necessary to render the binary (source code) of the
task compatible with the ISA of the destination core. Similar to state-transformation
services, binary-translation services are too complex to be provided in an embedded
system. In this context, fat binaries may be used to empower task migrations between
heterogeneous-ISA cores. A fat binary consists of a collection of binaries, one
for each available ISA, from which the compatible binary can be selected at the



132
                                    6.1 Mapping Adaptation in Many-Core Systems


location where the task will be executed [MR14]. Note that, due to the lack of per-
instruction correspondence between different binaries of a task, migrations between
heterogeneous-ISA cores are typically restricted to non-preemptive or cooperative
suspension schemes.

Adaptation Timing Guarantees

Task migration, especially in the case of an inter-tile migration, entails a temporal
overhead that must be accounted for in the context of real-time applications. In
fact, a migration process may affect the timing behavior of the application in two
aspects: (i) During the migration process, the timing behavior of the migrating tasks
is affected due to their suspension. This also affects the timing of the consumption
and production of messages by the migrating tasks. (ii) After the migration process,
the (worst-case) timing properties of migrating tasks and their input/output messages
may differ from their pre-migration measures. For instance, the migration of a task
between heterogeneous cores often results in a change in its Worst-Case Response
Time (WCRT). Likewise, a change in the length of the NoC route of an input/output
message of a migrating task results in a change in its Worst-Case Traversal Time
(WCTT) as well.
   To preserve a consistent satisfaction of the timing constraints of real-time appli-
cations during and after mapping adaptation, it is necessary to verify that both the
temporal overhead of the adaptation process and the changes in the timing behavior
of the tasks and messages of the application adhere to the given real-time constraints.
Such a verification scheme necessitates a (i) worst-case timing analysis to bound
the latency of the migration process, (ii) worst-case timing analyses to bound the
post-migration WCRT of tasks and WCTT of messages, and a (iii) feasibility check
to verify that the bounds derived in (i) and (ii) are guaranteed to respect the timing
constraints of the application seamlessly. The adaptation mechanisms employed in
many-core systems are, however, typically timing unpredictable which renders a
formal worst-case timing analysis of the adaptation process impossible or results in
timing guarantees that are too loose to be of any practical use.

Contributions Overview

This work presents two methodologies, namely, a (i) mapping reconfiguration method-
ology and a (ii) task migration methodology, which enable the online mapping adapta-
tion of real-time applications in heterogeneous many-core systems while addressing
the adaptation challenges outlined above. In the proposed mapping reconfigura-
tion methodology, mapping adaptations are realized in the form of a reconfiguration
(switch) between the precomputed mappings of an application. The proposed task
migration methodology, on the other hand, enables a finer granularity of adaptation by
allowing any subset of an application’s tasks to be migrated to any desired locations.



                                                                                  133
6 Hard Real-Time Online Mapping Adaptation


   Each of the proposed mapping adaptation methodologies is supported with a
(i) timing-predictable adaptation mechanism (reconfiguration or migration) that en-
ables the RPM to perform mapping adaptations in a timing-predictable manner. More-
over, for each methodology, a (ii) worst-case timing analysis is developed to derive a
safe upper bound on the latency of the adaptation process formally. The availability of
mappings at design time enables the mapping reconfiguration methodology to benefit
from an offline or hybrid (offline/online) analysis to derive all necessary information
(including timing guarantees) for the reconfiguration between each pair of source and
target mappings. This information is then provided to the RPM to perform informed
reconfigurations. On the other hand, the task migration methodology benefits from a
lightweight timing analysis that enables the RPM to consider any form of mapping
adaptation without being restricted to a precomputed set of adaptation options. Each
methodology provides the RPM also with an (iii) adaptation admission check which
examines the admissibility of an adaptation w.r.t. the timing constraints of the applica-
tion to ensure a seamless satisfaction of the timing requirements during and after the
adaptation process. For its examination, the admission check takes into account the
timing properties of the application before and after the adaptation, the changes in its
timing behavior during the adaptation process, and its given timing constraints.
   Note that an investigation of mapping adaptation policies used by the RPM, i.e., the
identification of conditions triggering a mapping adaptation and the decision criteria
of the RPM for selecting a proper form of adaptation, falls out of the scope of this
work. Since the proposed adaptation methodologies provide a means to perform a
given adaptation timing-predictably with worst-case timing guarantees, they can be
employed in conjunction with any mapping adaptation policy that decides on the time,
type, and choice of adaptations to be performed.


6.2 Hard Real-Time Mapping Reconfiguration
This section presents the proposed mapping reconfiguration methodology. Figure 6.1
provides an overview of the reconfiguration methodology in the context of Hybrid
Application Mapping (HAM) where the reconfiguration-related steps and components
are highlighted in blue and outlined in the following.
   The proposed methodology enables the RPM to perform mapping adaptations
in form of a reconfiguration (switch) between the precomputed mappings of the
application. Each mapping reconfiguration involves the migration of several tasks to
construct the target mapping from the source mapping. In the proposed methodology,
at design time, mapping reconfiguration analysis is performed on the (Pareto-optimal)
mappings obtained from the Design Space Exploration (DSE) to collect all necessary
information (including timing guarantees) for each and every possible reconfiguration.
To that end, for each pair of source and target mappings, the analysis first determines
migrating tasks and their destination cores. For each inter-tile task migration, it

                                           6.2 Hard Real-Time Mapping Reconfiguration


                     specification (application, architecture, mapping edges)

                                Design Space Exploration (DSE)

identifies a NoC route with minimal allocation overhead (primary goal) and lowest
worst-case transfer latency (secondary goal). Finally, using a formal worst-case timing
analysis, it bounds the latency of each migration and, subsequently, the end-to-end
latency of the mapping reconfiguration process.
   The statically derived migration routes and timing guarantees for each reconfigura-
tion option are provided to the RPM to perform deadline-adhering mapping reconfigu-
rations on-demand. For this purpose, the RPM is provided with a timing-predictable
reconfiguration mechanism that enables it to perform mapping reconfigurations in a
timing-predictable manner (for which the statically derived timing bounds are guar-
anteed to hold). The RPM is also provided with a reconfiguration admission check
to verify the real-time conformity of each reconfiguration option based on the timing
guarantees provided for it w.r.t. the current timing constraints of the application and,
thereby, avoid reconfiguration options which may lead to the violation of real-time
constraints.
   As an alternative to the offline analysis, a hybrid reconfiguration analysis may
also be established. In such a scheme, the computation of efficient migration routes
and parts of the reconfiguration timing analysis are performed offline, while some
parts of the timing analysis are performed online by the RPM to obtain tighter timing



                                                                                            135
6 Hard Real-Time Online Mapping Adaptation


guarantees by considering the actual availability of the NoC bandwidth in the analysis.
   In the following, Section 6.2.1 presents the mapping reconfiguration mechanism
which specifies the procedure of performing mapping reconfigurations at run time.
Then, Section 6.2.2 presents the offline reconfiguration analysis and details on the
optimization of migration routes and reconfiguration worst-case timing analysis. Fol-
lowing that, Section 6.2.3 presents the reconfiguration admission check to be used
online by the RPM to examine the real-time conformity of mapping reconfigura-
tions. Finally, the effectiveness of the proposed reconfiguration methodology and the
overheads associated with it are experimentally evaluated in Section 6.2.4.

6.2.1 Mapping Reconfiguration Mechanism
A mapping reconfiguration between a source and a target mapping involves the
migration of a set of tasks to construct the target mapping from the source mapping.
Figure 6.2 illustrates an exemplary application with two mappings of it (mapping µ 0
and mapping µ 1 ) on a 2 × 3 heterogeneous many-core architecture. In each mapping,
the red dashed arrows indicate the set of migrating tasks and their destination cores for
a reconfiguration to the other mapping. For instance, for the mapping reconfiguration
µ 0 → µ 1 , tasks t 1 , t 2 , and t 3 must be migrated as indicated by the red dashed arrows
in Figure 6.2b.
    The shared memory scheme within each tile of a many-core platform enables intra-
tile migrations to be realized implicitly, i.e., without the relocation of data between
different memories, e.g., for task t 1 in Figure 6.2b. In contrast, the distributed inter-tile
memory scheme necessitates an explicit relocation of the task’s context between the
source and destination tiles in the case of an inter-tile migration, e.g., for tasks t 2 and
t 3 in Figure 6.2b. The inter-tile migration of a task requires a so-called migration route
over the NoC to establish a connection between the source and destination tiles of the
migrating task. To preserve composability, the migration route of each task must be
reserved prior to the initiation of the reconfiguration process. To that end, given the
migration route of a task, one or more arbitration slots must be reserved on each link
located on the migration route to establish the necessary connection. In the proposed
mapping reconfiguration methodology, given a pair of source and target mappings,
(i) the migration route of each task, (ii) the (minimum) number of arbitration slots to
be reserved per router for the reconfiguration process, and (iii) the worst-case latency
of each migration are analyzed offline (presented in Section 6.2.2) and are provided
to the RPM. Therefore, all necessary information about the migrations involved in
a mapping reconfiguration process is available to the RPM at run time to perform
mapping reconfigurations on-demand.
    In the context of hard real-time applications, a mapping reconfiguration must be
performed timing-predictably so that worst-case timing guarantees can be derived for
it to enable examining its conformity with the hard timing constraints of the applica-
tion. To enable that, the proposed mapping reconfiguration methodology introduces

              µ 0 and µ 1 , of an application. In each mapping, the red dashed arrows
              indicate the set of migrating tasks and their destination cores for a recon-
              figuration to the other mapping.


a timing-predictable reconfiguration mechanism to be used online by the RPM to
perform mapping reconfigurations timing-predictably. For the proposed reconfigu-
ration mechanism, a reconfiguration timing analysis is developed as well (presented
in Section 6.2.2) which provides worst-case latency guarantees for each mapping recon-
figuration performed in line with the proposed reconfiguration mechanism. Moreover,
a lightweight reconfiguration admission check (presented in Section 6.2.3) is provided
to be used by the RPM online to examine the real-time conformity of a mapping
reconfiguration based on the timing guarantees provided for it by the reconfiguration
timing analysis.
   At run time, when a mapping reconfiguration shall take place2 , the RPM first exam-
ines the admissibility of the reconfiguration w.r.t. the current availability of resources

 2 As mentioned earlier, an investigation of mapping adaptation policies, i.e., the decisions on the time,

   the type (reconfiguration or migration), and the concrete choice of adaptation to be performed
   falls out of the scope of this work. The methodologies proposed in this chapter provide timing-
   predictable mapping adaptation mechanisms that enable performing adaptations with hard timing
   guarantees and can be employed in conjunction with any mapping adaptation policy.



                                                                                                        137
6 Hard Real-Time Online Mapping Adaptation


and current timing constraints of the application3 . A reconfiguration from mapping
µ 0 to mapping µ 1 is considered admissible if (i) the required resources for mapping
µ 1 are available, (ii) the NoC budgets required to establish the migration routes are
available, and (iii) the reconfiguration is verified to respect the timing requirements of
the application. Once an admissible reconfiguration option has been identified, the
RPM reserves the required resources as outlined in (i) and (ii) and initiates the recon-
figuration process which is coordinated by the proposed reconfiguration mechanism
as follows.
   To perform a mapping reconfiguration, first, the execution of the application on the
source mapping is suspended. To that end, the execution of its tasks is suspended in
line with the preemption scheme in use, in a procedure similar to the context-switch
procedure of the operating system. Also, the injection of its inter-tile messages into the
NoC is blocked such that the application comes to a complete standstill. Note that the
latter is crucial for preserving the data-flow consistency of the application as it prevents
the loss and out-of-order delivery of messages without relying on message-forwarding
and buffer-reordering service routines which are often not available in an embedded
system. The suspension process entails a delay termed suspension latency whose upper
bound is analyzed previously at design time. Therefore, once the suspension command
has been issued, the RPM waits for the duration of the suspension latency to ensure a
complete suspension of the application. After the application has been suspended, the
migration of tasks takes place. While intra-tile migrations do not involve the relocation
of the context of tasks, for inter-tile migrations, the context of each respective task is
relocated to its destination tile over the statically determined migration routes. The
unprocessed input messages and blocked output messages of each migrating task
which are available at the source location of the migration are relocated together with
the task to prevent data-flow inconsistencies, e.g., consequent to the loss of messages
or their out-of-order delivery. Once all migrations have been performed, the target
mapping is constructed. Thus, the RPM may resume the execution of the application
on the target mapping and release those reserved resources which were reserved for
the source mapping or the migration routes but are not required by the target mapping.

6.2.2 Mapping Reconfiguration Analysis
Given a set of (Pareto-optimal) mappings, the proposed mapping reconfiguration
analysis provides three sets of information for the mapping reconfiguration between
each pair of mappings: (i) the migration route of each migrating task, (ii) an upper
bound on the worst-case latency of each step of the reconfiguration process as well as
the whole reconfiguration, and (iii) an upper bound on the end-to-end latency of the

 3 Note that in the case of a thermally composable HAM methodology as introduced in Chapter 4, the

   thermal-safety admission check is performed as well to ensure that the target mapping is thermally
   safe for the current system state.



138
                                       6.2 Hard Real-Time Mapping Reconfiguration


application in case the reconfiguration is performed. This information empowers the
RPM to perform mapping adaptations on-demand with worst-case timing guarantees.
   Given the pair of source and target mappings of a reconfiguration, first, efficient
migration routes are identified for the migrating tasks that are to be relocated between
different tiles. To that end, a migration-route optimization approach is presented which
identifies migration routes with minimal allocation cost (primary goal) and minimal
worst-case latency (secondary goal). Once the migration routes have been determined,
worst-case timing guarantees are derived for different steps of the reconfiguration
process and on the end-to-end latency of the application in view of the reconfiguration.
The three steps of the reconfiguration analysis are detailed in the following.

Migration Route Optimization

The choice of migration routes for a mapping reconfiguration strongly affects the re-
source and timing efficiency of the reconfiguration and, hence, its run-time feasibility.
Therefore, achieving a minimal allocation cost for establishing the migration routes for
a mapping reconfiguration is a primary goal of the proposed reconfiguration method-
ology to enhance the feasibility of mapping reconfigurations in view of a dynamic
availability of resources at run time. The allocation overhead of a reconfiguration can
be mitigated by utilizing the NoC budget that is reserved for the transfer of messages
in the source and target mappings of the reconfiguration. Recall from Section 6.2.1
that the resources required by the source and target mappings are reserved for the
application prior to the initiation of the reconfiguration process. Since the execution
of the application is suspended during the reconfiguration process, the NoC budget
reserved for the messages in the involved mappings can be used to establish the
necessary migration routes. This budget may, however, not suffice for establishing all
necessary migration routes. For instance, for the mapping reconfiguration µ 0 → µ 1
in Figure 6.2, the migration route of task t 2 can be established merely using the NoC
budget reserved for the transfer of messages m0 , m1 , and m2 in mappings µ 0 and µ 1 .
For task t 3 , however, a connection between the source and destination tiles cannot be
established merely based on the reserved budget. Therefore, additional NoC budget
must be reserved to construct its migration route.
   In this work, the efficiency of a migration route is quantified in terms of (i) its
allocation overhead, denoting the number of arbitration time slots that must be
reserved additionally on the NoC for its construction, and (ii) its Worst-Case Relocation
Latency (WCRL), denoting an upper bound on its worst-case latency for the relocation
of the migrating data. To minimize the allocation overhead, the NoC budget reserved
for the source/target mappings is utilized for the construction of migration routes so
that additional budgets are allocated only if the available budget does not suffice for
establishing the required connection. To mitigate the WCRL, those links that offer a
higher reserved budget are prioritized for inclusion in the migration route of each task.
   For the identification of efficient migration routes, this work introduces a graph-

Figure 6.3: Routing graph constructed based on mappings µ 0 and µ 1 in Figure 6.2. It
             reflects the NoC availability w.r.t. NoC budgets reserved in µ 0 and µ 1 and
             is used for the identification of low-cost and low-latency NoC routes for
             the migration of tasks in mapping reconfigurations between µ 0 and µ 1 .


based intermediate representation of the NoC availability termed the routing graph and
denoted as Groute (Vroute , Eroute ). The vertices Vroute = R ∪ N of a routing graph com-
prise the set of routers R and the set of Network Adapters (NAs) N in the architecture
graph of the platform. Edges Eroute ⊂ Earch represent the NoC links connecting each
router to its adjacent routers and its local NA. The routing graph is constructed for
each given pair of mappings and is used to identify efficient migration routes for the
reconfigurations between the two mappings. To reflect the NoC availability w.r.t. the
reserved NoC budget in the source/target mappings, each edge e ∈ Eroute in the routing
graph is annotated with two attributes, namely, allocation cost κ and reserved arbitra-
tion weight ω. Figure 6.3 illustrates the routing graph corresponding to mappings µ 0
and µ 1 in Figure 6.2, assuming reserved arbitration weights of Wρmm11 = 3 and Wρmm22 = 4
for inter-tile messages in mapping µ 0 and Wρmm00 = 3, Wρmm11 = 4, and Wρmm22 = 5 for inter-
tile messages in mapping µ 1 . In the following, first, the link attributes are introduced,
and the cost and WCRL analysis of migration routes is elucidated. Then, the route
optimization procedure is presented.

Route Allocation Cost

The allocation overhead of a migration route denotes the overall additional NoC
budget (arbitration time slots) required to construct that route. To reflect the allocation
overhead per NoC link, each edge e ∈ Eroute in the routing graph is annotated with
two attributes: (i) allocation cost κ e ∈ {0, 1} which denotes whether the source/target



140
                                            6.2 Hard Real-Time Mapping Reconfiguration


mappings have reserved any budget on the NoC link corresponding to edge e, and
(ii) reserved arbitration weight ω e ∈ N+  ≤Ke which denotes the number of arbitration
time slots available on the link corresponding to edge e where Ke reflects the arbitration
capacity of the respective link. For edges corresponding to already allocated links, the
allocation cost is κ = 0 and the reserved arbitration weight ω is equal to the number
of time slots reserved on that link as part of the source/target mappings4 . For edges
corresponding to links that are not allocated in either of the involved mappings, the
allocation cost is κ = 1 and the arbitration weight is equal to an adjustable minimal
budget ω = ωex . Here, a total of ωex time slots must be reserved additionally on the
respective link if it is contained in the migration route. In Figure 6.3, the attributes of
allocated links (with κ = 0) are given in color which denotes whether the available
arbitration weight is allocated by mapping µ 0 (green) or mapping µ 1 (blue). Based on
the link attributes above, the allocation cost of a migration route ρmig = (e | e ∈ Eroute )
consisting of a connected sequence of edges e ∈ Eroute is calculated as:

                                   cost(ρmig ) =     ∑ κe · ωe                                  (6.1)
                                                   e∈ρmig


Worst-Case Relocation Latency (WCRL)

The WCRL of a migrating task t on migration route ρmig denotes the maximum end-
to-end latency of the migration process of t which can be analyzed formally based
on the size of the migration data-set of t and the amount of budget available on the
links involved in the transfer of t. The migration data-set of a migrating task t denotes
the set of data that is required at the destination location for a seamless resumption of
t’s execution after the migration. It consists of t’s context (code, data, state, etc.) of
size Ψctx (t) and its unprocessed input- and blocked output messages m ∈ IO(t) which
are available at the source tile of the migration and must be relocated together with t.
Thus, the size of the migration data-set of a task t, denoted by Ψmig (t), can be bounded
using Eq. (6.2) where PLDm denotes the maximum payload size of message m.

Migration Route Selection Procedure

The identification of efficient migration routes is realized using an iterative procedure
developed based on Dijkstra’s shortest path algorithm [Dij+ 59]. Given a pair of NA
vertices in the routing graph (denoting the source and destination tiles of the migra-
tion), the proposed procedure identifies an efficient migration route with minimized
allocation cost (primary goal) and WCRL (secondary goal) between the two vertices.
For this purpose, starting at the source node, partial migration routes are iteratively
constructed and extended towards the destination node until the complete migration
route with the lowest cost (primarily) and WCRL (secondarily) is obtained. Initially,
the partial route consists of the source node (NA) and its immediate router. The cost
and WCRL of the partial route are derived using Eqs. (6.1) and (6.3), respectively, and
the partial route is stored in an initially empty route queue.


142
                                         6.2 Hard Real-Time Mapping Reconfiguration


   Once the initial partial route has been constructed and evaluated, further partial
routes are constructed iteratively by extending the existing partial route that has the
lowest cost (primarily) and WCRL (secondarily) by one hop towards the destination
node. To that end, we iteratively (i) take out from the route queue the partial route
with the lowest cost (primarily) and WCRL (secondarily). The selected partial route
is then (ii) extended by one hop to the adjacent router(s) of its tail router towards the
destination node. (iii) The cost and the WCRL of the resulting partial route(s) are
then calculated using Eqs. (6.1) and (6.3), respectively, and (iv) the partial route(s) are
added to the route queue. Steps (i)–(iv) are repeated iteratively until the partial route
selected from the route queue in step (i) already ends at the destination node (NA),
denoting that the optimal route is found.
   The route selection procedure above is performed for all migrating tasks sequentially.
After the route selection for a migrating task is finished, the link attributes in the routing
graph are updated to reflect the availability of the additionally allocated links for the
subsequent migrations. To that end, the cost attribute of the additionally allocated
links (initially set to κ = 1) will be updated to κ = 0.

Worst-Case Timing Analysis

To guarantee a seamless satisfaction of the timing constraints of a real-time application
in spite of its reconfiguration, the worst-case latency of the mapping reconfiguration
process must be bounded and its admissibility w.r.t. the timing constraints of the
application must be examined. The reconfiguration timing analysis presented in the
following derives timing upper bounds (i) on the worst-case latency of each step of the
reconfiguration process as well as the whole reconfiguration, and (ii) on the end-to-end
latency of the application in case the reconfiguration is performed. The analysis is
performed offline and the obtained guarantees will be provided to the RPM for a
lightweight admission check of mapping reconfigurations at run time.

Reconfiguration Latency (∆reconfig )     The timeline of a mapping reconfiguration is
illustrated in Figure 6.4. As also summarized in Eq. (6.5), the end-to-end latency of a
mapping reconfiguration, denoted by ∆reconfig , consists of (i) the worst-case latency of
suspending the execution of the application on the source mapping, referred to as the
suspension latency of the reconfiguration and denoted by ∆susp , and (ii) the worst-case
latency of relocating the migrating tasks to construct the target mapping, referred to as
the relocation latency of the reconfiguration and denoted by ∆reloc . In the following, a
safe upper bound is formally derived for each of these latency components.

                                 ∆reconfig = ∆susp + ∆reloc                             (6.5)

Suspension Latency (∆susp )      The suspension latency of a mapping reconfiguration,
denoted by ∆susp , reflects the time it takes (i) to stop the execution of the tasks of


                                                                                         143
6 Hard Real-Time Online Mapping Adaptation

              suspension latency                    relocation latency
                   (∆susp )                               (∆reloc )
                          end-to-end reconfiguration latency (∆reconfig )

                    Figure 6.4: Timeline of a mapping reconfiguration.


the application and (ii) to have the messages injected into the NoC delivered to their
respective destination tiles such that the application comes to a complete standstill5 .
Suspending the execution of each task is performed according to the preemption
scheme in use and entails a worst-case latency termed the preemption latency of that
task and denoted by δ (t) for task t. In the case of a preemptive suspension scheme,
the execution of tasks can be suspended at any time during their execution. Thus, the
preemption latency of a task t under a preemptive suspension scheme is guaranteed not
to exceed the context-switch latency of the operating system denoted as DOS , hence,
δ (t) = DOS . In the case of a cooperative suspension scheme, the execution of tasks
can be suspended only at certain points during their execution termed the migration
points. Thus, the preemption latency of a task t under a cooperative suspension
scheme can be bounded as δ (t) = DOS + Dmp (t) where Dmp (t) denotes the maximum
temporal distance between any two consecutive migration points in the binary of t
which can be derived statically6 . Finally, in the case of a non-preemptive suspension
scheme, the execution of tasks can be suspended only after their current iteration
of execution (job) is completed. Thus, the preemption latency of a task t under a
non-preemptive suspension scheme can be bounded as δ (t) = DOS + WCRT(t) where
WCRT(t) denotes the Worst-Case Response Time of t. The suspension of an inter-tile
message m imposes a worst-case latency equal to the WCTT of that message which is
analyzed as discussed in Section 3.3.2. Therefore, given the preemption latency δ (t)
of each task t ∈ T derived as detailed above, the worst-case suspension latency ∆susp
of the application can be bounded as:
                                                              
                        ∆susp = max δ (t), max WCTT(m)                            (6.6)
                                        t∈T           m∈IO(t)

denoted by ∆reloc , reflects the time it takes to relocate all migrating tasks to their

    of the temporal distance between migration points of a task fall out of the scope of this work.

destination location after the application has been suspended on the source mapping.
Each migrating task is relocated over the migration route identified for it using the
route selection procedure presented before. The WCRL of the migration data-set
Ψmig (t) of task t over its migration route ρmig is also analyzed as part of the route
selection procedure using Eq. (6.3). In the proposed reconfiguration mechanism,
the relocation of migrating tasks is performed sequentially. Thus, the latency of the
relocation process for all tasks can be bounded using Eq. (6.7) where T mig ⊆ T denotes
the set of migrating tasks that are relocated between different tiles. Note that, due
to the shared memory scheme within each tile, intra-tile migrations (i.e., migrations
between cores on the same tile) are realized implicitly without an actual relocation of
data between different memories.
