Compound Application Latency              To ensure a seamless satisfaction of the deadline
of a real-time application in spite of a mapping reconfiguration taking place, it must
be guaranteed that the end-to-end processing time of the application never exceeds the
given deadline of the application in spite of the suspension of the application’s execu-
tion during the reconfiguration process and the difference in the timing behavior of its
tasks and messages between the source and target mappings of the reconfiguration. In
view of a mapping reconfiguration, the processing of some inputs of the application
through the tasks of the application will (i) advance partially on the source mapping
before the reconfiguration, (ii) be suspended during the reconfiguration process, and
(iii) progress to completion on the target mapping after the reconfiguration process.
While the reconfiguration latency analysis presented before provides an upper bound
on the duration of (ii), an upper bound is also required on the duration of (i) and (iii)
to derive a safe upper bound on the end-to-end processing latency of the application
considering the reconfiguration process and the changes in the timing behavior of the
application before and after the reconfiguration. To that end, we derive the so-called
compound latency of the application denoted as L̂app which serves as an upper bound
on the processing time of any input of the application that may be partially processed
on the source mapping and partially on the target mapping. Thus, L̂app safely bounds
the collective latency of (i) and (iii) above. For a given pair of mappings, the compound
latency of the application L̂app is derived as follows:

    • For each task t ∈ T , the so-called compound latency of t, denoted by L̂(t), is
      determined as the maximum WCRT of t between the source and target mappings.
    • For each message m ∈ M, the so-called compound latency of m, denoted by
      L̂(m), is determined as the maximum WCTT of m between the source and target
      mappings. The WCTT of an intra-tile message is considered to be zero.




                                                                                      145

      • The compound latency of the application is determined using Eq. (6.8) based on
        the compound latency of its tasks and messages where Π denotes the set of all
        end-to-end paths in the application graph, cf. Eq. (3.12).

6.2.3 Mapping Reconfiguration Admission Check
The offline reconfiguration analysis presented in Section 6.2.2 provides the RPM with
the following set of information for each pair of source and target mappings: (i) the
migration route of each migrating task, (ii) the amount of additional NoC budget
to be allocated for the construction of migration routes, (iii) an upper bound on the
suspension latency ∆susp , relocation latency ∆reloc , and end-to-end latency ∆reconfig of
the reconfiguration process, and (iv) the compound application latency L̂app w.r.t. the
source and target mappings in question.
   At run time, when selecting a target mapping for performing a mapping reconfigu-
ration for an application, the RPM examines the admissibility of the reconfiguration to
each potential target mapping w.r.t. the (i) resource requirements of the target mapping
and the reconfiguration process and (ii) the given timing constraints of the application.
In this context, any reconfiguration option for which (parts of) the required resources
are not available is discarded. For any reconfiguration option which passes the re-
source check, a lightweight timing admission check is performed which examines
the satisfaction of the condition in Eq. (6.9) where Dapp denotes the current deadline
of the application, L̂app the compound application latency for the reconfiguration in
question, and ∆reconfig the end-to-end reconfiguration latency. Any reconfiguration
option which satisfies Eq. (6.9) is guaranteed to respect the application’s deadline
seamlessly and, therefore, is admissible.

                               Dapp − L̂app − ∆reconfig ≥ 0                         (6.9)

Hybrid Reconfiguration Analysis

In case a reconfiguration fails in passing the reconfiguration timing admission check,
the RPM may optionally repeat the lightweight parts of the reconfiguration timing
analysis at run time, taking into account the current availability of the NoC to tighten
the reconfiguration latency bound ∆reconfig at the cost of a minimal computational
overhead: As will be demonstrated experimentally in Section 6.2.4, the relocation
latency ∆reloc of a reconfiguration process is typically far larger than its suspension
latency ∆susp . The relocation latency of a reconfiguration is determined based on the
WCRL of each migrating task (see Eq. (6.7)) which, in turn, strongly depends on the
available budget on the NoC links contained in the migration route (see Eqs. (6.3)
and (6.4)). At run time, the available budget on the NoC is often considerably larger



146
                                       6.2 Hard Real-Time Mapping Reconfiguration


than the budget allocated by the source/target mappings of a reconfiguration process
and the minimal additional budget reserved to enable constructing the migration routes.
Therefore, a considerably tighter upper bound may be derived on ∆reloc if its analysis
is performed based on the actual availability of the NoC. Note that an analysis of
the relocation latency ∆reloc is typically computationally affordable at run time as
it is performed using Eqs. (6.3), (6.4) and (6.7) which are lightweight and impose
an overall computational complexity of O(|T ||R|). Here, |T | denotes the size of the
application (in number of tasks), and |R| denotes the size of the architecture (in number
of routers) where the set of routers in the architecture is denoted by R.
   Analyzing the relocation latency at run time establishes a hybrid reconfiguration
analysis in which the compute-heavy analyses (e.g., the optimization of migration
routes) are performed offline, while lightweight parts of the analysis with significant
impact on the reconfiguration latency guarantees are performed online, taking into
account the actual availability of the NoC. Such a hybrid reconfiguration analysis
(i) tightens the reconfiguration latency bound considerably which, in turn, (ii) renders
many of the reconfiguration options admissible which did not pass the condition
in Eq. (6.9) based on their statically derived reconfiguration latency. This not only
(iii) increases the chances of finding at least one feasible reconfiguration option to
preserve the execution of the application (e.g., in view of a resource failure) but
also (iv) enhances the flexibility of the RPM in choosing between a larger number of
reachable target mappings, e.g., to improve the system’s performance by optimizing
secondary goals such as energy efficiency or load balance.
   Note that reserving the available NoC budget for a reconfiguration process does not
impose a notable utilization overhead compared to the regular load of the NoC since
mapping reconfigurations are typically performed infrequently and the duration of
each reconfiguration process is far shorter than the typical lifespan of an application.
Therefore, the NoC load of mapping reconfigurations is imposed for only a small
fraction of time such that their NoC overhead can be considered insignificant.

6.2.4 Experimental Results
This section presents the results of a series of experiments on the applicability, effi-
ciency, and run-time feasibility of the proposed mapping reconfiguration methodology
for hard real-time applications in heterogeneous many-core systems.

Experiments Setup

Architectures     The experiments are performed based on a heterogeneous many-core
architecture with 6 × 6 tiles. The architecture encompasses 36 cores (one per tile) with
diverse numbers of instances from three different core types (IBM PowerPC, AMD
K6-2, and AMD K6-III) whose specifications are provided by Embedded System
Synthesis Benchmarks Suite (E3S) [Dic10]. Each shared resource is considered to



                                                                                    147
6 Hard Real-Time Online Mapping Adaptation


have a Weighted Round-Robin (WRR) arbitration policy. For each core c, a scheduling
capacity of Kc = 10 slots, a scheduling slot length of Sc = 50 us, and a context-switch
overhead of Dc = DOS = 10 us is considered. For each outbound NoC link of each
router r, an arbitration capacity of Kr = 10 slots and an arbitration slot length of
Sr = τ NoC = 10 ns are considered.

Applications    For the experiments, four real-time embedded applications are used
from the areas of networking (7 tasks, 9 messages), consumer (11 tasks, 12 messages),
telecommunications (14 tasks, 20 messages), and automotive (18 tasks, 21 messages).
The applications are provided as benchmarks by E3S [Dic10]. For each application,
[Dic10] also provides the Worst-Case Execution Time (WCET) of each task on
each core type, its memory demand, the payload size of each message, and the
execution/transfer period of each task/message which are required for the timing
analysis of mappings.

Design Objectives The optimization of mappings for each application is performed
w.r.t. three design objectives: (i) worst-case application latency (makespan) derived
using the timing analysis presented in Section 3.3, (ii) resource cost calculated as
the number of required core instances from each type, and (iii) energy consumption,
calculated based on the WCRT of tasks, the core power-consumption parameters
provided by [Dic10], the payload size of each message provided by [Dic10], and the
NoC/bus energy model from [WSK+ 05] considering a length of 2 mm for NoC links
and 5 mm for buses.

Design Space Exploration (DSE)         The offline optimization of mappings per ap-
plication is performed using the OpenDSE framework [RLG+ 18] which uses the
NSGA-II [DPA+ 02] multi-objective evolutionary algorithm provided by the opti-
mization framework O PT 4J [LGR+ 11]. The DSE is configured to perform 2,000
optimization iterations with a population size of 100 mappings where 25 mappings
are generated per iteration. Once the DSE has finished, the distillation methodology
presented in Chapter 5 is used to distill 20 Pareto-optimal mappings per application.
The following experimental results represent an average over five runs of DSE per
application.

Mapping Reconfiguration Analysis          Given the set of 20 mappings per application,
the mapping reconfiguration analysis presented in Section 6.2.2 is performed for the
20 × 19 = 380 possible pairs of source and target mappings. For the optimization of
migration routes, ωex = 1 additional time slot is allocated on those NoC links that
must be allocated additionally to establish the required migration routes. We have
verified empirically that ωex = 1 results in an efficient trade-off between the allocation
overhead and the latency of mapping reconfigurations. The suspension of applications



148
                                        6.2 Hard Real-Time Mapping Reconfiguration


is taken to follow a cooperative preemption scheme. For each task t ∈ T , the maximum
temporal distance Dmp (t) between migration points and the context size Ψctx (t) is
provided by E3S [Dic10].

Investigated Approaches          The experiments investigate mapping reconfiguration
based on two variants of the proposed reconfiguration analysis: (i) offline reconfigura-
tion analysis in which the reconfiguration analysis is completely performed offline and
the results of the analysis are provided to the RPM and (ii) hybrid reconfiguration anal-
ysis in which ∆reloc is analyzed online by the RPM based on the current availability of
the NoC while the other aspects of the reconfiguration analysis are performed offline.
In addition to an investigation of the allocation and temporal overheads associated with
mapping reconfiguration, the two variants of reconfiguration analysis are evaluated
comparatively in a case study on dynamic thermal management of many-core systems.

Reconfiguration Allocation Overhead

Achieving a minimal allocation overhead for mapping reconfiguration is of crucial im-
portance to enhance the feasibility of reconfigurations in view of a dynamic availability
of the NoC at run time. For the four benchmark applications, Figure 6.5 illustrates the
Cumulative Distribution Function (CDF) of mapping reconfigurations as a function of
their relative NoC allocation overhead. Here, the relative NoC allocation overhead of
a reconfiguration is defined as the ratio of the additionally allocated NoC budget (peri-
odic time slots) for the construction of the migration routes for it relative to the overall
NoC budget reserved as part of its source/target mappings. As illustrated, depending
on the application, 7–23% of mapping reconfigurations impose no allocation overhead
at all and, thus, depend merely on the NoC budget allocated for the involved mappings
of the reconfiguration. For all applications, over 95% of the mapping reconfigurations
exhibit an allocation overhead of less than 10%. The proposed methodology achieves
such low levels of allocation overhead (i) by utilizing the already allocated NoC budget
(of the source/target mappings) for the construction of migration routes and (ii) by
performing task migrations sequentially so that different migrations can be performed
over a common set of allocated links.
   The allocation overhead of a mapping reconfiguration grows proportionally to
the number ωex of time slots reserved on additional links, see Section 6.2.2. The
experimental results presented here are derived based on ωex = 1 which has proven
empirically to offer an efficient trade-off between the allocation overhead and latency
of mapping reconfigurations. A higher value of ωex results in a linear increase in the
allocation overhead of mapping reconfigurations, while achieving only a sub-linear
reduction in the reconfiguration latency. Note that, in Figure 6.5, the reconfiguration al-
location overhead for the larger applications (telecom and automotive) is notably lower
than that of the smaller applications (networking and consumer) at each density of
enabled reconfigurations (horizontal intersects). This trend is observed because (i) the


                                                                                       149
6 Hard Real-Time Online Mapping Adaptation


      mapping reconfigurations [%]           networking       consumer|       telecom        automotive

                                     100
                                                95%
                                     80

                                     60

                                     40
                                           23%
                                     20
                                           7%
                                      0
                                             0          5         10          15        20        25
                                                      relative NoC allocation overhead [%]

Figure 6.5: Cumulative Distribution Function (CDF) of mapping reconfigurations as
                                       a function of their NoC allocation overhead relative to the NoC budget
                                       reserved for the respective source/target mappings [PWG+ 17].


larger number of messages in larger applications typically results in a large amount of
NoC budget allocated for their mappings which can be used for the migration of tasks.
This alleviates the amount of additional NoC budget required to establish the migra-
tion routes. Moreover, (ii) the allocation overhead of a given amount of additional
NoC budget is typically lower in larger applications as the allocation overhead of the
reconfigurations is calculated relative to the allocated NoC budget of source/target
mappings where the latter scales with the number of messages in the application.

Reconfiguration Temporal Overhead (Offline Analysis)

The temporal overhead of a mapping reconfiguration is another aspect with a crucial
impact on the feasibility of each reconfiguration option in view of the hard timing con-
straints of the application at run time. For the four benchmark applications, Figure 6.6
illustrates the distribution of the latency bound of the 380 mapping reconfigurations
relative to the hard deadline of the application. The deadline of each application is
reported in parentheses below the application’s name in Figure 6.6 which corresponds
to an overhead of 100% in the plots of each respective application. For the experi-
ments at hand, the deadline of each application is taken to be equal to the sum of the
execution periods of tasks on the longest path of the application. Note that, in practice,
the deadline of a real-time application may change dynamically at run time, e.g., upon
user request.
   To investigate the contribution of different steps of a mapping reconfiguration
to the overall reconfiguration latency, the distribution of the suspension latency
∆susp and relocation latency ∆reloc of the mapping reconfigurations is also provided

                                                                   6.2 Hard Real-Time Mapping Reconfiguration

      reconfig. time overhead [%]   100

                                          networking            consumer|           telecom          automotive
                                           (5.4 ms)              (100 ms)           (5.0 ms)          (7.2 ms)


Figure 6.6: Distribution of suspension latency (∆susp ), relocation latency (∆reloc ) and
                                      end-to-end latency (∆reconfig = ∆susp + ∆reloc ) of mapping reconfigurations
                                      relative to the application’s deadline (given in parentheses), derived in an
                                      offline analysis [PWG+ 17].


in Figure 6.6 alongside the distribution of the end-to-end reconfiguration latency
∆reconfig = ∆susp + ∆reloc . As illustrated, the distribution of the reconfiguration latency
varies considerably across different applications. Nonetheless, for all applications, the
results demonstrate that suspension latency contributes only marginally to the end-
to-end reconfiguration latency, compared to the relocation latency. Recall from Sec-
tion 6.2.1 that the suspension of the application prior to the reconfiguration plays a
crucial role in preserving the data-flow consistency and synchronization of the ap-
plication. Figure 6.6 demonstrates that the advantages of execution suspension are
achieved at the expense of only a marginal latency overhead.
   Subject to the size of the application and its given deadline, the reconfiguration
latency may account for a large share of the overall time available for the execution
and adaptation of the application, see, e.g., ∆reconfig for the telecom and automotive
benchmarks in Figure 6.6. Evidently, the time margin left above the ∆reconfig box plots
in Figure 6.6 may be too tight for the compound latency L̂app of the application to
guarantee a seamless satisfaction of the given deadline. In this context, if a reconfigu-
ration is verified not to satisfy the admission check in Eq. (6.9), the RPM may benefit
from a hybrid timing analysis of the reconfiguration to tighten the derived bound on
the reconfiguration latency as investigated in the following experiment.

Reconfiguration Temporal Overhead (Hybrid Analysis)

Hybrid timing analysis of mapping reconfiguration empowers the RPM to reflect the
actual availability of the NoC in the timing analysis and, thereby, tighten the derived



                                                                                                                        151
6 Hard Real-Time Online Mapping Adaptation


timing guarantees. As a result, many reconfigurations which fail in the timing feasibil-
ity check of Eq. (6.9) will indeed pass this check based on the tightened guarantee on
their relocation latency ∆reloc and, thereby, their end-to-end reconfiguration latency
∆reconfig = ∆susp + ∆reloc . The following experiment investigates the impact of a hybrid
reconfiguration latency analysis on tightening the reconfiguration latency bound in
comparison to the offline analysis.
   Figure 6.7 illustrates the distribution of the reconfiguration latency overhead relative
to the application’s deadline, derived at different levels of NoC traffic using a hybrid
reconfiguration latency analysis which is partly performed by the RPM online. At
each NoC traffic level, for each reconfiguration, first, the resources required by the
source/target mappings or the reconfiguration itself are allocated before additional
traffic is introduced into the NoC to reach the desired traffic level. This scheme ensures
that all reconfigurations are investigated since the additional NoC traffic is introduced
on top of the resources reserved for the reconfiguration and, hence, cannot harm its
feasibility. For the analysis of the relocation latency ∆reloc and, thereby, the overall
reconfiguration latency ∆reconfig , the whole available NoC budget on the migration
route of each task is taken into account to tighten its WCRL guarantee. In Figure 6.7,
the box plots corresponding to a NoC traffic level of [90–100]% (depicted in red)
closely resemble the distribution of ∆reconfig in Figure 6.6 derived in an offline analysis.
As illustrated in Figure 6.7, a hybrid analysis of reconfiguration latency provides
the RPM with a considerably tighter ∆reconfig bound compared to an offline analysis,
especially at system states with relatively low NoC traffic.
   The amount of reduction achieved in ∆reconfig not only depends on NoC availability
but also the length of the migration routes which directly depends on the NoC size of
the many-core platform. The impact of the NoC size on the reconfiguration latency
overhead is illustrated in Figure 6.8. The results are provided for two representative
benchmark applications, namely, networking (small) and automotive (large), on four
many-core platforms with NoC sizes of 4 × 4, 6 × 6, 8 × 8, and 12 × 12. For each
application, the provided results correspond to the average reconfiguration latency
overhead among all reconfigurations. As illustrated, at each NoC traffic level, the
reconfiguration latency overhead increases with the NoC size due to the corresponding
increase in the length of migration routes which results in an increase in the WCRL
of migrations. Moreover, as the NoC traffic increases, ∆reconfig on larger NoCs grows
faster. The larger gap between the ∆reconfig at traffic levels of [0–10]% and [90–100]%
for the larger NoCs gives evidence that the mitigation of the reconfiguration latency
bound achieved by a hybrid latency analysis increase as the NoC size grows.

Reconfiguration Success and Flexibility

The following experiment investigates the viability of the proposed mapping reconfig-
uration methodology for dynamic mapping adaptation of hard real-time applications.
To that end, a dynamic thermal management case study is considered in which map-

its execution in a deadline-adhering manner. In this case study, each application
is initially launched on an otherwise empty platform (100% resource availability).
Then, available resources of the platform are occupied iteratively to establish various
resource availability scenarios until all resources are occupied (100% system utiliza-
tion). At each system utilization step, one of the cores in use by the application is
selected randomly and marked as hot such that a mapping reconfiguration in near
future becomes necessary in order to evacuate the hot core and, thereby, preserve the
execution of the application. Subsequently, the RPM considers all possible recon-
figuration options from the mapping in use to the other precomputed mappings and
identifies the feasible reconfiguration options. As detailed in Section 6.2.3, a mapping
reconfiguration is feasible if (i) the required resources by the source/target mappings
and the reconfiguration itself are available and (ii) it passes the timing admission check

hybrid variants of the reconfiguration analysis are used to bound the latency ∆reconfig
of each reconfiguration, and the proportion of feasible reconfiguration options based
on each variant of the analysis is recorded.
   For the exemplary application of automotive, Figure 6.9 illustrates the success
rate (left) and flexibility rate (right) of mapping reconfiguration at various system
utilization levels. Similar results are obtained for other applications as well. The results
represent an average over 100 repetitions of the thermal management experiment to
incorporate diverse mixes of hot cores and resource/NoC availability.

Success Rate      Success rate denotes the proportion of adaptation attempts in which
the RPM has identified at least one feasible reconfiguration option such that an
adaptation can at all take place to preserve the execution of the application in view
of the sudden failure of the resources used by it. As illustrated in Figure 6.9 (left), at
low levels of system utilization, the RPM achieves a reconfiguration success rate of
nearly 100% using either of the offline or hybrid analyses. The success rate under both
analysis approaches diminishes as the system utilization increases (i.e., the availability
of the resources decreases). In this context, the hybrid analysis exhibits a higher
success rate as it provides a tighter reconfiguration latency bound which enhances
the chances of the reconfiguration options in passing the timing admission check
in Eq. (6.9). Compared to the offline analysis, the hybrid analysis results in an average
improvement of 10% (up to 22%) in the success rate of mapping reconfiguration.

Flexibility Rate     Complementary to success rate, flexibility rate denotes the propor-
tion of feasible reconfigurations at each adaptation attempt and, hence, reflects the
flexibility of the RPM in choosing between different reachable target mappings, e.g., to
also improve the system’s performance w.r.t. energy efficiency or load balance in addi-
tion to preserving the execution of the application. As illustrated in Figure 6.9 (right),
the reconfiguration flexibility rate achieved by each of the investigated reconfiguration
latency analysis approaches, offline and hybrid, diminishes as the availability of the
resources decreases. In general, mapping reconfigurations analyzed using the hybrid
analysis exhibit a higher flexibility rate. The advantage of the hybrid analysis is best
demonstrated in a comparison of the flexibility rate achieved by the two analyses
at system utilization of [0–10]%. Here, nearly all reconfiguration options pass the
resource availability admission check as nearly all resources are available. Hence,
the flexibility rate gap of 28% between the hybrid and offline analysis here is mainly
governed by the timing feasibility of the reconfigurations. Compared to the offline
analysis, the hybrid analysis results in an average improvement of 6% (up to 28%) in
the flexibility rate of mapping reconfiguration.


6.3 Hard Real-Time Task Migration
The mapping reconfiguration methodology presented in Section 6.2 is the first method-
ology known to us which enables individual and on-demand mapping adaptation of
hard real-time applications with worst-case adaptation timing guarantees in the context
of heterogeneous many-core systems with a distributed memory scheme. Mapping
adaptations in the form of a reconfiguration between precomputed mappings benefit
from an offline/hybrid analysis of different reconfiguration options such that the RPM


                                                                                                                            155
6 Hard Real-Time Online Mapping Adaptation


                                    System Management
 Run Time


             Management Policy        migration option       Admission Checks
            – migration mechanism            x            – resource demand, . . .
            – and timing analysis                         – migration timing check
                                          pass/fail

                 requests (launch, termination, requirement change, etc.)

Figure 6.10: An overview of the proposed task migration methodology in the context
               of HAM. The contributions are highlighted in blue.


is provided with all necessary information for performing timely mapping reconfigura-
tions at run time with negligible run-time computational overhead. However, mapping
reconfiguration enables the dynamic adaptation of real-time applications only to a
limited extent: The adaptation flexibility of mapping reconfiguration is restricted to a
precomputed set of adaptation options which may not be able to address all possible
online situations necessitating an adaptation. Moreover, mapping reconfiguration
typically involves a coarse-grained adaptation of the application where most or even
all tasks of the application must be rearranged on the platform in order to construct
the target mapping. Such adaptations typically entail a significant temporal overhead
that may not be affordable in many cases as discussed in Section 6.2.
    To address these restrictions of mapping reconfiguration, this section presents a task
migration methodology that empowers a finer granularity of hard real-time mapping
adaptations compared to mapping reconfiguration. Figure 6.10 provides an overview of
the proposed task migration methodology in the context of HAM where the migration-
related steps and components are highlighted in blue and outlined in the following. The
proposed task migration methodology provides the RPM with a (i) timing-predictable
migration mechanism which enables the RPM to timing-predictably migrate a given
set of tasks between given source and destination locations on the platform. Moreover,
the RPM is provided with a lightweight (ii) migration timing analysis that formally
bounds the worst-case end-to-end latency of the migration processes, and a lightweight
(iii) migration admission check that examines the admissibility of the migration latency
w.r.t. the given timing constraints of the application and the fluctuations in its timing
behavior during and after the migration processes. The proposed task migration
methodology, therefore, enables the RPM to perform fine-grained mapping adaptations
on-demand in the form of the migration of any subset of an application’s tasks to any
destination cores with worst-case timing guarantees and without relying on an offline
timing analysis or being restricted to a precomputed set of adaptation options7 .
    The proposed task migration methodology is presented in the following. First,

 7 See Footnote 2 on Page 137.




156
                                                  6.3 Hard Real-Time Task Migration


Section 6.3.1 presents the task migration mechanism which specifies the procedure of
performing task migrations at run time. Then, Section 6.3.2 presents the worst-case
timing analysis of migrations followed by Section 6.3.3 which presents a migration
admission check to examine the real-time conformity of migration options. Finally, ex-
perimental results assessing the effectiveness of the proposed migration methodology
in comparison to mapping reconfiguration are presented in Section 6.3.4.

6.3.1 Task Migration Mechanism
The migration mechanism in the proposed task migration methodology enables the
RPM to perform a given set of task migrations in a timing-predictable manner so that
worst-case timing guarantees can be derived formally on the latency of different steps
of the migration process as well as on its end-to-end latency. In general, the migration
of a given set of tasks may entail a set of intra-tile migrations where the respective
tasks shall be migrated between different cores within a tile as well as a set of inter-
tile migrations where the respective tasks shall be migrated between different tiles.
The shared memory scheme within each tile of a many-core platform enables intra-
tile migrations to be realized implicitly, i.e., without entailing the relocation of data
between different memories. However, due to the distributed memory scheme between
tiles, an explicit relocation of tasks becomes necessary for inter-tile migrations. The
inter-tile relocation of a migrating task requires a migration route over the NoC to
establish a connection between the source and destination tiles of the migration. The
migration route of a migrating task must be reserved prior to the initiation of the
migration to preserve system composability.
   The migration of a set of tasks may be realized sequentially or in parallel, subject to
the availability of the NoC and the amount of time available for the migration process.
Given a subset T mig ⊆ T of tasks of an application selected for migration and the
migration scheme (sequential or in parallel) specified by the RPM, the RPM must
first derive an upper bound on the latency of the migrations and, based on that, on the
end-to-end latency of the overall mapping adaptation process. This can be realized
using the lightweight migration timing analysis that will be presented in Section 6.3.2.
Subsequently, the RPM must examine the admissibility of the migrations in question in
terms of (i) the availability of the required resources and (ii) the real-time conformity
of the migration process using the lightweight admission check that will be presented
in Section 6.3.3. If the migrations in question pass the admission check, the RPM must
reserve the required resources, namely, (i) the (required budget on the) destination
core of each migrating task, (ii) the post-migration NoC routes for inter-tile messages
to/from each migrating task, and (iii) the migration route of the migrating tasks that
are to be relocated between different tiles. The migration process of each task can be
initiated after the required resources have been reserved and consists of a suspension
phase followed by a relocation phase as elucidated in the following.




                                                                                     157
6 Hard Real-Time Online Mapping Adaptation


Suspension Phase        The migration of tasks begins with the suspension of their exe-
cution on their source location according to the suspension scheme in use (preemptive,
cooperative, or non-preemptive). To preserve the data-flow consistency of the applica-
tion, in addition to the migrating tasks, the input/output messages m ∈ IO(t) of each
task t to be migrated between different tiles are suspended as well by blocking the in-
jection of new instances of these messages into the NoC. The suspension of messages
prevents their loss or out-of-order delivery without relying on message-forwarding and
buffer-reordering system services which are typically not available in an embedded
system. Once the suspension phase has been initiated, the RPM waits for the duration
of the suspension process (whose upper bound is derived by the proposed migration
latency analysis presented in Section 6.3.2 and known to the RPM) to ensure that the
execution of tasks and the transfer of messages affected by the migration process have
come to a complete standstill.

Relocation Phase         Once the suspension process has been completed, the inter-
tile relocation of the migrating task may be performed according to the migration
scheme selected by the RPM (sequential or parallel). During the relocation phase,
the migration data-set of each migrating task is relocated from its current tile to the
destination tile over the migration route specified by the RPM. The migration data-set
of a task consists of its (i) context (code, data, stack, etc.) and its (ii) unprocessed input
messages and blocked output messages available at the source tile of the migration.
The execution of a migrating task may be resumed after its relocation is completed.
To preserve the data-flow consistency, however, the injection of suspended messages
into the NoC is resumed only after the relocation of all migrating tasks is completed.

6.3.2 Task Migration Timing Analysis
The migration process of a task t ∈ T over a given migration route ρmig entails a
(i) suspension latency and a (ii) relocation latency. Give the migrating subset of tasks
T mig ⊆ T of an application, for each migrating task t ∈ T mig , the following analysis
derives an upper bound on its worst-case suspension latency denoted by δsusp (t) and
its worst-case relocation latency denoted by δreloc (t). The bounds derived for the
migrating tasks are subsequently used to derive a safe upper bound on the end-to-end
latency of the multi-task migration process subject to the migration scheme in use
(parallel or sequential migrations).

Suspension Latency

The suspension of a migrating task t ∈ T mig involves two parallel processes: (i) sus-
pension of t’s execution on the source core and (ii) suspension of its input/output
messages m ∈ IO(t). The suspension of t’s execution can be realized preemptively,
cooperatively, or non-preemptively. In the case of a preemptive suspension scheme,


158
                                                 6.3 Hard Real-Time Task Migration


the execution of t may be suspended at any time during its execution. Therefore, the
latency of the preemption process, denoted by δ (t), is guaranteed not to exceed the
context-switch latency of the operating system denoted as DOS , thus, δ (t) = DOS . In
the case of a cooperative suspension scheme, the execution of t can be suspended
only at the migration points augmented to its source code (binary). Thus, the preemp-
tion latency of t can be bounded as δ (t) = DOS + Dmp (t) where Dmp (t) denotes the
maximum temporal distance between any two consecutive migration points in the
binary of t which can be derived statically. Finally, in the case of a non-preemptive
suspension scheme, the execution of a task can be suspended only after its current iter-
ation of execution (job) is completed. Thus, the preemption latency of task t under a
non-preemptive suspension scheme can be bounded as δ (t) = DOS + WCRT(t) where
WCRT(t) denotes the Worst-Case Response Time of t.
   The suspension of input/output messages m ∈ IO(t) of a task t is realized by block-
ing the injection of new instances of these messages into the NoC while allowing the
already-injected messages to reach their destination. Since the transfer latency of each
inter-tile message is guaranteed not to exceed its Worst-Case Traversal Time (WCTT),
and given the parallel transfer of messages, the worst-case suspension latency of the
input/output messages of a migrating task t is guaranteed not to exceed the largest
WCTT among all input/output messages of t. Thus, the worst-case suspension latency
of a migrating task t ∈ T mig , denoted by δsusp (t), can be bounded as:
                                                                
                       δsusp (t) = max δ (t), max WCTT(m)                         (6.10)
                                             m∈IO(t)


Relocation Latency

The relocation process of a migrating task t ∈ T mig involves the transfer of t’s mi-
gration data-set between the source and destination tiles of its migration. The max-
imum size of the migration data-set, denoted as Ψmig (t) for task t, can be bounded
using Eq. (6.2). Subsequently, given the migration route ρmig (t) of a migrating
task t and the size of its migration data-set Ψmig (t), the Worst-Case Relocation La-
tency (WCRL) of t denoted by δreloc (t) can be bounded using Eq. (6.11) where
WCRL(Ψmig (t), ρmig (t)) is derived using Eq. (6.3).
                                                             
                         δreloc (t) = WCRL Ψmig (t), ρmig (t)                    (6.11)

End-To-End Migration Latency

The end-to-end migration latency of a given set of migrating tasks T mig ⊆ T reflects
the overall temporal overhead imposed by the migration process of T mig on the regular
execution of the application. Thus, it denotes the time interval between the moment
at which the preemption of the migrating tasks begins until the relocation of all



                                                                                   159
6 Hard Real-Time Online Mapping Adaptation


migrating tasks is completed. In the case of multiple migrating tasks (|T mig | > 1), the
RPM may decide to perform the relocation of tasks (i) sequentially or (ii) in parallel.
The following analysis bounds the worst-case end-to-end migration latency of the
migrating tasks T mig ⊆ T , denoted by δmig (T mig ), for the cases of sequential and
parallel relocation of tasks.

Sequential Relocation        In the case of a sequential relocation of tasks, the end-to-
end migration latency will depend on the order in which the migrating tasks will
be relocated. Since the relocation of each migrating task may begin only after its
suspension is completed, once the migration process has been initiated, the relocation
of the first migrating task is guaranteed to begin no later than the maximum suspension
latency among all migrating tasks. Thus, the end-to-end migration latency for a
sequential relocation of migrating tasks can be bounded as:
                     seq                                    
                    δmig (T mig ) = max δsusp (t) + ∑ δreloc (t)                   (6.12)
                                 t∈T mig             t∈T mig


Parallel Relocation        In the case of a parallel relocation of tasks, the relocation of
each migrating task begins immediately after the completion of its suspension. Here,
the end-to-end migration latency of each migrating task t ∈ T mig can be bounded as
δsusp (t) + δreloc (t). Thus, the end-to-end migration latency for all migrating tasks is
guaranteed not to exceed the longest migration latency among all migrating tasks and,
therefore, can be bounded as:
                            par               
                          δmig (T mig ) = max δsusp (t) + δreloc (t)                 (6.13)
                                      t∈T mig


Computational Complexity          Any timing analysis to be used by the RPM must be
lightweight such that its computational overhead is acceptable for online use. The
following analysis derives the computational complexity of the proposed migration
timing analysis. The analysis of a task’s suspension latency using Eq. (6.10) introduces
a computational complexity of O(|M|). The relocation latency analysis of each
task introduces a computational complexity of O(|R|) where R denotes the set of
NoC routers and, thus, |R| reflects the longest possible hop-length for a migration
route, see Eqs. (6.3), (6.4) and (6.11). Therefore, the analysis of the end-to-end
migration latency according to Eqs. (6.12) and (6.13) imposes an overall computational
complexity of O(|T mig ||M| + |T mig ||R|) in the general case. However, since each
message is unicast (i.e., has only one consumer task), the total number of calls for
WCTT(m) when deriving δsusp (t) in Eqs. (6.12) and (6.13) will never exceed 2|M|
which reduces the overall computational complexity of the migration timing analysis
to O(|T mig | + 2|M| + |T mig ||R|) which can be simplified as O(|M| + |T mig ||R|).




160
                                                   6.3 Hard Real-Time Task Migration


6.3.3 Task Migration Admission Check
At run time, when selecting a set of tasks T mig ⊆ T for migration, the RPM examines
the admissibility of the migrations in question in terms of their (i) resource require-
ments and (ii) real-time conformity. For the examination of resource requirements,
the RPM checks the availability of (i) the (required budget on the) destination core of
each migrating task t ∈ T mig . In case t must be relocated between different tiles, the
RPM also examines the availability of (ii) the migration route ρmig (t) of t and (iii) the
post-migration NoC routes for inter-tile input/output messages m ∈ IO(t) of t. Any
migration option (i.e., set of migrating tasks T mig to be relocated to given destination
locations) for which all required resources are not available is discarded.
   For any migration option that passes the resource check, a lightweight timing
admission check is performed subsequently to examine whether the timing constraint
(deadline) of the application is guaranteed to be seamlessly satisfied during and after
the migration(s) in spite of the changes in the timing behavior of the application.
The timing admission check of migration options is realized similarly to the timing
admission check of mapping reconfigurations in which the compound application
latency denoted by L̂app and the end-to-end adaptation latency (here, δmig (T mig )) are
examined against the application’s deadline Dapp as given in Eq. (6.14).

                             Dapp − L̂app − δmig (T mig ) ≥ 0                      (6.14)

   The compound application latency L̂app provides a safe upper bound on the end-
to-end processing time of the application, taking into account the changes in the
WCRT of migrating tasks and the WCTT of their input/output messages. The post-
migration WCRT of each task and the WCTT of each message can be bounded using
the analyses presented in Sections 3.3.1 and 3.3.2, respectively. Based on the pre- and
post-migration WCRT of tasks and WCTT of messages, the compound application
latency L̂app is derived as follows:

    • For each task t ∈ T , the compound latency of t denoted by L̂(t) is determined as
      the maximum of the pre- and post-migration WCRT of t. Note that the WCRT
      of any non-migrating task t ∈ T \ T mig remains intact.
    • For each message m ∈ M, the compound latency of m denoted by L̂(m) is
      determined as the maximum of the pre- and post-migration WCTT of m. The
      WCTT of messages communicated between non-migrating tasks remains intact.
      The WCTT of an intra-tile message is considered to be zero.
    • The compound latency of the application, denoted by L̂app , is determined us-
      ing Eq. (6.8) based on the compound latency of its tasks and messages where Π
      denotes the set of all end-to-end paths in the application graph, cf. Eq. (3.12).




                                                                                     161
6 Hard Real-Time Online Mapping Adaptation


Computational Complexity         Since the migration timing admission check is to be
used by the RPM, it must be lightweight so that its computational overhead is ac-
ceptable for online use. The following analysis derives the computational complexity
of the proposed migration timing admission check. Given the set T mig ⊆ T of mi-
grating tasks, the analysis of the end-to-end migration latency δmig (T mig ) introduces
a computational complexity of O(|M| + |T mig ||R|) as detailed in Section 6.3.2. For
the analysis of the compound application latency L̂app , the post-migration WCRT of
each migrating task t ∈ T mig is derived using the constant-time non-iterative WCRT
analysis presented in Section 3.3.1 with a computational complexity of O(1). There-
fore, the WCRT analysis for all migrating tasks t ∈ T mig imposes a computation
complexity of O(|T mig |). Similarly, the post-migration WCTT of each message
m ∈ M is derived using the WCTT analysis presented in Section 3.3.2 with a com-
putational complexity of O(1). Therefore, the WCTT analysis of all messages af-
fected by the migration imposes a computation complexity of O(|M|). Having the
application graph provided as an adjacency list, the calculation of the compound
application latency L̂app can be realized using a Depth-First Search (DFS) algo-
rithm with a computational complexity of O(|T | + |M|). Hence, the overall com-
putational complexity of the migration timing analysis and feasibility check will
be O((|M| + |T mig ||R|) + (|T mig |) + (|M|) + (|T | + |M|)) which can be simplified as
O(|T mig ||R| + |T | + |M|) given that T mig ⊆ T . Thus, the migration timing analysis
and feasibility check introduce a computational overhead with a linear dependence on
the application’s size which renders it scalable for online use.

6.3.4 Experimental Results
This section presents the results of a series of experiments which assess the effective-
ness and the efficiency of the proposed task migration methodology in comparison to
mapping reconfiguration for hard real-time application adaptations in heterogeneous
many-core systems.

Experiments Setup

Architectures      The experiments are performed based on two heterogeneous many-
core architectures with 6 × 6 and 8 × 8 tiles, respectively. Each architecture comprises
processing cores of three different types (IBM PowerPC, AMD K6-2, and AMD K6-
III) whose specifications are provided by Embedded System Synthesis Benchmarks
Suite (E3S) [Dic10]. Each tile encompasses four homogeneous cores as well as pe-
ripherals, e.g., memories and a NA. Each shared resource is considered to have a WRR
arbitration policy. For each core c, a scheduling capacity of Kc = 10 slots, a schedul-
ing slot length of Sc = 50 us, and a context-switch overhead of Dc = DOS = 10 us is
considered. Each router r is considered to have an arbitration capacity of Kr = 10
slots and an arbitration slot length of Sr = τ NoC = 10 ns.



162
                                                6.3 Hard Real-Time Task Migration


Applications    For the experiments, four real-time embedded applications are used
from the areas of networking (7 tasks, 9 messages), consumer (11 tasks, 12 messages),
telecommunications (14 tasks, 20 messages), and automotive (18 tasks, 21 messages).
The applications are provided as benchmarks by E3S [Dic10]. For each application,
[Dic10] also provides the WCET of each task on each core type, its memory demand,
the payload size of each message, and the execution/transfer period of each task/mes-
sage which are required for the timing analysis of mappings. The deadline Dapp of
each application is considered to be equal to 80% of the sum of the execution period of
tasks and production period of messages on the longest path in the application graph.

Design Objectives The optimization of mappings for each application is performed
w.r.t. three design objectives: (i) worst-case application latency (makespan) derived
using the timing analysis presented in Section 3.3, (ii) resource cost calculated as
the number of required instances from each core type, and (iii) energy consumption,
calculated based on the WCRT of tasks, the core power-consumption parameters
provided by [Dic10], the payload size of each message provided by [Dic10], and the
NoC/bus energy model from [WSK+ 05] considering a length of 2 mm for NoC links
and 5 mm for buses.

Design Space Exploration (DSE)        The offline optimization of mappings for each
application is performed using the OpenDSE framework [RLG+ 18] which uses the
NSGA-II [DPA+ 02] multi-objective evolutionary algorithm provided by the opti-
mization framework O PT 4J [LGR+ 11]. The DSE is configured to perform 1,000
optimization iterations with a population size of 100 mappings where 25 mappings
are generated per iteration. The following experimental results represent an average
over five runs of DSE per application on each architecture.

Investigated Approaches        The experiments investigate mapping adaptations based
on the proposed task migration methodology in comparison to the mapping reconfig-
uration methodology presented in Section 6.2. For the migration methodology, the
RPM uses the XY-routing algorithm [NM93] for the construction of migration routes
and routing of messages among tasks. For fairness of comparison, the relocation of
tasks is realized sequentially, and the mapping reconfiguration methodology employs
a hybrid reconfiguration analysis where the WCRL of each migrating task is analyzed
online considering the actual availability of the NoC. The suspension of applications
is taken to follow a non-preemptive scheme. For each task t ∈ T , the context size
Ψctx (t) is provided by the benchmarks suite [Dic10].




                                                                                  163
6 Hard Real-Time Online Mapping Adaptation


Results Discussion

The effectiveness and the efficiency of the proposed task migration methodology have
been investigated in a case study on the thermal management of many-core systems
using mapping adaptation. The case study provides a comparative investigation of the
proposed task migration methodology and the mapping reconfiguration methodology
presented in Section 6.2 as possible schemes for on-demand mapping adaptation for
hard real-time applications.
   Similarly to the thermal-management case study presented in Section 6.2.4, the
experiment begins with the application in question begin mapped on an otherwise
empty platform. Then, through a series of iterations, additional load is introduced
gradually into the system to realize various system utilization states. At each iteration,
one of the cores in use by the application is selected randomly and marked as hot
which necessitates an online adaptation of the application in near future. Here, (i) task
migration and (ii) mapping reconfiguration are considered as mapping adaptation
schemes. Using task migration, the RPM relocates all tasks running on the affected
core to a core on another tile subject to the availability of required resources and
the real-time conformity of the adaptation examined as detailed in Section 6.3.3.
Using mapping reconfiguration, the RPM adapts the application’s mapping in the
form of a reconfiguration to another precomputed mapping for which the resource
availability and real-time conformity admission criteria detailed in Section 6.2.3 are
satisfied. At each iteration of the experiments, it has been recorded whether each
adaptation approach has been able to evacuate the hot core successfully. The iterations
of introducing additional load into the system and evaluating the adaptation of the
application are continued until the platform is fully occupied. The results presented in
the following are an average over 20 repetitions of the experiment per application on
each many-core architecture to incorporate diverse mixes of preoccupied resources for
each system utilization level.

Success Rate        The success rate of the investigated adaptation approaches, task mi-
gration and mapping reconfiguration, in evacuating the hot core at various system
utilization levels is illustrated in Figure 6.11 for the networking and consumer applica-
tions and in Figure 6.12 for the telecom and automotive applications. In each figure,
each column (row) of plots corresponds to one application (architecture). The high suc-
cess rate of task migration in all plots confirms the effectiveness of the proposed task
migration methodology as an online mapping adaptation scheme for hard real-time
applications. Using task migration, the RPM achieves a significantly higher adaptation
success rate, compared to mapping reconfiguration. The substantial gap between the
success rate of the two adaptation approaches roots in the following three advantages
of task migration over mapping reconfiguration: Since task migration empowers a fine
granularity of adaptation, mapping adaptations based on task migration often involve
the relocation of only a subset of the application’s tasks. Thus, a mapping adaptation

based on task migration (i) requires a smaller set of resources which enhances its
admissibility w.r.t. the availability of resources, (ii) entails a lower temporal over-

and (iii) empowers the RPM to consider all possible adaptation options instead of
restricting it to a set of statically computed options. These advantages enable the
proposed task migration methodology to achieve an average improvement of 35% (up
to 95%) in the adaptation success rate compared to mapping reconfiguration among all
considered applications on both architectures. Note that the success rate gap between
the task migration and mapping reconfiguration approaches scales with the NoC size.
This renders the proposed task migration methodology an attractive approach for
on-demand mapping adaptation, particularly for large scale many-core systems.
