Online Computational Overhead         At run time, once a core has been identified as
hot and, hence, the adaptation of the application that uses it becomes necessary, the
RPM begins the search for a suitable target location for the migration of the tasks
that are currently running on that core. During the search process, the application
continues its regular execution. Therefore, the overhead of the search process is
not critical w.r.t. the real-time constraints of the application. However, given the
limited computational power available to the RPM for the search, the overhead of the
search process (which is mainly due to the resource- and timing admission checks)
must be acceptable for online use. In Section 6.3.3 the scalability of the proposed
migration timing analysis and feasibility check was demonstrated by proving their
linear computational complexity w.r.t. the size of the application. To investigate the
imposed overhead in absolute time, during the thermal-management experiment, the
time spent by the RPM searching for a suitable destination location for the migrations



166
                                                                   6.4 Related Work


has been recorded as well. This time span begins with the emergence of the hot spot
and terminates when the RPM finds the first migration destination for which both
the resource- and timing admission conditions are satisfied. The records denote an
average overhead of 1.08 ms (with a standard deviation of 0.16 ms) for the resource
check and 0.57 ms (with a standard deviation of 0.06 ms) for the timing check. These
measures were derived on an Intel i7-4770 CPU operating at 3.4 GHz with 32 GiB
of RAM. According to these results, the overhead of the proposed migration timing
analysis and feasibility check is on average 47% lower than that of the resource check
which verifies their lightness to be used at run time.


6.4 Related Work
There exists a large body of work on online mapping adaptation in multi/many-core
systems. These works employ mapping adaptation for dynamic load balancing [HIB10;
AVB+ 10; KBL+ 11; FCW+ 14], thermal management [RPM+ 19; LTH+ 14; GMQ10;
MAA+ 09], energy management [HV12], or fault resilience [AMZ16; SPM09]. Simi-
larly to the mapping reconfiguration and task migration methodologies proposed in this
work, existing mapping adaptation approaches employ task migration for modifying
the arrangement of tasks of running applications on the platform. The majority of ex-
isting mapping adaptation approaches, however, either (i) rely on certain assumptions
about the target platform which do not necessarily hold for many-core platforms in
general, or (ii) disregard the temporal overhead imposed by the adaptation process
which renders them inapplicable for hard real-time applications. In the following, an
overview of current practices in mapping adaptation in multi/many-core systems is
provided, and their applicability in the context of real-time applications and systems
targeting many-core platforms is elucidated.
   The memory scheme of a system has a significant impact on the choice of migration
mechanisms applicable to that system. The vast majority of existing task migration
mechanisms assume a globally shared memory scheme, e.g., [AAC+ 08; BAB+ 06;
HV11; HLL+ 13; PAC+ 07; SPM09]. In a many-core context, such mechanisms can be
employed for an intra-tile migration of tasks. However, due to the distributed memory
scheme across tiles of a many-core system, inter-tile migrations can be realized merely
by an explicit relocation of the migration data-set between the source and destination
tiles of the migrating tasks as employed in [EGR+ 15; FWL+ 13; BBW+ 07; VSK+ 15]
and in the adaptation methodologies proposed in this work.
   Existing works on task migration use three schemes to provide the migration data-
set to the destination location: A large share of works rely on a static replication of
the code of tasks on every memory across the platform so that only the dynamic part
of the migration data-set is relocated in the case of a migration [PAC+ 07; EGR+ 15;
GLB+ 09; AAC+ 08; MAA+ 09]. Other approaches terminate the migrating task at the
source location and recreate it from scratch at the destination location [NMA+ 05;



                                                                                  167
6 Hard Real-Time Online Mapping Adaptation


VSK+ 15; BAB+ 06]. Both strategies, replication and recreation, mitigate the temporal
overhead of task migration by reducing the size of data to be relocated. However,
recreation relies on a shared memory scheme, and replication lacks storage scalability
such that its storage overhead is often intolerable for embedded many-core systems.
Alternatively, in [BBW+ 07; MJD+ 11], similarly to the adaptation methodologies
proposed in this work, the whole migration data-set is relocated between the source
and destination locations which is practical for many-core systems due to its storage
scalability and conformity to a distributed memory scheme.
   In an application, processing tasks communicate with each other via messages. To
preserve the data-flow consistency of the application during a migration process, it
must be ensured that (i) each and every instance of messages communicated between
the tasks of the application are indeed provided to their respective target tasks which
must also happen (ii) in the original order they would have reached the target task
in the absence of the adaptation process. To avoid the loss of messages consequent
to a migration process, [NMA+ 05] employs a buffer-and-forward scheme in which
input messages of migrating tasks, which arrive at the old location after the initiation
of the migration process, are buffered in the old location and, after the relocation
of the task, are forwarded to the new location. In [WRW+ 14], dual-casting and
data forwarding schemes are employed as alternatives to the buffer-and-forward
scheme, while [EGR+ 15] uses a drop-and-resend scheme in which unprocessed input
messages are dropped at the old location and are re-transmitted by the producer to
the new location after the migration process. These approaches may lead to an out-
of-order delivery of messages which harms the application’s data-flow consistency.
Moreover, they either rely on message-forwarding services which are typically not
available in embedded systems or necessitate the involvement of the application in
the migration process which annihilates the transparency of the migration to the
application. Noteworthy, the majority of existing migration mechanisms do not
address the data-flow consistency concerns at all. The adaptation methodologies
proposed in this work are transparent to the application and employ communication
suspension to prevent the loss or out-of-order delivery of messages without relying on
message-forwarding or buffer-reordering services.
   Mapping adaptation of hard real-time applications requires a (i) timing-predictable
adaptation mechanism supported with a (ii) worst-case timing analysis to bound the
temporal overhead of the adaptation process. In the literature, only a few works
investigate the temporal overhead of task migration, e.g. [AAC+ 08; BBW+ 07; HV11;
SMR11]. Nonetheless, they either focus on soft real-time constraints and, hence, do
not provide timing guarantees [AAC+ 08; BBW+ 07], or investigate hard real-time task
migrations but rely on certain assumptions, e.g., a globally shared memory scheme,
which renders them inapplicable for inter-tile migrations in many-core systems [HV11;
SMR11]. Contrarily, the mapping reconfiguration and task migration methodologies
proposed in this work employ a timing-predictable adaptation mechanism supported
with a formal worst-case timing analysis and temporal admission check of the adapta-


168
                                                                       6.5 Summary


tion processes. Authors in [DSI17] investigate hard real-time system reconfigurations
between statically known system modes, each corresponding to a unique choice and
deployment of running applications. This approach is, however, not a viable solution
for dynamic systems with a large number of independent applications as the number
of system modes and involved migrations per mode transition grows exponentially
with the number of applications. The mapping reconfiguration methodology proposed
in this work offers a better adaptation scalability as it empowers a per-application
adaptation scheme in which each running application can be reconfigured between a
set of precomputed mappings independently of the other applications. The proposed
task migration methodology further lifts the restriction of precomputed adaptation
options and enables the finest granularity of mapping adaptation for hard real-time
applications in many-core systems.


6.5 Summary
Online and on-demand adaptation of mappings is an indispensable utility in emerging
embedded systems to guarantee the timely execution of hard real-time applications in
view of the steady and unforeseeable changes in the workload of applications and the
availability of resources. This chapter presented two mapping adaptation methodolo-
gies that empower a timing-predictable adaptation of hard real-time applications in
heterogeneous many-core systems with worst-case adaptation timing guarantees.
   The mapping reconfiguration methodology presented in Section 6.2 enables online
mapping adaptations in the form of a reconfiguration between the precomputed map-
pings of each application. The task migration methodology presented in Section 6.3
enables a finer granularity of adaptation by empowering the migration of any subset of
an application’s tasks to any desired destination locations. Both proposed adaptation
methodologies employ a (i) timing-predictable adaptation mechanism and are sup-
ported with a (ii) worst-case timing analysis to formally bound the worst-case latency
of the adaptation process and an (iii) adaptation admission check which examines
the real-time conformity of the adaptation temporal overhead and the changes in the
timing behavior of the application during and after the adaptation process. While the
mapping reconfiguration methodology benefits from a computationally heavy analysis
of reconfiguration options at design time and, thereby, imposes a negligible online
computational overhead, the task migration methodology provides the RPM with a
lightweight migration timing analysis to enable mapping adaptations without being
restricted to a statically computed set of adaptation options. The effectiveness of the
proposed methodologies for an online adaptation of mappings on-demand with hard
timing guarantees was demonstrated experimentally in Sections 6.2.4 and 6.3.4.




                                                                                  169
          Conclusion and Future Directions
                                                                               7
Embedded systems in various areas, e.g., automotive, telecommunications, and in-
dustrial automation, are increasingly encompassing applications with hard real-time
constraints. A seamless satisfaction of the timing requirements of such applications
throughout their lifespan is crucial as any violation of their timing constraints may lead
to the failure of the whole system. Recent years give evidence of a rapid increase in the
number and workload dynamism of applications in emerging embedded systems. The
resulting massive computational-power demand of such systems is projected to render
(heterogeneous) many-core architectures their de facto standard target platform in
the near future. In this context, novel design methodologies are required to empower
an adaptive mapping and management of applications on many-core platforms to
achieve high utilization of the underlying resources, while satisfying the individual
requirements of the applications, e.g., real-time constraints, in view of the steady,
unforeseeable fluctuations in the mix of concurrently executed applications, the state
and the availability of system resources, and the workload of each running application
during its lifespan. To that end, this work proposes a collection of methodologies,
analyses, and techniques that address the major design challenges of hard real-time
systems targeting heterogeneous many-core platforms. Together, the contributions of
this work enable an automated mapping and adaptive management of hard real-time
applications on many-core platforms with verified timing guarantees in line with their
real-time constraints. In the following, the key contributions of this work are outlined,
followed by a perspective on possible directions for future work.


7.1 Summary
Given the computational complexity of timing verification procedures, adaptive map-
ping and management of hard real-time applications on many-core platforms nec-
essarily relies on a Hybrid Application Mapping (HAM) scheme consisting of a
design-time application characterization phase followed by a run-time system man-
agement phase: At design time, mapping optimization, analysis, and evaluation is
performed in an automatic Design Space Exploration (DSE) of each application to
compute a set of high-quality mapping alternatives with verified timing guarantees



                                                                                     171
7 Conclusion and Future Directions


and diverse resource requirements. At run time, the computed mappings for each
application are provided to a Run-time Platform Manager (RPM) to launch each appli-
cation on-demand using a precomputed mapping that adheres to the current timing
constraints of the application and the availability of resources. By performing the
compute-intensive optimization and evaluation of mappings offline, HAM enables
adaptive mapping and management of hard real-time applications on many-core plat-
forms with an affordable run-time computational overhead. The contributions of this
work outlined in the following are developed in line with this hybrid design scheme.
   Many-core timing analysis procedures are fundamental prerequisites for the adop-
tion of many-core platforms for real-time applications and systems. The timing
analyses used for many-core systems often cannot analyze all aspects of timing in-
terferences that may arise during the execution of applications, in the general case.
Therefore, to provide safe timing guarantees, they rely on several restrictions on the
mapping of applications and the resource-reservation policy they follow which often
result in massive under-utilization of resources or unsatisfactory timing guarantees.
As a remedy, in Chapter 3, this work proposes a novel methodology for mapping
optimization and timing analysis of hard real-time applications on many-core plat-
forms. This methodology provides a (i) worst-case timing analysis that is specifically
developed for many-core systems and, thus, can provide formal timing guarantees
for applications, without depending on the aforementioned restrictions imposed by
existing analyses. In light of this analysis, a novel (ii) mapping optimization approach
is introduced which enables a fine-grained adjustment of the amount of resources
allocated per mapping during DSE. Together, (i) and (ii) lift the aforementioned restric-
tions imposed by existing analyses and enable the DSE to deliver resource-efficient
mappings with satisfactory timing guarantees.
   Many-core systems are susceptible to critically high temperatures on the chip. To
preserve the thermal integrity of the system, they employ autonomous Dynamic Ther-
mal Management (DTM) mechanisms that continuously monitor the thermal state of
the chip and, upon the emergence of hot spots, suspend or decelerate the execution of
applications in the thermally affected region to reduce its temperature. This practice,
however, may lead to an unacceptable violation of hard timing constraints. As a
remedy, in Chapter 4, this work proposes a thermally composable HAM methodology
that enforces thermal safety in the system proactively at all times and, thereby, elimi-
nates the exposure of hard real-time applications to DTM mechanisms. The proposed
methodology introduces a (i) thermal-safety analysis into the DSE to derive an abstract
thermal-safety index for each mapping and provides a lightweight (ii) thermal-safety
admission check to the RPM to be used when launching an application to examine
the thermal safety of each mapping candidate for the current system state based on its
precomputed thermal-safety index. Together, (i) and (ii) enable the RPM to preserve
the thermal safety of the system proactively and, thereby, eliminate DTM interrupts
on the execution of hard real-time applications in many-core systems.
   During the DSE in HAM, mappings are often optimized w.r.t. a set of design


172
                                                                       7.2 Future Work


objectives assessing their quality, e.g., in terms of timeliness and energy consumption,
and a set of objectives assessing the number of required resources of each type. This
relatively large number of design objectives often results in a huge set of Pareto-
optimal mappings which may impose an immense overhead for the RPM if considered
in its entirety at run time. Particularly, as this overhead degrades the responsiveness of
the RPM, it can become fatal for real-time applications due to their dependence on
prompt reactions from the RPM, e.g., upon a change in their timing constraints. As a
remedy, in Chapter 5, this work proposes a mapping-set distillation methodology that
selects a representative subset of the Pareto-optimal mappings whose cardinality can
be adjusted according to the computational power available to the RPM. The proposed
methodology distinguishes quality objectives from resource objectives and treats each
properly. Thereby, it distills a subset of mappings such that diverse quality trade-offs
are retained to meet the varying quality requirements of the application at run time
while retaining diverse yet efficient resource combinations to enhance the application’s
embeddability w.r.t. the dynamic availability of resources at run time.
   Real-time applications in many-core systems are often subject to unforeseeable
online events, e.g., resource failures due to overheating or on-demand changes in
their timing constraints. Such events may invalidate the mapping currently in use
by them. To preserve a seamless satisfaction of hard real-time constraints despite
such events, adapting the mapping of applications on-the-fly during their execution
becomes inevitable. To that end, in Chapter 6, this work proposes two mapping
adaptation methodologies for hard real-time applications in many-core systems: a
(i) mapping reconfiguration methodology that enables mapping adaptations in the
form of a reconfiguration (switch) between precomputed mappings of an application,
and a (ii) task migration methodology that enables a finer granularity of adaptation
by allowing the migration of any subset of an application’s tasks to any desired
destination locations. Each proposed methodology provides a (i) timing-predictable
adaptation mechanism, a (ii) worst-case timing analysis to formally bound the latency
of the adaptation process, and an (iii) admission check to examine the real-time
conformity of each adaptation option w.r.t. the real-time constraints of the application.
While the mapping reconfiguration methodology benefits from a computationally
intensive analysis of reconfiguration options at design time and, thereby, imposes a
negligible online computational overhead, the task migration methodology provides
the RPM with a lightweight migration timing analysis to enable fine-grained mapping
adaptations without being restricted to a precomputed set of adaptation options.


7.2 Future Work
The methodologies proposed in this work provide the fundamental utilities and mech-
anisms necessary for the adoption of many-core platforms for real-time applications
and systems. These contributions can serve as the groundwork for several directions



                                                                                     173
7 Conclusion and Future Directions


of future research, some of which are outlined in the following.
   The timing analysis presented in Chapter 3 serves as the backbone for the majority
of the methodologies proposed in this work. Currently, this analysis does not reflect
the impact of local caches of cores on alleviating the worst-case timing properties
of applications. Extending the timing analysis by an investigation of Cache-Related
Preemption Delays (CRPDs) is, hence, a promising future direction for improving the
tightness of the derived timing guarantees. Given that the proposed timing analysis is
developed in a compositional manner, an extension of it will introduce only minimal
modifications to its existing parts.
   The thermally composable HAM methodology presented in Chapter 4 relies on an
offline thermal-safety analysis of mappings. An offline thermal safety analysis entails a
certain degree of pessimism in the provided thermal safety guarantees which manifests
itself in the form of a system utilization penalty at run time as elaborated in Chapter 4.
In this line, future research can be invested in postponing parts of the offline thermal
analysis to run time to empower the RPM to mitigate this penalty by reflecting the
actual system state into the analysis of the thermally safe power-density constraint
and, thereby, alleviate the pessimism entailed in thermal composability. Moreover,
with such a shift, the uniform power-density constraint imposed on all active cores can
further be substituted with an adaptive power-density constraint in which the thermally
safe power budget of each core is adjusted according to its peak power consumption
under nominal frequency and the power profile of the tasks executed on it.
   The mapping adaptation methodologies presented in Chapter 6 provide mechanisms
for an on-demand mapping adaptation of hard real-time applications in many-core
systems. These mechanisms determine how a given mapping adaptation must be
performed. In light of these mechanisms, future research is required to investigate
adaptation policies and strategies to support the RPM in deciding when adaptations
must take place, which applications must be adapted, which adaptation mechanism
(mapping reconfiguration or task migration) to be used, and which adaptation option
(target mapping or post-adaptation deployment) to opt for.
   In general, the methodologies proposed in this work enable an on-demand mapping
and adaptation of real-time applications in many-core systems based on a set of
precomputed mapping options and subject to several online admission checks in terms
of resource demand, timing, thermal safety, etc. These methodologies are guaranteed
to meet the timing requirements of a hard real-time application as long as at least
one of its precomputed mappings fulfills each and every admission criterion when
launching the application or adapting its mapping due to the failure of its current
mapping, e.g., consequent to hardware faults. In this context, a promising and crucial
direction for future research lies in the investigation of fallback solutions in case
none of the precomputed mappings fulfills all admission criteria. An investigation of
strategies for terminating or reconfiguring low-priority applications to enhance the
system state in favor of high-priority applications or to release the resources required
by them can serve as initial steps in that direction.


174
                     German Part




Methoden zur Abbildung, Analyse
      und Verwaltung von
    Echtzeitanwendungen in
      Mehrkernsystemen




                                  175
                                          Zusammenfassung

Eingebettete Systeme in verschiedenen Bereichen, z. B. Automobil, Telekommu-
nikation und Industrieautomatisierung, beinhalten zunehmend Anwendungen mit
harten Echtzeitanforderungen. Bei Anwendungen dieser Art ist die Einhaltung der
applikationsspezifischen zeitlichen Beschränkungen von entscheidender Bedeutung,
da eine Verletzung dieser zum Systemausfall oder im schlimmsten Fall sogar zur
Gefährdung von Menschen führen kann. In den letzten Jahren ist eine rasche Zu-
nahme der Anzahl und der Dynamik von Anwendungen in eingebetteten Systemen
zu beobachten. Der daraus resultierende massive Bedarf an Rechenleistung wird
voraussichtlich dazu führen, dass (heterogene) Vielkernarchitekturen in naher Zukunft
zu der De-Facto-Standard-Zielplattform in diesem Bereich werden.
   In diesem Zusammenhang sind neuartige Entwurfsmethoden erforderlich, die eine
adaptive Abbildung und Verwaltung von Anwendungen auf Vielkernplattformen er-
möglichen, um eine hohe Auslastung der zugrundeliegenden Rechenressourcen zu
erreichen und gleichzeitig die individuellen Anforderungen der Anwendungen zu
erfüllen. Die Einhaltung dieser Anforderungen, die sowohl funktionale (z. B. Echtzeit)
als auch nichtfunktionale Anforderungen beinhalten, muss über die gesamte Lebens-
dauer der Anwendung hinweg garantiert werden. Dies stellt besonders im Hinblick
auf die sich stetig und unvorhersehbar ändernden Ausführungsbedingungen (z. B.
Art und Anzahl der ausgeführten Anwendungen, die Menge der Arbeitslast oder
die Verfügbarkeit der Systemressourcen) eine signifikante Herausforderung dar. Zu
diesem Zweck schlägt diese Arbeit eine Reihe von Methoden, Analysen und Techniken
vor, die sich mit den wichtigsten Entwurfsherausforderungen harter Echtzeitsysteme
für heterogene Vielkernplattformen befassen. Zusammen ermöglichen die Beiträge
dieser Arbeit eine automatisierte Abbildung und adaptive Verwaltung von Anwen-
dungen mit harten Echtzeitanforderungen auf Vielkernplattformen mit verifizierten
Laufzeitgarantien entsprechend ihrer Echtzeitanforderungen. Im Folgenden werden
die wichtigsten Beiträge dieser Arbeit zusammengefasst.
   Angesichts des hohen Rechenaufwands von Zeitanalyseverfahren beruht die adap-
tive Abbildung und Verwaltung von harten Echtzeitanwendungen auf Vielkernplat-
tformen notwendigerweise auf einem hybriden Anwendungsabbildungsschema (en-
glisch: Hybrid Application Mapping (HAM)). Die HAM-Methodik besteht aus einer
Phase zur Charakterisierung der Anwendungen zur Entwurfszeit. Dieser folgt ein
dynamisches Laufzeitmanagemant während ihrer Ausführung, die dabei die zur En-
twurfszeit gewonnen Charakteristika ausnutzt. Zur Entwurfszeit wird die Optimierung,
Analyse und Bewertung der Abbildungsmöglichkeiten jeder Anwendung auf die



                                                                                 177
German Part


Zielplattform in einer automatischen Entwurfsraumexploration (englisch: Design
Space Exploration (DSE)) durchgeführt, um eine Menge Pareto-optimaler Abbil-
dungsalternativen mit verifizierten Zeitgarantien und unterschiedlichen Ressource-
nanforderungen zu berechnen. Zur Laufzeit werden die berechneten Abbildungsalter-
nativen für jede Anwendung einem Laufzeit-Plattformmanager (englisch: Run-time
Platform Manager (RPM)) zur Verfügung gestellt, um jede Anwendung bei Bedarf
entsprechend einer vorberechneten Abbildung zu starten, die zu den Echtzeitan-
forderungen der Anwendung und der Verfügbarkeit von Ressourcen passt. Da die
rechenintensive Optimierung und Auswertung der relavanten Charakteristiken der
betrachteten Abbildungen bereits zur Entwurfzeit stattgefunden hat, ermöglicht HAM
ein skalierbares Laufzeitmanagement adaptiver Echtzeitanwendungen mit geringem
Rechenaufwand. Im Folgenden sind die Beiträge dieser Arbeit zusammengefasst.
   Als ersten Beitrag schlägt diese Arbeit eine formale Zeitanalyse vor, die ver-
schiedene Arten von zeitlichen Garantien für Echtzeitanwendungen in Mehrkern-
systemen ableiten kann. Basierend auf dieser Analyse wird ein neuartiger Ansatz zur
Optimierung der Abbildungen auf Vielkernplattformen vorgeschlagen, bei dem die
Menge der pro Abbildung zugewiesenen Ressourcen entsprechend den Anforderun-
gen der Anwendung bezüglich Zeit und Rechenleistung fein abgestimmt wird, um
qualitativ hochwertige Abbildungen mit zufriedenstellenden Laufzeitgarantien und
verbesserter Ressourceneffizienz zu erhalten.
   In Mehrkernsystemen sind Anwendungen mit harten Echtzeitanforderungen beson-
ders anfällig für die autonomen eingebauten Mechanismen des dynamischen Tem-
peraturmanagements (englisch: Dynamic Thermal Management (DTM)), die für
die Einhaltung der thermischen Sicherheit des Systems verantwortlich sind. Beim
Auftreten von Überhitzung unterbrechen oder verlangsamen die DTM-Mechanismen
die Ausführung der Anwendungen, die in den thermisch betroffenen Regionen laufen,
was zur Verletzung ihrer Echtzeitanforderungen führen kann. Um solche fatalen
Interferenzen zu eliminieren, schlägt diese Arbeit eine neuartige HAM-Methodik vor,
die die thermische Sicherheit des Systems jederzeit proaktiv bewahrt und dadurch
verhindert, dass Echtzeitanwendungen den DTM-Mechanismen ausgesetzt werden,
ohne die thermische Integrität des Systems zu gefährden.
   Die DSE in HAM-Methodiken liefert in der Regel eine riesige Menge an Pareto-
optimalen Abbildungsalternativen, die dem RPM einen erheblichen Managementauf-
wand aufbürden und seine Reaktionsfähigkeit verschlechtern. Dies kann insbesondere
für Echtzeitanwendungen fatal sein, da sie—beispielsweise bei Ausfall einer der von
ihnen genutzten Ressourcen—auf eine schnelle Reaktion des RPM angewiesen sind.
Als Abhilfemaßnahme schlägt dies Arbeit eine automatische Destillationsmethode vor.
Ihre Aufgabe ist es, eine Teilmenge der Pareto-optimalen Abbildungsalternativen zu
bestimmen, die dann dem Laufzeitmanagement zur Verfügung stehen. Das Ziel dabei
ist, den Overhead des RPM zu verringern, während gleichzeitig die beibehaltenen
Abbildungsalternativen im Hinblick auf Qualität und Ressourcenbedarf sehr divers
sind, um verschiedenen Laufzeitszenarien und -anforderungen gerecht zu werden.


178
   Um zu garantieren, dass Echtzeitanforderungen auch bei unerwarteten Laufzeit-
ereignissen, z. B. beim Ausfall von Ressourcen, erfüllt werden können, ist es notwendig,
die Abbildungen von Echtzeitanwendungen zur Laufzeit dynamisch anpassen zu
können. Zu diesem Zweck schlägt diese Arbeit zwei Methoden zur dynamischen
Anpassung der Abbildung von Anwendungen mit harten Echtzeitanfoderungen in
Vielkernsystemen vor. Im ersten Ansatz werden Anpassungen in Form einer Rekonfig-
uration zwischen vorberechneten Abbildungen realisiert, während der zweite Ansatz
feingranulare Anpassungen der Abbildungen in Form der Migration einer beliebi-
gen Teilmenge der Tasks der Anwendung zwischen beliebigen Kernen im System
ermöglicht. Beide Methoden bieten formale Zeitanalysen und leichtgewichtige Zulas-
sungsprüfungen, um die Echtzeit-Konformität jeder Anpassungsoption entsprechend
den Zeitbeschränkungen der Anwendung zur Laufzeit zu überprüfen.

[PGT17]     Behnaz Pourmohseni, Michael Glaß, and Jürgen Teich. Automatic

            7927160.
            Contributions: This work proposes an automatic mapping-set distil-
            lation methodology that, given a set of Pareto-optimal mappings in the
            space multiple quality- and resource objectives, distills a configurable
            subset of mappings such that diverse trade-offs w.r.t. quality objectives
